# Blood Glucose Regulation in Type 1 Diabetes — A Mechanistic and Clinical Reference

## Scope and rationale

Type 1 diabetes (T1D) is characterized by autoimmune destruction of pancreatic β-cells and the consequent absolute or near-absolute deficiency of endogenous insulin. The clinical sequelae — life-long dependence on subcutaneously delivered exogenous insulin, recurrent hypoglycemia, and a residual lifetime risk of microvascular and macrovascular complications — are well established. The *physiology* by which exogenous insulin replacement only imperfectly substitutes for the native β-cell, however, remains incompletely articulated outside the specialist literature. Subcutaneous insulin pharmacokinetics, the loss of paracrine α-cell regulation, the disruption of the portal-peripheral insulin gradient, and the second-order effects of antecedent hypoglycemia on autonomic counterregulation each contribute to a glycemic phenotype that cannot be predicted from a "simple insulin replacement" model. This document is a mechanistic and quantitative reference for the physiological factors that govern blood glucose (BG) in established T1D, written for clinicians, researchers, and modellers who require a citable synthesis at peer-review depth.

## Clinical relevance

Glycemic management in T1D operates on a substrate of intersecting physiological systems that vary substantially within and between individuals. A given insulin dose, carbohydrate intake, or exercise bout produces a different glycemic trajectory depending on insulin on board, antecedent glycemia, hormonal milieu, hepatic glycogen status, gastric emptying, time of day, menstrual phase, and dozens of additional modulators detailed in the chapters that follow. Quantitative knowledge of these modulators — typical magnitudes, time courses, inter-individual variability, and T1D-specific deviations from healthy physiology — underpins evidence-based dose adjustment, structured patient education, technology development (closed-loop control, decision-support algorithms, simulation platforms), and meaningful interpretation of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) data. The document is structured to support all four use cases.

## Document roadmap

The 30 sections, numbered 1–29 with an additional section 10b, are organised into eight parts:

- **Part 1: Glucose homeostasis** (§1–§4) — distribution kinetics across plasma, interstitial, and intracellular pools; hepatic glucose production; renal glucose handling; and brain glucose consumption. The physiological substrate on which all subsequent perturbations act.
- **Part 2: Macronutrient handling** (§5–§6) — digestion, absorption, and postprandial appearance kinetics for carbohydrate, fat, and protein, including the often-overlooked glycemic effects of the latter two.
- **Part 3: Insulin pharmacology** (§7) — pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of subcutaneous insulin analogs from injection-site depot to receptor binding to systemic effect.
- **Part 4: Physical activity and relaxation** (§8–§10b) — aerobic and resistance exercise, muscle damage and inflammatory responses, and evidence on autonomic and glycaemic outcomes of relaxation-based practices.
- **Part 5: Hormones and circadian rhythm** (§11–§16) — counterregulatory hormone biology, the dawn phenomenon, the Somogyi effect (with modern CGM-era reframing), diurnal insulin-sensitivity variation, menstrual cycle effects, and seasonal patterns.
- **Part 6: Lifestyle and external factors** (§17–§22) — acute illness and infection, sleep deprivation, alcohol, psychological stress, temperature and climate, and injection-site and lipohypertrophy biology.
- **Part 7: Complications and warning signs** (§23–§26) — ketone-body biochemistry and diabetic ketoacidosis; insulin overdose and the structural limits of counterregulation; non-linear insulin dose-response; and glucotoxic insulin resistance.
- **Part 8: Technology and research models** (§27–§29) — CGM sensor technology and signal processing; mathematical modeling frameworks used in T1D research; and a comparative evaluation of the major glucose-insulin models.

Each section provides the underlying biochemistry and kinetics, quantitative parameters with primary citations, the T1D-specific phenotype, and identified controversies or knowledge gaps. Glossary-style inline parentheses introduce specialist terminology at first use within each section so that individual chapters remain readable in isolation. A final per-section *Implementation* footer flags which aspects of the section's physiology are reproduced in the companion T1D simulator and points to the relevant entries in `MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md` for readers concerned with computational reproduction.

## Out of scope

This document focuses on the physiology and pharmacology of blood-glucose regulation in established T1D. It does *not* cover: type 2 diabetes-specific mechanisms (β-cell exhaustion, primary insulin resistance) except where T2D evidence is the only available proxy for T1D; the immunology and prevention of T1D (autoantibodies, HLA risk, disease-modifying therapies); the operational details of pediatric versus adult dosing strategy beyond what is needed to interpret physiology (referenced in §17 sick-day rules but not given dedicated treatment); psychosocial determinants of self-management and adherence; the comparative effectiveness of insulin delivery devices beyond their pharmacokinetic differences; and cost-effectiveness or healthcare-system considerations. Topics where the underlying physiology is described here but the simulator implementation differs — typically by deliberate simplification for tractability — are flagged in the per-section Implementation footers and detailed in `MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md`.

## A note on quantitative claims and uncertainty

Numerical values throughout the document are reported with their primary source. Where the underlying study was small, methodologically heterogeneous, or restricted to specific populations (healthy young-adult volunteers, narrow age range, single-ethnicity cohort), the limitation is flagged inline at the point of citation rather than collected in a separate caveats section. Many of the canonical T1D dose-response and counterregulation studies were conducted in young-adult cohorts of limited demographic diversity; pediatric, elderly, pregnant, and non-European populations remain comparatively under-studied. Readers should treat published thresholds — insulin EC50 values, hypoglycemic counterregulatory thresholds, gastric-emptying kinetics, and similar — as population descriptors rather than individual parameters. Where the literature is genuinely contested (the Somogyi effect's clinical frequency, the relative contributions of insulin, GABA, and zinc to α-cell paracrine suppression, the dominance of any single hyperglycemic damage pathway in Brownlee's framework), the controversy is presented explicitly rather than resolved.

# Part 1: Glucose homeostasis

<a name="glucose-distribution"></a>
## 1. How glucose moves through the body

Glucose reaches peripheral cells through the circulation and interstitial fluid before crossing the cell membrane. Delivery, membrane transport and intracellular metabolism are distinct processes: a change in plasma glucose does not specify the glucose concentration or utilisation rate in every tissue. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), inadequate insulin availability reduces insulin-stimulated disposal in muscle and adipose tissue while also impairing suppression of endogenous glucose production. Postprandial hyperglycaemia therefore reflects the balance between glucose appearance and disposal, not a transport defect alone ([Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/); [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/)).

### Mechanism

**Transmembrane transport is tissue-specific.** Facilitative glucose transporters (GLUT; the SLC2 family) mediate glucose movement across many cell membranes; sodium-coupled transport in intestinal and renal epithelia is addressed in §§3 and 5. Four extensively studied GLUT isoforms differ in tissue distribution and regulation ([Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/)):

- **GLUT1** (erythrocytes, blood–brain-barrier endothelium and many other tissues) supports basal uptake; reported transport affinity is approximately 1–2 mmol/L.
- **GLUT2** (hepatocytes and intestinal/renal basolateral membranes) has lower affinity, approximately 17 mmol/L, and supports transport in either direction according to the glucose gradient. Its prominent glucose-sensing role in rodent pancreatic β-cells should not be assigned unchanged to human β-cells ([Holman, 2020](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7462842/fullTextXML)).
- **GLUT3**, prominent in neurons, has high affinity, approximately 1–2 mmol/L. Affinity measured in transport systems does not imply that neuronal uptake remains saturated during hypoglycaemia: glucose must first cross the blood–brain barrier, and plasma glucose is not the concentration at the neuronal membrane ([Holman, 2020](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7462842/fullTextXML)).
- **GLUT4** in skeletal and cardiac muscle and adipose tissue has reported affinity near 5 mmol/L. Insulin promotes movement of intracellular GLUT4 vesicles to the cell surface through phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K), protein kinase B (Akt) and vesicle-regulatory proteins including TBC1D4. Muscle contraction recruits partly distinct signalling, involving AMP-activated protein kinase (AMPK) and the cytoskeletal regulator Rac1. Transport capacity therefore changes through transporter recruitment as well as substrate concentration ([Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/); [Sylow et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27739515/)).

These affinity estimates are apparent Michaelis constants (*K*m) from experimental transport systems, not universal in vivo tissue-clearance constants. They depend on transport direction, assay conditions and the expression system; tissue uptake additionally depends on perfusion, transporter abundance and glucose phosphorylation ([Holman, 2020](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7462842/fullTextXML)).

**Phosphorylation traps glucose intracellularly — except in liver and kidney.** Once across the membrane, glucose is phosphorylated to glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) by hexokinase II (muscle, adipose) or glucokinase (hexokinase IV; hepatocytes, β-cells). G6P is anionic and cannot exit through any GLUT isoform. Whether glucose can return to the circulation therefore depends on whether the cell expresses **glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase)**, the gluconeogenic enzyme that hydrolyzes G6P back to free glucose. G6Pase is robustly expressed in hepatocytes and renal proximal tubule cells, but is essentially absent from skeletal muscle and adipose tissue. As a consequence:

- In muscle and adipose tissue, the GLUT4-hexokinase couple constitutes a near-unidirectional glucose sink under physiological conditions. Glucose imported via GLUT4 commits to local glycolysis, glycogen synthesis, or lipogenesis. There is no significant counterflux of free glucose to plasma.
- In liver and kidney, GLUT2-mediated transport is bidirectional and the G6Pase pathway permits net glucose release. The liver thus alternates between net uptake (postprandial, high portal-vein glucose and insulin) and net release (fasting, low insulin, counterregulatory hormone activation).

**Interstitial glucose is a separate measurement compartment.** Exchange with plasma and local cellular uptake determine interstitial glucose; a subcutaneous sensor consequently measures neither plasma nor intracellular glucose directly. Physiological transport delay, sensor response and signal processing are separate contributors to a displayed continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) trace ([Cengiz and Tamborlane, 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19469670/)).

### Quantitative characterization

| Quantity | Value | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Plasma glucose distribution volume (initial, V₁) | ~0.10 L/kg (~7 L for 70 kg adult) | Reflects central extracellular fluid; estimated from 3-min IV glucose increment | [Ishihara et al., 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10233198/) |
| GLUT1 apparent *K*m | ~1–2 mmol/L | Experimental transport affinity, not a cerebral hypoglycaemia threshold | [Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/) |
| GLUT2 apparent *K*m | ~17 mmol/L | Bidirectional transport; hepatic net balance also depends on phosphorylation and glucose production | [Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/) |
| GLUT3 apparent *K*m | ~1–2 mmol/L | High affinity does not remove dependence on cerebral glucose delivery | [Holman, 2020](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7462842/fullTextXML) |
| GLUT4 apparent *K*m | ~5 mmol/L | Does not establish linear whole-body disposal across physiological glucose concentrations | [Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/) |
| Plasma → subcutaneous interstitial glucose lag (healthy adults, fasting) | mean 5.3–6.2 min (95th-percentile upper bound ~9.8 min) | Microdialysis with 6,6-²H₂ and 1-¹³C glucose tracers | [Basu et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24009261/) |
| Plasma → abdominal subcutaneous interstitial tracer appearance, fasting T1D | Median 6.8 min; range 4.8–9.8 min | Six adults; intravenous insulin maintained near-euglycaemia. Time to tracer detection, not total CGM delay; abstract-level evidence | [Basu et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25305282/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

Insulin deficiency affects disposal and production through different mechanisms. The relevant physiological distinctions are:

1. **Reduced insulin-stimulated disposal.** Inadequate insulin stimulation limits GLUT4 recruitment and muscle glucose uptake. The effect depends on insulin availability and tissue sensitivity rather than following inevitably from the diagnostic label ([Chadt and Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/)).

2. **Uptake without insulin-stimulated GLUT4 recruitment.** GLUT1- and GLUT3-mediated transport continues, including delivery to the brain. These pathways still require an adequate glucose supply; transporter affinity alone does not define a universal plasma threshold for neuroglycopenia ([Holman, 2020](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7462842/fullTextXML); see §4).

3. **Continued endogenous production.** Reduced insulin restraint can coexist with reduced disposal, increasing net glucose accumulation. In human T1D studies, improved insulin treatment and glycaemic control can bring hepatic fluxes towards control values; elevated production is not an invariant property of T1D ([Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/); see §2).

4. **Contraction-stimulated uptake.** Muscle contraction can increase GLUT4-mediated transport without initiating the insulin-signalling pathway. Its contribution to glucose lowering depends on simultaneous glucose production and circulating insulin; transport recruitment alone does not predict the direction or magnitude of the blood-glucose response ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27739515/); see §§8–9).

5. **Measurement delay.** Tracer studies quantify the appearance of labelled glucose in sampled interstitial fluid. The healthy-adult experiment and the six-person T1D experiment support a physiological delay of several minutes under fasting conditions, not a universal offset applicable during meals or exercise. The T1D estimate above is available from the abstract; full-text access remains unresolved. Device filtering and calibration require separate evaluation when interpreting rapid excursions ([Basu et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24009261/); [Basu et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25305282/); see §27).

<a name="hepatic-glucose-production"></a>
## 2. Hepatic glucose production

The liver maintains fasting glucose availability and contributes to postprandial glucose disposal by changing glycogen metabolism and glucose production. Net hepatic output must be distinguished from individual pathway fluxes: glycogen breakdown, glycogen synthesis and gluconeogenesis can occur simultaneously. Whole-body endogenous glucose production (EGP) also includes renal glucose release, so a systemic tracer estimate cannot automatically be labelled hepatic output ([Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/); [Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/)).

Three features matter in T1D: subcutaneous insulin does not reproduce pancreatic delivery into the portal circulation; glucagon responses differ between hypoglycaemia and other stimuli; and poor glycaemic control can be associated with reduced glycogen synthesis and increased gluconeogenesis. These abnormalities are not interchangeable or uniformly present: improved metabolic control can restore several hepatic fluxes towards control values despite continued subcutaneous insulin delivery ([Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33813879/); [Bisgaard Bengtsen and Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/); [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/)).

### Mechanism

#### Glycogenolysis

Hepatic glycogenolysis releases glucose units from stored glycogen. **Glycogen phosphorylase** cleaves α-1,4 linkages to release glucose-1-phosphate, while the debranching enzyme permits degradation across branch points. **Phosphoglucomutase** converts glucose-1-phosphate to glucose-6-phosphate (G6P); transport into the endoplasmic reticulum and hydrolysis by **glucose-6-phosphatase** permit subsequent release of free glucose. Glycogen synthesis competes with this output, making net glycogen loss a different quantity from total phosphorylase flux ([Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/); [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/)).

Glucagon activates its G-protein-coupled receptor, adenylyl cyclase, cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) and protein kinase A (PKA), favouring phosphorylase activation and opposing glycogen synthesis. Glucose and G6P also regulate glycogen-metabolising enzymes. Consequently, insulin concentration alone does not determine whether the liver stores or releases glucose: portal glucose delivery and intracellular substrate availability matter ([Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/)).

#### Gluconeogenesis

De novo glucose synthesis proceeds from three-carbon precursors: **lactate** (Cori cycle, the lactate → liver → glucose recycling pathway, dominant in the post-absorptive state), **alanine** and other glucogenic amino acids (Cahill cycle, alanine-based gluconeogenesis from muscle proteolysis), and **glycerol** released by adipose lipolysis. Pyruvate enters the mitochondrion and is carboxylated by **pyruvate carboxylase** (PC, biotin-dependent) to oxaloacetate — the anaplerotic entry step. Oxaloacetate is then decarboxylated and phosphorylated by **phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase** (PEPCK; cytosolic *PCK1* and mitochondrial *PCK2* isoforms) to phosphoenolpyruvate, which traverses a series of glycolytic enzymes in reverse before encountering the second rate-limiting step, **fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase** (FBP1). The pathway terminates at the same G6Pase step as glycogenolysis. Pyruvate carboxylase activity is allosterically driven by **acetyl-CoA** generated from hepatic β-oxidation, providing a direct mechanistic link between insulin deficiency, lipolysis, ketogenesis and accelerated gluconeogenesis — central to the pathophysiology of diabetic ketoacidosis.

After an overnight fast, gluconeogenesis contributes ~50% of EGP in healthy humans, rising to ~64% at 22 hours, ~82% at 36 hours and ~96% by 64 hours of fasting ([Rothman et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1948033/)).

#### Hormonal regulation

**Insulin acts through hepatic signalling and extrahepatic substrate control.** Hepatocyte insulin receptors recruit insulin receptor substrate proteins and activate the PI3K–Akt pathway. Akt-mediated exclusion of forkhead box O1 (FOXO1) from the nucleus reduces transcriptional stimulation of gluconeogenic genes including *PCK1* and *G6PC*. Insulin also modifies glycogen metabolism and suppresses adipose lipolysis, reducing glycerol supply and fatty-acid-derived acetyl-CoA activation of pyruvate carboxylase. Transcriptional regulation and acute substrate-mediated changes operate on different timescales ([Hatting et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28868790/); [Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/)).

The relative importance of direct and indirect effects depends on the experiment. In 42-hour-fasted conscious dogs, [Edgerton et al. (2017)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28352665/) increased portal insulin sixfold and independently prevented reductions in fatty acids or glucagon, or blocked brain insulin signalling; blocking all three indirect routes did not prevent acute suppression of hepatic output. By contrast, glycogen-depleted rodent experiments reviewed by [Petersen et al. (2017)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/) demonstrated strong dependence of gluconeogenic suppression on reduced lipolysis. The contrast concerns species, glycogen availability, delivery route and the flux measured. It does not establish a universal 15-minute hepatic or 60-minute extrahepatic time constant in humans.

**Glucagon has rapid metabolic and slower transcriptional effects.** In addition to glycogen mobilisation, glucagon signalling alters glycolytic enzyme activity and promotes gluconeogenic gene expression through cAMP-responsive transcriptional machinery. Cortisol, catecholamines and growth hormone contribute through different pathways and timescales; their counterregulatory and diurnal effects are examined in §§11–12 ([Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/); [Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24265365/)).

### Quantitative characterization

[Kacerovsky et al. (2011)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/) combined carbon-13 magnetic resonance spectroscopy (¹³C-MRS; repeated hepatic glycogen measurements) with glucose and deuterated-water tracers after a standardised dinner. The study included 22 individuals, with overlapping group observations because four participants were studied before and after improved control. The table reports means ± standard deviations; flux units are µmol·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹.

| Measurement | Controls, n = 8 | T1D, improved control, n = 8 | T1D, poor control, n = 10 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Glycated haemoglobin (HbA1c), % | 5.2 ± 0.4 | 7.0 ± 0.3 | 8.5 ± 0.4 |
| Net hepatic glycogen synthesis, 0–6 h after dinner | 3.3 ± 2.3 | 2.7 ± 1.4 | 1.0 ± 1.8 |
| EGP, 6–12 h after dinner | 11.6 ± 2.0 | 13.2 ± 1.3 | 16.5 ± 2.3 |
| Estimated gluconeogenesis, same fasting interval | 7.6 ± 2.3 | 7.9 ± 3.1 | 13.2 ± 3.0 |
| Net hepatic glycogenolysis, same fasting interval | 4.0 ± 1.5 | 5.3 ± 3.2 | 3.3 ± 1.6 |
| Net glycogenolysis as a fraction of EGP, % | 35 ± 9 | 41 ± 19 | 20 ± 9 |

Poor-control EGP was approximately 42% higher than in controls (*P* < .001), not 60%; net glycogen synthesis was approximately 70% lower (*P* = .038). Net glycogenolysis did not differ significantly between groups. The lower glycogenolytic fraction in poor control therefore chiefly reflects the larger gluconeogenic contribution, not evidence of faster glycogen depletion. The reported percentages 20%, 41% and 35% are net glycogenolysis fractions in three groups, not a gluconeogenesis-to-glycogenolysis ratio.

Two methodological issues change interpretation. First, the calculation assigned systemic EGP to the liver; unmeasured renal production would inflate the estimated hepatic gluconeogenic contribution. Second, phosphorylase flux can exceed net glycogen loss because glucose is recycled into glycogen. In the poor-control subgroup with the required samples, phosphorylase flux contributed 43 ± 8% of EGP whereas net glycogenolysis contributed 20 ± 9% (*P* < .001). Measurements of these two fluxes should not be pooled as if they were equivalent ([Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/)).

### T1D-specific aspects

**Delivery route changes relative insulin exposure.** Pancreatic secretion normally produces higher portal than peripheral insulin concentrations. Subcutaneous delivery reduces or reverses this gradient, making simultaneous reproduction of hepatic and peripheral exposure difficult ([Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33813879/)). Nevertheless, route alone does not determine measured EGP: glycaemia, substrate supply, glucagon and insulin sensitivity also differ between experimental conditions. A concentration gradient is not an inevitable proportional defect in hepatic flux.

**Metabolic improvement is possible, but the evidence is not a randomised treatment comparison.** In Kacerovsky's study, four participants who achieved the prespecified improvement after at least five months of continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion were restudied; six non-responders were not included in that improved-control comparison, and four additional well-controlled pump users completed the group. Near-control glycogen synthesis and gluconeogenic flux demonstrate that the abnormalities are not fixed, but responder selection and partially overlapping groups limit attribution of the improvement to the delivery device or treatment regimen ([Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/)).

**Alpha-cell dysfunction is stimulus-specific.** The glucagon response to hypoglycaemia can deteriorate within months to years of diagnosis, while amino acids, insulin withdrawal, inflammation and exercise can still elicit substantial responses. Proposed intra-islet mechanisms include altered β-cell signalling and somatostatin-mediated inhibition; several derive principally from animal experiments. Loss of the glucagon response can occur despite otherwise intact counterregulation and should not be equated with hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF). Avoidance of recurrent hypoglycaemia can improve adrenaline responses and symptoms without restoring glucagon secretion ([Bisgaard Bengtsen and Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/); see §11).

The resulting physiological picture is conditional rather than a fixed T1D parameter set. Hepatic glycogen availability, portal insulin exposure, renal contribution and the stimulus for glucagon secretion must be considered separately. Overnight fasting data, glycogen-depleted animal clamps and postprandial human measurements answer different questions; their numerical estimates cannot be substituted for one another.

NB: the simulator models a basal EGP rate (EGP0) suppressed by insulin and amplified by stress hormones (acute and chronic). Glycogen-vs-gluconeogenesis split, the portal-peripheral insulin gradient asymmetry, and the substrate-specific kinetics are abstracted away. Glycogen depletion limits acute counterregulation in the simulator.*

<a name="renal-glucose-handling"></a>
## 3. The kidneys in glucose homeostasis

The kidneys affect systemic glucose through three distinct processes: production, metabolic uptake and urinary excretion. Filtration and tubular reabsorption are much larger recycling fluxes, but ordinarily have little net effect on systemic glucose because nearly all filtered glucose returns to the circulation. Distinguishing these processes is essential in type 1 diabetes (T1D): renal glucose production can support circulating glucose during fasting, whereas glucosuria lowers glucose while contributing to water loss. Neither process replaces insulin's suppression of lipolysis and ketogenesis.

### Filtration, transport and the onset of glucosuria

The filtered glucose load is the product of glomerular filtration rate (GFR) and plasma glucose concentration. At 180 L/day and 5.5 mmol/L, the calculated load is 990 mmol/day, or approximately 178 g/day using a glucose molecular mass of 180.16 g/mol. The commonly cited 180 g/day is therefore a conditional physiological example, not an invariant renal flux ([Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/)).

Glucose enters proximal tubular cells through apical sodium–glucose cotransporters, using the sodium electrochemical gradient maintained by basolateral Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase. SGLT2 (*SLC5A2*) in the early proximal tubule handles most of the normal filtered load; SGLT1 (*SLC5A1*) in the later proximal tubule recovers glucose remaining downstream. GLUT2 (*SLC2A2*) provides a basolateral route to interstitial fluid and peritubular blood. The serial arrangement matters more than assigning a universal 90:10 division: the amount reaching SGLT1 changes with filtered load and SGLT2 inhibition, and its reserve transport capacity is substantial ([Ghezzi et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30132032/), sections on renal glucose transport and pharmacology).

Transporter affinity must be distinguished from whole-kidney capacity. Ghezzi et al. summarize measurements of expressed human transporters under specified experimental conditions—150 mmol/L extracellular sodium, −50 mV membrane potential and 37°C—with glucose half-saturation concentrations of approximately 5 mmol/L for SGLT2 and 2 mmol/L for SGLT1. Their sodium:glucose coupling ratios are 1:1 and 2:1, respectively. These cell-level kinetic values are not plasma thresholds for glucosuria; different assay conditions help explain differing affinity estimates in older reviews. Nor does a small normal SGLT1 contribution imply a low maximum capacity when more glucose is delivered to the late tubule.

The whole-kidney maximum glucose reabsorptive rate, TmG, is an aggregate transport capacity. Urinary glucose begins to appear *before* that maximum is reached because individual nephrons differ in filtration and transport capacity. The resulting curved transition between near-complete recovery and saturated reabsorption is termed **splay**. Reviews often use approximately 10–11 mmol/L as an illustrative onset range and approximately 375 mg/min as an absolute adult TmG; neither is a universal value or interchangeable with a body-surface-area-indexed rate ([Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/); [Poudel, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23961473/)).

Direct T1D observations illustrate the variation. Johansen et al. infused glucose gradually in 23 people with T1D and detected the threshold at the first trace of urinary glucose. Thresholds ranged from 6.0 to 14.3 mmol/L, and maximal reabsorption from 0.93 to 1.98 mmol/min ([Johansen et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6714538/), original abstract inspected; full-text methods appraisal pending). These are observed ranges in one small study, not diagnostic limits. Urinary glucose alone cannot identify contemporaneous plasma glucose without knowing the individual's filtration and excretion relationship.

### Renal production and consumption

The renal cortex produces glucose through gluconeogenesis, using substrates including lactate, glutamine and glycerol. Insulin suppresses renal glucose production, while epinephrine can stimulate it. Renal glucose uptake, particularly in glycolytic medullary tissue, occurs simultaneously. Consequently, net renal glucose release can be small even when gross renal production is substantial; arteriovenous balance alone does not resolve both opposing fluxes ([Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/), renal gluconeogenesis section and Table 2).

In the overnight-fasted human physiology summarized by Gerich, renal production is approximately 2.0–2.5 μmol/kg/min, representing 20–25% of total endogenous glucose production; the remainder is hepatic. This is a fasting-state estimate rather than a fixed T1D parameter, and renal contribution increases during prolonged fasting. Reports of substantially increased renal production and uptake in type 2 diabetes (T2D) establish that both fluxes can change together. They do not directly quantify either flux in insulin-deficient T1D. Reduced insulin suppression provides a mechanistic reason to expect altered renal production in T1D, but its magnitude requires T1D-specific measurements.

### Hyperfiltration and renal disease in T1D

Hyperfiltration describes abnormally high filtration, but its classification depends on the measurement method, threshold, age, body-size indexing and disease stage. Tonneijck et al. report prevalences of 10–67% across T1D studies; this wide range should not be interpreted as the probability for a contemporary individual. Their synthesis distinguishes three interacting mechanisms ([Tonneijck et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28143897/), Table 1 and pathogenesis sections):

1. Increased proximal sodium–glucose reabsorption reduces sodium chloride delivery to the macula densa. Reduced tubuloglomerular feedback can lower afferent arteriolar resistance and increase filtration.
2. Vasoactive pathways, including nitric oxide, prostaglandins and the renin–angiotensin system, alter afferent and efferent resistance and glomerular capillary pressure.
3. Tubular and glomerular growth alters transport capacity and filtration surface. Evidence that hypertrophy precedes hyperfiltration comes principally from animal experiments; temporal precedence is harder to establish in humans.

Glycaemic improvement can reduce hyperfiltration. The review cites an insulin-pump study in which glycated haemoglobin (HbA1c) fell from approximately 10% to 7% and measured GFR from 149 to 129 mL/min/1.73 m²; the latter corresponds to a calculated 13.4% reduction, not 16%. The original experiment was not independently inspected here, so these paired values remain a review-reported example rather than a calibrated treatment effect.

Whether hyperfiltration independently predicts progressive kidney disease is less certain than its mechanistic plausibility. Some measured-GFR cohorts associate it with later albuminuria, whereas larger estimated-GFR studies have not consistently reproduced that association. Confounding by glycaemia, disease stage and the method used to estimate filtration complicates causal interpretation. Hyperfiltration, albuminuria and subsequent GFR decline are related but distinct endpoints; an acute haemodynamic reduction in GFR is not itself evidence of long-term renal protection ([Tonneijck et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28143897/), clinical significance section).

Increased reabsorptive capacity has also been reported in diabetes. However, the expression evidence summarized by Poudel includes cultured urinary proximal tubular cells from people with **T2D**, and should not be described as a uniform increase in SGLT2 mRNA and protein in intact T1D kidneys. Historical increases in TmG cannot by themselves identify which transporter, cell number or regulatory process accounts for the difference ([Poudel, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23961473/), “Renal glucose handling in diabetes”).

### Glucosuria, water balance and SGLT inhibition

Glucose retained in tubular fluid contributes to osmotic diuresis, but there is no fixed conversion from grams of glucose excreted to millilitres of water lost. Urine volume depends on the combined excretion of solutes and achievable urine concentration; net dehydration additionally depends on fluid intake and extrarenal losses. A factor such as 15 mL/g would silently impose a particular urinary glucose concentration and would not specify electrolyte-free water clearance. It cannot predict daily fluid loss from plasma glucose alone. Severe hyperglycaemia can therefore produce polyuria and dehydration without supporting a universal 200–500 g/day glucose or 3–7 L/day water-loss range at 25 mmol/L.

SGLT2 inhibition lowers reabsorption and increases glucose delivery to downstream SGLT1. Partial recovery by SGLT1 explains why blocking SGLT2 does not cause excretion of all filtered glucose; this does not require assuming compensatory upregulation of SGLT1 expression. The effect depends on glucose concentration, filtration and residual transport. Dapagliflozin and empagliflozin principally inhibit SGLT2; sotagliflozin inhibits both SGLT1 and SGLT2 and should not be treated as pharmacologically identical ([Ghezzi et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30132032/)).

The clinical balance in T1D differs from that in T2D. In the exploratory pooled Dapagliflozin Evaluation in Patients With Inadequately Controlled Type 1 Diabetes (DEPICT-1/2) analysis, adults with T1D and HbA1c 7.5–10.5% received adjunctive dapagliflozin or placebo for up to 52 weeks. The efficacy population comprised 530, 529 and 532 participants receiving 5 mg, 10 mg and placebo; 55 incorrectly allocated participants were excluded from efficacy analyses but retained in safety analyses. At week 24, adjusted HbA1c changes were −0.40, −0.43 and 0.00 percentage points; at week 52 they were −0.20, −0.23 and +0.07 points. These are within-group changes, not all placebo-adjusted differences ([Phillip et al., 2021](https://eprints.whiterose.ac.uk/id/eprint/169674/1/dom.14248.pdf), Methods §2.5 and Results §3.2).

In the safety populations, adjudicated definite diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) affected 22/548 participants (4.0%) with 5 mg, 20/566 (3.5%) with 10 mg and 6/532 (1.1%) with placebo. Glucosuria can moderate hyperglycaemia while insufficient insulin still permits lipolysis and hepatic ketogenesis. Consequently, marked hyperglycaemia is not required for DKA. The trial classified euglycaemic DKA using glucose below 250 mg/dL (13.9 mmol/L) near peak β-hydroxybutyrate, not a universal 200 mg/dL cutoff; percentages of “euglycaemic” events depend on that definition. Missed insulin and pump failure were frequently assigned precipitating factors (Phillip et al., Table 2). The physiological implication is that glucose concentration alone cannot establish adequate suppression of ketogenesis.

Renal protection in T1D is not established by these glycaemic trials. In a post-hoc DEPICT analysis restricted to 251 participants with baseline albuminuria, the 52-week placebo-adjusted urinary albumin-to-creatinine ratio change was −13.3% (95% CI −37.2 to 19.8) with 5 mg and −31.1% (−49.9 to −5.2) with 10 mg. Corresponding eGFR differences were +3.27 (−0.92 to 7.45) and +2.12 (−2.03 to 6.27) mL/min/1.73 m². Thus, the stronger dose showed an albuminuria signal, but neither eGFR confidence interval excluded no difference. These surrogate findings do not demonstrate slower chronic GFR decline or fewer kidney-failure events in T1D ([Groop et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32946821/), original abstract inspected; full-text appraisal pending). Renal outcome benefits demonstrated in T2D cannot simply be transferred to T1D.

The institutional Phillip PDF was retrieved and its identity verified. This was a targeted claim audit, not a comprehensive literature search update.

<a name="brain-glucose-consumption"></a>
## 4. The brain's glucose consumption

Cerebral function depends on continuing substrate delivery, transport into tissue and intracellular energy metabolism. Glucose is the principal fuel in the usual fed and overnight-fasted state, but it is not the only fuel the brain can use. The dominant uptake pathways differ from insulin-responsive GLUT4 recruitment in skeletal muscle: GLUT1 and GLUT3 support uptake without requiring an acute rise in insulin. This distinction does not make cerebral metabolism independent of glucose supply, vascular function or insulin signalling ([Mergenthaler et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23968694/)).

For type 1 diabetes (T1D), three consequences follow. Excess exogenous insulin can lower the circulating substrate supply; recurrent hypoglycaemia can diminish warning and counterregulatory responses; and diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) can cause cerebral injury through mechanisms that are not reducible to plasma glucose alone. These processes must not be collapsed into one deterministic concentration-to-injury relationship.

### Transport across the blood–brain barrier and into cells

Blood-to-brain transport crosses both the luminal and abluminal membranes of brain microvascular endothelium. The heavily glycosylated approximately 55-kDa GLUT1 species is prominent in the endothelium; a less glycosylated approximately 45-kDa species is found in glia, and neurons prominently express GLUT3. Glucose phosphorylation and subsequent metabolism maintain an inward concentration gradient, while facilitative carriers permit transport in both directions ([Mergenthaler et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23968694/)).

Membrane distribution is experimentally and species dependent. In rat brain capillaries, Devraj et al. reported a luminal:intracellular:abluminal immunogold labelling ratio of approximately 1:1:2.45; their biochemical membrane work also used bovine preparations. This is not a measured human 2.5-fold transport-capacity ratio. The study further showed that phosphorylation affects antibody recognition, illustrating why measured labelling density must not be equated directly with functional transporter abundance ([Devraj et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21910135/), electron microscopy results and Discussion).

Apparent blood–brain transport parameters likewise depend on what is measured and how compartments are represented. Duarte et al. reanalysed published human and rat brain-glucose data using reversible and four-state conformational models. Their frequently cited Kt range of 1.5–3.5 mmol/L spans **different species and datasets**, not a directly established human normal range. The human four-state fit gave Kt 2.1 ± 5.2 mmol/L (standard error; reported confidence interval 0–12.6), indicating poor precision. A simpler reversible model fitted those data comparably well ([Duarte et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20027232/), Figure 2 and Table 1).

These models explain why bulk brain glucose can be approximately linearly related to plasma glucose over a physiological range despite saturable molecular transport. They do not establish that the barrier is “near saturated” at all euglycaemic concentrations, or that a universal failure point occurs at 3 or 4 mmol/L. Bulk glucose in μmol/g tissue, intracellular concentration, interstitial concentration and glucose-tracer uptake are different measurements. A tissue-to-plasma ratio therefore requires an explicit distribution volume and experimental context before it can be used quantitatively. Molecular transporter affinity is also not a threshold for symptoms or neuronal injury.

### Glucose oxidation, regional demand and glycogen

Mergenthaler et al. summarize an adult cerebral glucose utilization rate of approximately 5.6 mg/100 g/min. For an assumed 1.4-kg brain, that corresponds to a **calculated** 113 g/day, or 31.1 μmol/100 g/min. These are unit conversions from a review-level reference value, not three independently measured constants. By contrast, 25–30 μmol/100 g/min would correspond to approximately 91–109 g/day for the same brain mass. The commonly cited approximately 20% cerebral share concerns whole-body energy demand; it should not be relabelled as a 20–25% share of systemic glucose disposal ([Mergenthaler et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23968694/), introduction and glucose metabolism sections).

Much of cerebral adenosine triphosphate (ATP) supports ion-gradient restoration, synaptic activity and cellular maintenance. The resting oxygen-to-glucose uptake ratio is close to, but below, the six oxygen molecules required for complete oxidation of one glucose molecule. A reported ratio around 5.5–5.8 is compatible with predominantly oxidative metabolism, while glucose carbon also supports biosynthesis and some net lactate release. It does not establish that precisely 10% of glucose is exported as lactate. Regional rates differ with cell composition, activity and measurement method; a single whole-brain rate should not be assigned unchanged to gray matter, white matter or individual nuclei (Mergenthaler et al.).

Glycogen, located predominantly in astrocytes, provides a small regulated supplement to blood-borne glucose. In a paired study of five healthy adults, Öz et al. used occipital-lobe ¹³C magnetic resonance spectroscopy during two-hour hypoglycaemic and euglycaemic clamps. Glycogen label washout was higher during hypoglycaemia, with glucose averaging approximately 3.2 rather than 5.3 mmol/L. The authors estimated that 0.4–0.6 μmol/g glycogen was mobilized—about 15% of the pool—and that its contribution remained approximately 1% of cerebral glucose utilization under their assumptions. Blood therefore remained the principal glucose source ([Öz et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19502412/), Methods, Results and Discussion).

Their euglycaemic turnover model estimated glycogen content around 3.5 μmol glucosyl units/g tissue. Converting that concentration gives approximately 0.57 g glycogen/kg tissue, or 0.8 g in an assumed 1.4-kg brain; this calculation uses the 162.14 g/mol mass of a glycogen glucosyl residue, not free glucose. Neither this regional, model-derived concentration nor division by a resting metabolic rate establishes a time to loss of consciousness. Pool accessibility, mobilization rate, residual blood supply and changing energy demand matter. A separate five-person experiment found increased glycogen synthesis after hypoglycaemia, but did not establish glycogen supercompensation as the cause of impaired awareness in T1D (Öz et al.).

### Lactate exchange and adaptation to alternative fuel

The astrocyte–neuron lactate shuttle hypothesis proposes that astrocytic glutamate uptake increases glycolysis and lactate export, with neuronal uptake supporting oxidation. Monocarboxylate transporters mediate lactate exchange; lactate dehydrogenase converts lactate to pyruvate, which can then enter mitochondrial oxidative metabolism. Lactate oxidation is therefore not completed by lactate dehydrogenase alone. Evidence that the brain can use lactate does not establish the magnitude or obligatory direction of lactate transfer between astrocytes and neurons in vivo. Direct neuronal glucose use, astrocytic oxidation and lactate efflux also have to be included in cerebral carbon and oxygen balances ([Mergenthaler et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23968694/), discussion of neuroenergetic models).

Prolonged fasting provides direct evidence that ketone bodies can replace much of cerebral glucose oxidation, but it is not equivalent to acute insulin-induced hypoglycaemia. Owen et al. studied three adults with obesity after 38–41 days of fasting using cerebral arteriovenous substrate measurements; cerebral blood flow was measured in two. Their calculated oxygen equivalents for β-hydroxybutyrate and acetoacetate totalled 1.77 mmol/L blood, compared with measured oxygen extraction of 2.96 mmol/L—approximately 60%, calculated from Table V. The authors estimated glucose oxidation at approximately 24 g/day after subtracting lactate and pyruvate efflux and assuming a 1.4-kg brain ([Owen et al., 1967](https://www.jci.org/articles/view/105650/files/pdf), Tables I, IV–V and pp. 1592–1593). The small, selected, sedated sample and prolonged starvation protocol preclude treating these values as acute T1D rescue fractions or a universal 3–7-day adaptation schedule. During hyperinsulinaemic hypoglycaemia, insulin also suppresses peripheral generation of alternative fuels ([McCrimmon and Sherwin, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20876723/)).

### Counterregulation, symptoms and cerebral function in T1D

In healthy adults, controlled lowering of glucose generally suppresses endogenous insulin before recruiting glucagon and sympathoadrenal responses, with prominent symptoms and cognitive impairment developing at lower concentrations. Sprague and Arbeláez summarize counterregulatory recruitment around 3.6–3.9 mmol/L and symptoms around 2.8–3.0 mmol/L; these are review-level approximations from experimental protocols, not a clinical staircase through which every person passes. Prior hypoglycaemia shifts responses downward, whereas chronically elevated glucose can shift them upward. Severe hypoglycaemia is defined by impaired function requiring assistance, not by a universal seizure or coma concentration ([Sprague and Arbeláez, 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22783644/), “Glycemic thresholds for hypoglycemic responses” and diabetes sections).

T1D removes the ability to rapidly suppress the delivery of exogenous insulin and often impairs the glucagon response to hypoglycaemia. Failure of changing intra-islet signals is one proposed contributor to the glucagon defect, but pancreatic and central mechanisms remain under investigation; it should not be assigned an invariant five-year onset. Antecedent hypoglycaemia, exercise and sleep can further reduce sympathoadrenal and warning responses, producing **hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF)**. The effect depends on exposure and context, rather than a single ≥60-minute qualifying event. Impaired awareness and defective counterregulation overlap but are not identical phenotypes, so their historical risk estimates should not be interchanged ([McCrimmon and Sherwin, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20876723/); [Sprague and Arbeláez, 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22783644/)).

HAAF is not synonymous with structural autonomic neuropathy. Components of awareness and counterregulation can improve after avoiding recurrent hypoglycaemia; historical intervention studies summarized by Dagogo-Jack reported recovery within two to three weeks in some participants. This demonstrates reversibility of components, not assured or complete recovery in every person ([Dagogo-Jack, 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26604275/)).

Evidence for altered cerebral substrate handling must also be separated from evidence for a particular molecular mechanism. Boyle et al. studied 24 insulin-dependent participants stratified by glycated haemoglobin (HbA1c) and 15 controls. At approximately 3.0 mmol/L, the lowest-HbA1c subgroup did not show a statistically significant fall in cerebral glucose uptake, whereas the other diabetic groups and controls did. This was not a test at 2.5 mmol/L, a randomized comparison of intensive therapy, or a direct measurement of GLUT1 upregulation ([Boyle et al., 1995](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7491135/), original abstract inspected; full-text methods pending). Other experiments have not consistently found increased whole-brain transport after hypoglycaemia, suggesting that exposure, regional responses and alternative substrates may matter ([McCrimmon and Sherwin, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20876723/), pp. 2337–2338). Preserved uptake in one protocol cannot be equated with protection from severe or prolonged hypoglycaemia.

Conversely, symptoms during a fall from chronically high glucose can occur at higher concentrations than in a person accustomed to normoglycaemia. This relative shift in perceived hypoglycaemia does not by itself demonstrate cerebral glucose deprivation at 5–6 mmol/L or prove downregulation of blood–brain barrier GLUT1. Symptom perception, autonomic activation and measured neuroglycopenia are different endpoints.

### Brain injury, cognition and DKA

Long-term cognitive outcomes depend on developmental stage, cumulative glycaemia, vascular complications and exposure definitions; imaging differences cannot automatically be attributed to recurrent hypoglycaemia alone. Earlier Diabetes Control and Complications Trial/Epidemiology of Diabetes Interventions and Complications (DCCT/EDIC) follow-up was reassuring about severe hypoglycaemia and adult cognition, but should not be used as an indefinite null result. At 32 years, an observational analysis of 1,051 participants, with median age increasing from 27 to 59 years, associated higher HbA1c, more severe hypoglycaemic episodes and higher systolic pressure with poorer psychomotor and mental efficiency. These associations do not isolate a causal effect of hypoglycaemia, but they contradict an unqualified conclusion that adult cognition is unaffected ([Jacobson et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34051936/), original abstract inspected; full-text appraisal pending). A universal 4–6-point IQ penalty or a precise age-six boundary is not established by the sources appraised here.

DKA-associated cerebral injury is especially important in children. Clinical cerebral oedema is uncommon, whereas imaging or cognitive abnormalities may occur without overt oedema. Azova et al. summarize clinically apparent oedema in approximately 0.3–1% of paediatric DKA episodes and case-fatality estimates of 20–30% among affected cases; these heterogeneous historical estimates are not mortality risks for all children with DKA. Subclinical imaging abnormalities have been observed during treatment and sometimes before it, so “most children already have swelling at presentation” is too specific ([Azova et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33197066/)).

The mechanistic evidence supports overlapping metabolic/cytotoxic injury, altered oxygen delivery or utilization, inflammation, blood–brain barrier permeability and osmotic effects. Hypoperfusion followed by reperfusion is a proposed pathway, not a complete established explanation. Azova et al. specifically caution that human cerebral-flow studies do not demonstrate the profound ischaemia observed in some rodent DKA preparations. Injury or oedema before treatment also shows that treatment-related reperfusion is not necessary in every case. Severe acidosis, hypocapnia and elevated urea identify higher-risk presentations, but correlations with severity or treatments do not prove that any one factor independently causes the injury.

The Pediatric Emergency Care Applied Research Network (PECARN) trial randomized fluid rate and saline concentration in 1,389 DKA episodes among 1,255 children. Clinically apparent brain injury occurred in 12 episodes (0.9%); the tested regimens did not differ significantly in neurological outcomes ([Kuppermann et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29897851/), original abstract inspected). This challenges a simple claim that faster fluid replacement within those protocols causes cerebral injury. It does not establish equivalence of all possible fluid regimens or generalize securely to profound neurological compromise at presentation; the trial's exclusions and limited number of severe events matter, as discussed by Azova et al. Plasma glucose alone provides neither an oedema threshold nor a sufficient description of the underlying injury.

Sudden unexplained nocturnal death is a separate clinical observation, not a demonstrated cerebral glucose-depletion endpoint. A reported case documented very low sensor glucose near death in a young man with T1D, supporting hypoglycaemia as one possible contributor. A single case cannot estimate incidence, establish fatal arrhythmia, or confirm an orexin-mediated arousal mechanism ([Tanenberg et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19833577/), abstract-level appraisal). These distinctions are important when separating observed outcomes from plausible mechanisms.

### Sources

Abbreviations: T1D, type 1 diabetes; DKA, diabetic ketoacidosis; ATP, adenosine triphosphate; GLUT, facilitative glucose transporter; CMRglc, cerebral metabolic rate of glucose; HAAF, hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure; HbA1c, glycated haemoglobin; DCCT/EDIC, Diabetes Control and Complications Trial/Epidemiology of Diabetes Interventions and Complications; PECARN, Pediatric Emergency Care Applied Research Network; IQ, intelligence quotient; CI, confidence interval.

The Owen publisher PDF was retrieved and its identity verified. This was a targeted claim audit, not a comprehensive search update.
