*Implementation: see [§3 in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#glucose). F01 is a lumped insulin-independent glucose-disposal term, including brain and other tissues, rather than a directly measured cerebral metabolic rate. Its low-glucose adjustment does not establish a universal symptom or injury threshold. Regional metabolism, explicit blood–brain barrier transport, cerebral glycogen dynamics, alternative-fuel use and DKA-associated cerebral injury are not individually represented by that term.*

---

# Part 2: Macronutrient handling

<a name="carbohydrates"></a>
## 5. Carbohydrates -- from mouth to blood glucose

Starch and disaccharides require digestion to monosaccharides before intestinal absorption; ingested glucose does not. Absorbed carbohydrate passes through the portal circulation before contributing to systemic glucose. Gastric emptying is an important determinant of nutrient delivery, but the observed glucose concentration also reflects endogenous glucose production and tissue uptake. A postprandial glucose curve is therefore not a direct measurement of absorption ([Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/); [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/)). Gastric emptying in T1D can be normal, delayed or accelerated; acute glycaemia, gastrointestinal phenotype and study population all affect interpretation.

### Mechanism

#### Cephalic phase and gastric processing

Sight, smell and oral exposure to food trigger a vagally mediated cephalic-phase response that increases gastric tone, salivary α-amylase secretion, and gastrin release before the bolus arrives. Once swallowed, the meal is mixed in the gastric body and fundus and propelled distally by phasic antral contractions ("antral grinding"). The pylorus functions as a graded sieve: liquid and finely dispersed semi-solid particles pass freely, whereas solid particles are retained until they have been reduced to ~1–2 mm by mechanical and acid–enzymatic disintegration ([Kong & Singh, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18577009/)). Liquids therefore empty in a roughly first-order pattern with a half-time of 15–25 min for water and 20–60 min for nutrient liquids, while solids show a characteristic biphasic profile — an initial lag phase (10–60 min) during which trituration occurs, followed by an approximately linear emptying phase ([Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/)).

Small-intestinal nutrient exposure inhibits further gastric delivery through neural and hormonal feedback, including cholecystokinin (CCK), glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) and peptide YY (PYY). Gastric energy delivery in healthy adults is often summarised as approximately 1–4 kcal/min, but this is a broad physiological range rather than an invariant clamp across meal size, physical form and composition. Larger meals can take longer to empty without implying a fixed linear relation between calories and emptying time. Gastric delivery is distinct from systemic glucose appearance, which also incorporates digestion, absorption and splanchnic metabolism ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/)).

#### Luminal digestion

Salivary α-amylase initiates starch hydrolysis in the mouth and gastric body, but is rapidly inactivated below pH ~3.5; pancreatic α-amylase, secreted into the duodenum, is the principal endoluminal starch-hydrolysing enzyme. α-Amylase cleaves α-1,4-glycosidic bonds randomly within amylose and amylopectin chains, generating maltose, maltotriose, and α-limit dextrins. Final hydrolysis to free monosaccharide occurs at the apical brush-border by the membrane-anchored disaccharidases **maltase-glucoamylase** (MGAM, encoded by *MGAM*), **sucrase-isomaltase** (SI), and **lactase-phlorizin hydrolase** (LCT). These enzymes liberate glucose, fructose, and galactose at or just within the unstirred water layer overlying the enterocyte apical membrane, positioning the monosaccharide products for immediate transporter capture.

#### Apical and basolateral transport in the enterocyte

Three transporters carry the bulk of dietary monosaccharide flux across the enterocyte ([Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/); [Roder et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24587098/)):

- **SGLT1** (SLC5A1; apical) is a Na⁺-coupled secondary-active symporter with 2 Na⁺ : 1 glucose stoichiometry, transporting both glucose and galactose. Reported apparent K_m values for glucose are ~0.5 mmol/L at fully active membrane potential and Na⁺ gradient ([Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/)) but rise toward ~1.8 mmol/L under enterocyte-realistic intracellular glucose concentrations in computational reconstructions ([Afshar et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34899386/)). Functional saturation occurs at luminal glucose concentrations around 30 mmol/L. Genetic loss of SGLT1 (glucose–galactose malabsorption) produces near-complete failure of intestinal glucose absorption — a ~73% reduction in plasma tracer appearance after an enteral load in murine knockouts and severe osmotic diarrhoea in humans ([Gorboulev et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22124465/)) — which establishes SGLT1 as the obligate quantitative pathway for dietary glucose under physiological conditions in humans.

- **GLUT5** (SLC2A5; apical) is a Na⁺-independent facilitative carrier specific for fructose, with K_m ≈ 6 mmol/L for human GLUT5 ([Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/)). It does not transport glucose or galactose.

- **GLUT2** (SLC2A2; basolateral) is a low-affinity, high-capacity facilitative carrier (K_m ≈ 17 mmol/L for glucose, 76 mmol/L for fructose, 92 mmol/L for galactose; [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/)) that mediates monosaccharide efflux from the enterocyte to the portal blood. Whether GLUT2 is also recruited to the apical membrane during high-carbohydrate meals — and provides up to ~3-fold additional absorptive capacity under those conditions — has been proposed in rodent models ([Kellett & Helliwell, 2000](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10677310/); [Kellett & Brot-Laroche, 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16186394/)) and disputed in others ([Gorboulev et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22124465/); apical GLUT2 was not detected by immunofluorescence in murine knockout-controlled studies). The clinical relevance in humans is unresolved — the persistence of severe diarrhoea in SGLT1-deficient patients argues against a major apical-GLUT2 bypass route in vivo (controversy summarised by [Roder et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24587098/)).

#### Splanchnic disposition

Monosaccharide absorbed from the small intestine drains directly via mesenteric and portal veins to the liver. In healthy adults, **first-pass splanchnic glucose extraction** (portal-bed and hepatic removal of absorbed glucose before it reaches systemic circulation) removes 25–40% of the ingested load before it reaches the systemic circulation, the bulk of which is hepatic uptake driven by the high portal-vein glucose and insulin concentrations ([Ludvik et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9410915/) — measured 37.8 ± 5.9% splanchnic extraction with hepatic vein catheterisation after a 75 g OGTT in normoglycaemic controls; [Ferrannini et al., 1985](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3893478/)). Glucose taken up by the liver is incorporated into glycogen or oxidised, suppressing endogenous glucose production via direct hepatic insulin signalling and the portal–peripheral insulin gradient (see §2). Fructose is almost entirely cleared in the first hepatic pass — hepatocyte fructokinase (KHK) phosphorylates fructose to fructose-1-phosphate without insulin involvement, yielding ~20% conversion to glucose, ~25% to glycogen and the remainder to lactate, with little fructose reaching peripheral plasma after typical doses. Galactose, similarly, is converted to UDP-galactose and UDP-glucose by the hepatic Leloir pathway and contributes minimally to systemic glycaemia.

#### Gastric emptying as the principal kinetic determinant

Because intestinal absorptive capacity (SGLT1 + GLUT2 + GLUT5 in the proximal small intestine) substantially exceeds the rate at which the stomach normally delivers nutrient to the duodenum, **gastric emptying is the rate-limiting step** for postprandial glucose appearance under most physiological conditions ([Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/); [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). Five meal properties account for most of the inter-meal variability in glycaemic response:

1. **Liquid versus solid form.** Liquids bypass the antral grinding and pyloric sieve mechanisms and empty in a roughly first-order pattern. A 75 g oral glucose drink (the standard OGTT; oral glucose tolerance test, 75 g oral glucose challenge) produces peak plasma glucose at 30–45 min in healthy adults; the same carbohydrate content in solid form (e.g., a starchy meal of equivalent mass) typically peaks at 60–90 min ([Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/)).

2. **Caloric and volumetric load.** Volume and energy content have distinguishable effects. Kwiatek et al. studied 16 healthy adults using iso-osmolar nutrient liquids of 200–800 mL and 200–400 kcal; larger volumes emptied faster in their fitted analysis. The indexed abstract and inspected publisher Results extracts disagree on the direction of the calorie coefficient, so that coefficient is withheld pending complete original-text appraisal. The experiment does not establish a universal T1D meal constant ([Kwiatek et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19779010/)).

3. **Macronutrient composition — fat in particular.** Fat is the most potent single-meal modulator of gastric emptying. Long-chain fatty acids in the duodenum trigger CCK release from I-cells, GLP-1 from L-cells, and PYY from L-cells; these hormones slow antral motility and stimulate tonic pyloric contraction, prolonging gastric retention ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). The mechanism is developed in §6.

4. **Fibre as a kinetic modulator.** Dietary fibre is not absorbed as glucose but it is far from inert kinetically. Two distinct mechanisms operate concurrently and on different time scales:

    - *Soluble viscous fibre* (β-glucan in oats and barley, pectin in fruit, psyllium): the dissolved polysaccharide forms a viscous gel that increases bulk gastric content viscosity, impairs antral grinding and pyloric flow, and — once in the duodenum and jejunum — thickens the unstirred water layer overlying the brush border. The unstirred-layer effect retards diffusion of liberated monosaccharide to SGLT1, and is now considered to account for the larger fraction of viscous fibre's glycaemic benefit ([Würsch & Pi-Sunyer, 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9353622/)). The magnitude depends on fibre dose, viscosity, food preparation and the comparator. A specific β-glucan dose–response or regulatory threshold is not retained here because the inherited 1997 citation does not establish the later meta-analytic and regulatory claims.

    - *Insoluble structural fibre* (cellulose, hemicelluloses, lignin in wheat bran, vegetable matrices): does not form a gel but adds bulk, prolongs the antral grinding phase, and traps starch granules within an intact cell-wall matrix that resists amylase access ("food-matrix effect"; [Liljeberg & Björck, 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8942411/)). The food-matrix effect explains why intact whole-grain rye bread produces a flatter postprandial glucose curve than white bread of the same starch content, despite similar chemical starch composition.

    Removing the natural fibre matrix from a starchy food (e.g., grinding whole grain into refined flour) accelerates both gastric emptying and intestinal digestion, raising both peak glucose and iAUC ([Frost et al., 2003](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12571662/)). Fibre is therefore quantitatively important to the glycaemic response and, on the EU/Nordic nutrition-label convention, is listed as a separate quantity from "carbohydrate" (which on those labels denotes digestible sugar + starch); the US/FDA convention bundles fibre into "total carbohydrate". The reporting difference matters for BG-modelling exercises and clinical carbohydrate counting but does not alter the underlying physiology.

5. **Glycaemic index (GI) is mechanistically a composite of these factors.** GI ranks foods by the iAUC of plasma glucose over 2 h after ingestion of 50 g available carbohydrate, normalised to glucose or white bread ([Jenkins et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6259925/); [Atkinson et al., 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2584181/)). Mechanistically, GI integrates gastric-emptying rate, food-matrix integrity, viscous-fibre content, fat content, and starch crystallinity into a single number, which limits its predictive precision. A specific percentage for between-laboratory and between-person variability is not retained because the inherited Wolever attribution did not identify the intended source.

Food structure, processing, viscosity and nutrient feedback alter carbohydrate delivery, but a food name or glycaemic-index value does not determine a unique time to peak systemic glucose appearance. Quantitative food comparisons require an identified preparation, portion, population and measurement method. Glucose tablets do not require disaccharidase hydrolysis; honey is a variable sugar mixture and should not share an assumed fixed profile with pure glucose.

### Quantitative characterization

| Quantity | Value | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gastric energy delivery | Approximately 1–4 kcal/min | Broad range in health, not an invariant meal-independent flux or T1D-specific maximum appearance rate | [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/) |
| Pyloric sieve solid-particle threshold | 1–2 mm | Established by gastric-aspirate sieving and disintegration studies | [Kong & Singh, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18577009/) |
| Gastric emptying t½ — water/dilute liquid | ~15–25 min | First-order kinetics | [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/) |
| Gastric emptying t½ — nutrient liquid | ~20–60 min | Review-level range; depends on meal composition, volume and measurement endpoint | [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/) |
| Gastric emptying lag phase — solid mixed meal | 10–60 min | Followed by approximately linear emptying | [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/) |
| SGLT1 K_m (glucose) | ~0.5–1.8 mmol/L | Range reflects heterologous expression conditions versus computational reconstruction at enterocyte-realistic intracellular glucose; direct in vivo human K_m has not been measured | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/); [Afshar et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34899386/) |
| SGLT1 saturation luminal glucose | ~30 mmol/L | Beyond which additional uptake mechanisms have been proposed but remain disputed in humans | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/) |
| GLUT5 K_m (fructose) | ~6 mmol/L | Human isoform | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/) |
| GLUT2 K_m (glucose; basolateral) | ~17 mmol/L | Low-affinity, high-capacity efflux | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/) |
| First-pass splanchnic glucose extraction (75 g OGTT, healthy) | ~25–40% (one HVC study: 37.8 ± 5.9%) | Hepatic vein catheterisation; small samples; method-dependent estimates | [Ludvik et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9410915/); [Ferrannini et al., 1985](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3893478/) |
| Plasma glucose peak time, 75 g OGTT, healthy | ~30–45 min | Substantial inter-individual variation | [Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/) |
| Plasma glucose peak time, mixed solid meal, healthy | ~60–90 min | Composition-dependent | [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/) |
| Bioavailability of digestible CHO (sugar + starch) | ~95–100% to portal blood | Excludes resistant starch (typically 1–10% of total starch depending on preparation) | [Englyst & Cummings, 1986](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3789750/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Loss of incretin-amplified insulin secretion does not abolish gastrointestinal feedback.** In T1D, loss of functional β-cell mass limits the insulin-secretory component of the incretin response. GLP-1 can still slow gastric emptying; this effect should not be assigned to GIP, which did not slow emptying in the human studies reviewed by Marathe and colleagues. Gastric emptying in T1D may be normal, delayed or accelerated, depending on population and physiological state. It should not be reduced to one normal absorption curve lacking only its accompanying insulin pulse ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/)).

2. **Diabetic gastroparesis: heterogeneous gastric neuromuscular dysfunction.**

    **Epidemiology and definition.** Estimates depend on whether studies require symptoms, objectively delayed emptying, or both, and whether participants are community-dwelling or referred for gastrointestinal symptoms. Choung's reported 5.2% ten-year cumulative incidence in T1D is not a prevalence estimate and should not be pooled informally with Ye's reported 8.3% prevalence (95% CI 6.4–10.2%). These inherited estimates have not been independently reappraised here; ascertainment and population must be checked before comparing them ([Choung et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22343744/); [Ye et al., 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37640738/)). Delayed emptying without symptoms is also not interchangeable with clinically diagnosed symptomatic gastroparesis.

    **Pathophysiology.** The relevant abnormalities include vagal dysfunction, loss or dysfunction of interstitial cells of Cajal (the gastric pacemaker cells), impaired inhibitory nitrergic transmission involving neuronal nitric oxide synthase, and immune-cell changes in the gastric wall. Their relative contributions vary; a diagnosis does not establish irreversible autonomic neuropathy as the sole cause ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/); [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/), review-level evidence). Current HbA1c, cumulative glycaemic exposure and coexisting neuropathy are different candidate predictors and should not be treated as equivalent measures of disease severity.

    **Measurement.** Standardised solid-meal scintigraphy quantifies the fraction of the meal remaining at specified times. Percentage retention at four hours and gastric half-emptying time are different endpoints: there is no universal conversion of diagnostic retention categories into half-times or compartment-model parameters. The detailed severity thresholds and half-time mappings previously listed here require checking against the original consensus ([Abell et al., 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18287197/), full-text appraisal pending).

    **Insulin–meal mismatch.** Delayed delivery can place more nutrient appearance after the strongest action of a pre-meal insulin dose, allowing early glucose lowering and later glucose elevation. This is a possible pattern, not a fixed 60–90-minute insulin peak followed by a 3–5-hour meal peak in every person. Injected insulin does not pass through the stomach, but its absorption and action also vary. Specific bolus timing and pump strategies are clinical-management questions outside this physiological account ([Krishnasamy and Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/)).

    **Natural history.** Delayed emptying may persist, but cross-sectional comparisons of early- and long-duration T1D do not demonstrate an inevitable sequence from accelerated emptying to permanent gastroparesis. Symptoms, measured emptying and tissue pathology need not change together. The heterogeneous mechanisms and longitudinal evidence reviewed by Marathe and colleagues do not justify assigning all established gastroparesis an irreversible, fixed kinetic state.

3. **Acute hyperglycaemia modulates gastric emptying — a feedback loop relevant to hyperglycaemic episodes.** The relationship between acute glycaemia and gastric emptying in T1D is supported by controlled experiments, but its magnitude depends on protocol and population. Phillips et al. 2015 estimate that gastric emptying accounts for ~35% of the variance in peak postprandial blood glucose, both in healthy individuals and in those with diabetes ([Phillips et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25421372/) — based on abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist).

    **Slowing during acute hyperglycaemia — well-established at the group level.** The canonical demonstration is Schvarcz et al. 1997, who used an insulin-glucose clamp to compare gastric emptying at 4 mmol/L versus 8 mmol/L in 8 healthy subjects and 9 IDDM patients without GI symptoms or neuropathy. Liquid-phase t½ rose from 32.2 → 57.0 min in healthy (+77%, P = 0.002) and from 29.1 → 41.3 min in IDDM (+42%, P = 0.002), with parallel — but smaller — effects on solid-meal retention at 100 min (healthy: 36.7% → 55.2%; IDDM: 35.7% → 44.2%; both P = 0.004) ([Schvarcz et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9207262/) — numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). The different group estimates do not by themselves identify autonomic damage as the explanation. For severe hyperglycaemia (16–20 mmol/L), the systematic review by Halland & Bharucha 2016 pooled two T1D studies (n = 18 total) and reported t½ prolongation of 17–31 min versus euglycaemia ([Halland & Bharucha, 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4912904/)), broadly consistent with Schvarcz at the lower glucose range but not establishing a clear dose-response across studies. Plateau or ceiling effects above ~15 mmol/L have not been systematically characterised in humans.

    **Acceleration in early disease and adolescent T1D — paradoxical but reproducible.** A separate, often overlooked literature documents *faster*-than-control gastric emptying in early-stage and adolescent T1D, independent of current glycaemia. Perano et al. 2015 found gastric half-emptying of a ¹³C-labelled solid meal of 77.6 min (IQR 61.4–99.3) in 30 adolescents with T1D versus 109.1 min (IQR 70.8–124.2) in 15 matched controls (P = 0.02), with acceleration independent of HbA1c, diabetes duration, BMI, and study-day glycaemia ([Perano et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4428870/)). Horowitz et al. 1991 had previously observed in a 87-patient diabetic cohort (67 T1D, 20 T2D) versus 25 healthy controls that asymptomatic diabetics without orthostatic hypotension had solid-meal t½ of 70.1 ± 41.6 min — faster than the typical 90–120 min healthy adult range ([Horowitz et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2070801/)). A mechanistic candidate is offered by Kishi et al. 2019, who showed that streptozotocin-diabetic mice in the early disease phase (2–4 weeks) develop expansion of antral interstitial cells of Cajal (ICC density 60.9% vs 46.2%), with parallel acceleration of gastric Tmax (~48 vs ~110 min) ([Kishi et al., 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6785066/) — mouse model; mechanism not directly verified in human T1D). These observations come from different populations and experimental models; they do not establish a longitudinal biphasic disease course or demonstrate that acute hyperglycaemia accelerates emptying in early T1D.

    **HbA1c is a poor cross-sectional predictor.** Four of five studies reviewed by Halland & Bharucha 2016 found no significant correlation between current HbA1c and gastric emptying rate, although the EDIC sub-study (n = 78 T1D followed 27 years) reported that *cumulative* glycaemic exposure was independently associated with delayed emptying — implicating long-term rather than current hyperglycaemia. The authors conclude that "there is no evidence that improved control of glycemia improves gastric emptying or vice versa."

    **Mechanism.** Acute slowing involves vagal and nitrergic-pathway feedback from duodenal nutrient and glucose sensors, with sympathetic involvement at higher glucose levels ([Phillips et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25421372/)); the signalling cascade is incompletely mapped. Chronic delay in long-standing T1D involves ICC loss and immune-cell infiltration of the gastric wall (see point 2). Acceleration in early disease has been proposed to involve ICC hyperplasia (Kishi et al. 2019) but is not yet mechanistically established in humans.

    **Clinical consequence and evidence-quality caveats.** During an acute hyperglycaemic excursion, the slowing component creates a self-prolonging loop: an under-bolused meal raises plasma glucose, the elevated glucose then slows gastric emptying of the remaining meal, and the appearance curve broadens, frustrating correction-bolus titration. However, the underlying evidence base is thinner than the textbook narrative implies: the Schvarcz study, while methodologically strong, is a single small (n = 9) IDDM cohort with neuropathy explicitly excluded; the severe-hyperglycaemia evidence rests on two pooled studies of 18 patients total; and the paradoxical acceleration literature suggests that the textbook "T1D = slow emptying" generalisation does not hold uniformly across disease stages. Inter-individual variability is large in every study reviewed.

4. **Exercise and stress do not impose one emptying response.** Sympathoadrenal activation, exercise intensity, the physical form of the meal and prevailing glucose can influence gastric function. The cited evidence does not support a general rule that exercise or psychological stress accelerates gastric emptying, nor a universal shortening of pre-exercise snack absorption. Gastric delivery and exercise-related glucose disposal must be considered separately.

5. **Carbohydrate counting and fibre.** Fibre content and food structure can change the time course of carbohydrate delivery. This does not by itself prescribe a longer pre-bolus interval or a fixed subtraction from an individual insulin dose; treatment decisions depend on the person's regimen, glucose trajectory and clinical guidance. Food-label carbohydrate definitions also differ between jurisdictions, but those reporting conventions are not physiological bioavailability measurements.

### Physiological modulators of gastric emptying and intestinal absorption

The rate at which ingested carbohydrates appear in the bloodstream depends on two sequential processes, each independently modulated by physiological state:

1. **Gastric emptying** — the rate of chyme delivery from the stomach to the duodenum (τG in compartment models). This is the rate-limiting step for most solid meals.
2. **Intestinal absorption** — the rate of glucose transport across the intestinal epithelium into the portal circulation. This depends on epithelial transporter capacity (SGLT1/GLUT2) and critically on splanchnic blood flow, which removes absorbed glucose from the basolateral side of the enterocyte and maintains the concentration gradient that drives further uptake.

These two mechanisms are physiologically distinct: gastric emptying is governed primarily by vagal/sympathetic tone, pyloric feedback, and gastric distension, while intestinal absorption rate is dominated by mesenteric perfusion and enterocyte transporter density. A modulator can affect one without the other (e.g., hyperglycaemia slows gastric emptying via vagal feedback but does not directly reduce splanchnic flow), or both simultaneously (e.g., intense exercise increases sympathetic tone *and* redistributes blood flow away from the splanchnic bed).

#### Gastric emptying modulators

Gastric-emptying rate depends on physiological state as well as meal composition. The distinction between solid-meal retention, liquid half-emptying time and plasma glucose is essential: percentage changes in one cannot automatically be assigned to another.

1. **Stress and exercise.** Neural, hormonal and circulatory changes can alter gastrointestinal motility, but the direction and size depend on the stressor, exercise protocol and test meal. The inherited stress and exercise percentages had conflicting citation identities; no universal 10–50% multiplier is retained pending original-source verification.
2. **Time of day and sleep.** Morning-versus-evening meal comparisons do not isolate an effect of sleep. They cannot by themselves establish a fixed nocturnal delay or predict that bedtime carbohydrate appearance extends into the dawn period.
3. **Fasting state.** The interdigestive migrating motor complex differs from the fed motor pattern. Its existence does not establish that a subsequent meal empties 10–20% faster or that its initial absorption has been predictably “primed”.
4. **Acute glycaemia.** The controlled glucose-target comparisons above support acute slowing during hyperglycaemia. Effects at 4 versus 8 mmol/L and at more severe hyperglycaemia came from different protocols and should not be added as sequential increments. A faster baseline emptying phenotype in a separate early-T1D cohort does not reverse the demonstrated within-person hyperglycaemic response.
5. **Protein and fat.** Nutrient exposure stimulates intestinal feedback, including CCK and GLP-1. Preloads and nutrients incorporated in the meal are different interventions. The whey-preload example reviewed by Marathe involved type 2 diabetes, not a general healthy/T1D population; it does not establish a universal whey–casein–plant-protein hierarchy ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/)). See §6 for directly appraised T1D meal comparisons.
6. **Alcohol and osmolality.** Beverage composition, nutrient load and concentration can influence emptying. Liquid form alone does not guarantee rapid delivery. The inherited numerical modifiers and their original sources were not independently verified; a fixed absorption percentage or hypoglycaemia time window is therefore not assigned here.
7. **GLP-1 receptor stimulation.** GLP-1 can slow gastric emptying, with effects depending on baseline emptying and duration of exposure. Endogenous physiological effects, pharmacological GLP-1 and different receptor-agonist regimens should not be treated as interchangeable. A quantified medication effect in another population is not automatically a T1D meal-time multiplier or a bolus instruction ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/)). Agent-specific estimates and long-term tachyphylaxis require their own source-level appraisal.

These are evidence boundaries, not claims that the proposed modulators have no effect. The original experiments needed to quantify several of them remain unresolved in the accompanying review audit.

#### Intestinal absorption rate modulators

Exercise changes mesenteric perfusion, but the percentage change in blood flow is not a measured percentage change in glucose absorption. In Qamar and Read's study, Doppler-measured superior mesenteric artery flow fell by 43% immediately after 15 min of treadmill exercise, with reductions of 29% and 24% at five and ten minutes. The experiment measured blood flow, not intestinal glucose uptake or systemic glucose appearance. Its abstract supports exercise-related hypoperfusion but not a 40–60% absorption reduction or a post-exercise absorption overshoot ([Qamar and Read, 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3596339/), abstract-only appraisal).

Meal-related hyperaemia likewise demonstrates a vascular response, not a proportional increase in glucose absorption. Gastric delivery, sodium-coupled transport through SGLT1, mucosal metabolism and perfusion should be distinguished. The cited flow observations cannot identify a proposed glucose-gradient/product-inhibition mechanism or a universal post-exercise absorption rebound.

#### Intra- and inter-individual variability

A central challenge for insulin dosing and simulation alike is that the postprandial glucose appearance curve varies substantially between individuals and — less intuitively — within the same individual from day to day, even when meal composition and timing are controlled. Gastric emptying contributes, but concentration-only observations cannot partition the variability among emptying, absorption, endogenous production and insulin-dependent disposal.

**Inter-individual variability.** Gastric-emptying half-time (t½) for a standardised solid meal shows an inter-subject coefficient of variation (COV) of ~24–40% in healthy adults, depending on the cohort and measurement protocol. Tougas et al. (2000) reported inter-subject COV of ~24.5% for t½ in a large cohort (n=319, standardised egg meal, 4-hour scintigraphy; [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/)). Desai et al. (2018) found inter-subject COV of ~40% for t½ in a mixed clinical cohort (n=60; [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/)). Mean t½ for the standardised egg meal was 121.7 ± 29.8 min (median 120, 5th–95th percentile 78–174 min), with females emptying ~15% slower than males (127.7 ± 28.7 vs. 109.9 ± 28.6 min, P<0.0001; [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/)). These values describe the population spread of basal gastric emptying — the range across which individual "default" emptying speeds differ, before any modulator (meal composition, stress, exercise) is applied.

**Intra-individual variability.** Within the same individual eating the same standardised meal on different days, t½ varies with an intra-subject COV of ~12–24%, depending on the population studied. Among participants classified as having normal emptying in Desai's symptomatic clinical cohort, the reported intra-subject COV of ~12% for t½ — substantially less than the inter-subject spread ([Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/)). In patients with delayed emptying the intra-subject COV rose to ~18%, and in those with rapid emptying it reached ~28%. Collier et al. (1990) similarly found that intrasubject variability was not statistically significant in healthy volunteers but was significant in diabetic patients, who showed higher day-to-day fluctuation ([Collier et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2378789/)). Siegel et al. (1993) confirmed the pattern: diagnostic classification (normal vs. delayed vs. rapid) was reproduced in 70% of patients across two tests, but changed in 30%, indicating that the intra-individual noise is large enough to straddle clinical cut-offs in a substantial minority ([Siegel et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8281844/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/)).

**T1D-specific variability.** Diabetic patients show greater intra-individual variability than healthy subjects, likely reflecting day-to-day fluctuation in autonomic function, antecedent glycaemia (the hyperglycaemia-mediated emptying delay varies with prevailing glucose), and subclinical neuropathic changes. Bao et al. (2021) demonstrated that in children with T1D on insulin pumps and CGM, postprandial time-to-peak had a within-person standard deviation of 39–65 min across controlled and free-living meals — larger than the between-person standard deviation (6–19 min), yielding intraclass correlation coefficients (ICC) below 0.2 ([Bao et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8620341/)). Controlling meal macronutrients (matched carbohydrate, fat, and protein) did not substantially reduce within-person variability, showing that standardising macronutrients did not remove glucose-concentration variability. This does not identify which physiological or insulin-related contributor was dominant.

| Variability measure | Healthy subjects | Diabetic patients | Source |
|---------------------|------------------|-------------------|--------|
| Inter-subject COV, t½ | 24.5% (n=319, scintigraphy) | 47% (n=60, mixed clinical) | [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-subject COV, t½ (normal GE) | 12% | 20–21% | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-subject COV, t½ (delayed GE) | — | 18% | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-subject COV, t½ (rapid GE) | — | 28% | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Diagnostic reproducibility (normal/delayed/rapid) | 92% (t½ classification) | 70% (30% changed classification) | [Siegel et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8281844/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Within-person SD, time-to-peak BG (T1D children, controlled meal) | — | 39 min (ICC 0.18) | [Bao et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8620341/) |
| Within-person SD, time-to-peak BG (T1D children, free-living dinner) | — | 65 min (ICC 0.01) | [Bao et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8620341/) |

> **Research notes — variability:**
> - The distinction between gastric-emptying variability (measured by scintigraphy or ¹³C breath test) and postprandial glucose-concentration variability (measured by CGM time-to-peak) is important: the latter integrates emptying, absorption, splanchnic extraction, and insulin-on-board effects. CGM variability is not a direct estimate of gastric-emptying variability, and need not be a mathematical upper bound on it.
> - Menstrual-cycle effects on gastric emptying have been documented (slower in the luteal phase; [Collier et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2378789/)) but are infrequently controlled for in variability studies, which may inflate reported intra-individual COV in female subjects.
> - The contribution of gut microbiome composition to inter-individual gastric-emptying variability is an emerging research area with no quantitative data suitable for modelling as of 2026.

#### Measurement and modelling boundary

Gastric emptying, intestinal absorption and systemic glucose appearance are related but distinct processes. A fitted meal-response time constant combines several of them and cannot automatically inherit the numerical change measured in a different endpoint. Independent multiplication of meal, exercise, stress and glycaemic-state modifiers is a modelling assumption requiring validation, not a demonstrated physiological law. Model structures and their calibration are discussed in [§28](#mathematical-models) and [§29](#model-comparison).

<a name="fat-and-protein"></a>
## 6. Fat and protein -- the forgotten macronutrients

Postprandial glycaemia after a mixed meal reflects carbohydrate delivery, endogenous glucose production and glucose disposal. Dietary fat can slow gastric emptying and, under suitable experimental conditions, reduce insulin-stimulated disposal; protein supplies gluconeogenic amino acids and can stimulate glucagon. These mechanisms plausibly contribute to delayed glucose elevation, but the directly appraised T1D meal experiments below did not simultaneously quantify hormones and glucose fluxes. Their concentration curves establish the net response to meal composition, not the relative contribution of each pathway.

### Mechanism

#### Fat: gastric emptying delay and postprandial peripheral insulin resistance

Lipid entering the duodenum is sensed by enteroendocrine I cells (CCK), L cells (GLP-1, PYY) and vagal afferents. The resulting feedback inhibits antral motility, stimulates tonic pyloric pressure, and slows the delivery of luminal contents to the intestine; CCK and GLP-1 also independently slow gastric emptying through vagally mediated and direct gastric smooth-muscle effects ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). The response is **load-dependent**. In healthy adults, intraduodenal lipid infused at 0.25 kcal/min already stimulates CCK release and isolated pyloric pressure waves, but loads ≥1.5 kcal/min are required to elevate basal pyloric pressure and PYY, and ≥4 kcal/min to reduce ad-libitum energy intake ([Pilichiewicz et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17942490/)). The threshold for a measurable gastric emptying delay is therefore well below the lipid load delivered by a typical mixed meal containing ≥10–15 g fat, but the magnitude of the delay scales continuously with caloric load up to the saturation of antropyloroduodenal feedback at ~3–4 kcal/min. A fat preload predictably and substantially slows emptying of a co-ingested carbohydrate meal but, in T2D, produces only a modest reduction in peak postprandial glucose because the additional caloric load and the absence of a frank insulin secretagogue effect of fat itself partially offset the kinetic benefit ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)).

In addition to delaying glucose appearance, sustained elevations of plasma FFA reduce the rate of insulin-stimulated glucose disposal. The original explanation proposed by Randle and colleagues (the Randle cycle, or glucose–fatty acid cycle: competing substrate utilization mechanism; [Randle et al., 1963](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13990765/)) — that fatty-acid-derived acetyl-CoA and citrate inhibit pyruvate dehydrogenase and phosphofructokinase, redirecting muscle fuel selection — has been superseded for the human in vivo phenotype. Combined ¹³C/³¹P MRS and tracer studies in healthy adults during euglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamps with lipid/heparin infusion show that elevation of plasma FFA does not alter insulin-stimulated glucose uptake during the first 3 h, then progressively reduces whole-body glucose disposal to ~46% of control by 6 h, with parallel reductions of intramyocellular glucose-6-phosphate (onset ~1.5 h) and muscle glycogen synthesis (~50% reduction over 3–6 h) ([Roden et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8675698/)). The kinetics support impaired glucose **transport/phosphorylation** as an early limiting step rather than primary inhibition of pyruvate oxidation. Glucose oxidation nevertheless also decreased by approximately 40% from the third hour. The retained Roden HTML contains the original abstract, not its full methods/results; original-PDF appraisal remains pending. Mechanistically, intracellular accumulation of diacylglycerol (DAG) activates novel PKC isoforms (PKCθ in skeletal muscle, PKCε in liver), which serine-phosphorylate IRS-1 and the insulin receptor; serine phosphorylation impairs tyrosine phosphorylation, attenuating PI3K/Akt signalling and the downstream GLUT4 translocation ([Boden et al., 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12086926/); [Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). The relevance for postprandial glycemia is that the FFA tail of a high-fat meal — postprandial plasma FFA suppression by insulin is itself impaired in T1D when bolus dosing is mistimed — overlaps in time with the delayed glucose appearance from gastric emptying, so the same meal both delivers glucose late and reduces peripheral disposal capacity at the time it arrives.

#### Protein: gluconeogenesis, glucagonotropic stimulation of α-cells, and incretin release

Three candidate processes link dietary protein to postprandial glycaemia; their relative importance cannot be quantified from a glucose curve alone.

**(1) Direct gluconeogenesis from amino acid carbon.** Many dietary amino acids can contribute carbon to gluconeogenesis, but biochemical capacity does not specify the amount converted after a meal. Doubly-labelled (¹⁵N, ¹³C) tracer studies measure the actual conversion as substantially smaller. Fromentin and colleagues fed healthy adults 23 g of intrinsically labelled egg protein as a four-egg meal containing 19 g fat after a 12-hour fast and quantified that only 3.9 ± 0.7 g of glucose over 8 h originated from dietary amino acids — approximately 8% of the 50 g of total endogenous glucose produced over the period, peaking at a 12% contribution at 4.5 h ([Fromentin et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23274906/)). The bulk of the deaminated amino acid carbon was oxidized directly. Comparable tracer measurements in T2D and healthy adults after a 50 g protein load (cottage cheese, beef) yield 4–10 g of glucose over 8 h ([Gannon & Nuttall, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613553/)). These measurements do not establish a universal protein-to-glucose conversion or a T1D dosing factor. An eight-hour cumulative amount should not be relabelled as a demonstrated constant production rate.

**(2) α-cell stimulation and unopposed glucagon in T1D.** Amino acids — particularly arginine, alanine, glutamine and leucine — are direct glucagon secretagogues. Intravenous arginine produces 5- to 10-fold increases in plasma glucagon; oral protein produces a more modest 1.5- to 3-fold increase, with magnitude scaling with the amino-acid absorption rate ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/)). In healthy subjects the simultaneous β-cell amino-acid response (insulin secretion) (a) restrains α-cell glucagon release through paracrine signalling and (b) suppresses hepatic glucose production directly; the net postprandial glucose excursion attributable to protein is therefore small. In T1D the β-cell response is absent, and the α-cell glucagon response to oral protein is largely **preserved** — unlike the glucagon response to hypoglycemia, which is lost within ~5 years of diagnosis ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/)). The result is unopposed hepatic glucose output (glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis) over 2–5 h. Hormonal regulation and substrate supply can both contribute. The Smart and Paterson experiments below do not quantify which contribution dominates in a given T1D meal.

**(3) Incretin signalling and gastric delivery.** Amino acids can stimulate intestinal hormone release, and nutrient-dependent gastric feedback can influence mixed-meal delivery ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3631884/)). This does not establish the cause of an early glucose fall after a protein-only drink. In Paterson's T1D protocol, no extra bolus was given for the test drink, but participants remained on intensive insulin therapy and had eaten an insulin-covered evening meal four hours earlier. Glucagon, residual insulin secretion and endogenous glucose-production fluxes were not measured; a residual-β-cell explanation is therefore untested.

#### Time course

Paterson and colleagues' randomised crossover protocol was completed by 27 participants with T1D (mean age 21.7 ± 11.7 years). Drinks containing 0–100 g whey protein or 10/20 g glucose were taken four hours after a standardised, insulin-covered evening meal, without an additional bolus for the drink. Participants were not insulin-free. With 75 and 100 g protein, CGM-derived glucose was higher than after water during 180–300 min and continued rising at 300 min. At 240–300 min, differences from water were 1.65 mmol/L (95% CI 0.93–2.36) and 1.72 mmol/L (1.04–2.41), respectively. Early negative differences occurred even at high doses: −1.10 mmol/L after 75 g and −1.22 mmol/L after 100 g during 60–120 min. Thus the results do not establish a peak at 180 min, a universal 75 g threshold, or disappearance of early lowering above 50 g ([Paterson et al., 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5064639/), Methods and Table 2).

In mixed meals, fat and protein can prolong glucose elevation even with carbohydrate held constant, but glucose-concentration peak timing is not a direct measure of gastric emptying or systemic appearance.

#### Additivity of fat and protein effects

In a randomised crossover trial, 33 young people with T1D aged 8–17 years received four breakfasts with carbohydrate held constant within each participant. Full portions contained 30 g carbohydrate, 4 or 35 g fat, and 5 or 40 g protein; 12 participants weighing ≤45 kg received 75% portions. An individually standardised carbohydrate-based insulin dose was used for all four meals ([Smart et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836096/), Methods).

At 180 min, changes from pre-meal glucose were 2.4 mmol/L (95% CI 1.1–3.7) after low-fat/high-protein and 0.5 mmol/L (−0.8 to 1.8) after low-fat/low-protein meals (P = 0.02). At 210 min, high-fat/low-protein and low-fat/low-protein excursions were 1.8 mmol/L (0.3–3.2) and −0.5 mmol/L (−1.9 to 0.8), respectively (P = 0.01). The combined high-fat/high-protein meal exceeded all other meals from 180 to 300 min; at 300 min its excursion was 5.4 mmol/L greater than the low-fat/low-protein comparator, not 5.4 mmol/L above the pre-meal baseline.

At 180 min the combined excursion was 4.2 mmol/L, compared with 1.8 and 2.4 mmol/L in the single high-fat and high-protein conditions. These are within-meal excursions, not three effects relative to the low-fat/low-protein comparator. The nonsignificant interaction was consistent with additivity within this experiment, but does not establish exact linearity across doses or identify non-overlapping organ mechanisms. Mean glucose time-to-peak was 79 min (95% CI 68–89) for the low-fat/low-protein meal and 143 min (112–174) for the combined meal.

High-protein meals were associated with lower odds of hypoglycaemia (OR 0.16, 95% CI 0.06–0.41). This is an odds ratio, not a direct measurement of glucagon-mediated protection. Observations after treated hypoglycaemia were excluded from subsequent glucose analysis, which matters when interpreting later meal trajectories ([Smart et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836096/), Results and Discussion).

#### Protein source and unresolved comparisons

Protein source, digestion and amino-acid composition may influence glycaemic responses, but a quantitative ranking across foods requires an identified controlled experiment. Dao and colleagues' 2025 article is a review, not the randomised five-protein trial previously described here. Its indexed abstract does not contain the chicken/egg/beef/salmon/whey incremental-AUC values attributed to it. Those estimates are withheld pending identification and appraisal of the original comparison ([Dao et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39951019/), abstract-only; full text unresolved).

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Hormonal and substrate effects coexist.** Loss of normal β-cell responses changes the balance between amino-acid-stimulated glucagon and insulin action. The relative contributions of glucagon, gluconeogenic substrate, gastric delivery and peripheral disposal are not quantified by the meal studies above.
2. **Early lowering and delayed elevation can coexist.** Paterson's 75/100 g conditions showed early lowering relative to water as well as later elevation. No hormonal measurement in that experiment establishes residual β-cell secretion as the cause.
3. **Insulin and nutrient time courses may be mismatched.** The net response depends on formulation, delivery, background insulin and meal composition. A fixed insulin peak/duration and a fixed protein peak should not be assigned to every person.
4. **Hypoglycaemia odds depend on the tested protocol.** Smart's lower odds after high-protein meals were observed with standardised carbohydrate-based insulin. The result does not establish a universal preventive dose or prove the proposed glucagon mechanism.

5. **No consensus dosing algorithm for fat and protein in T1D.** Several practical algorithms (Pankowska fat–protein units, percentage uplift of carbohydrate-calculated dose, dual-wave/extended bolus) are in clinical use and are reviewed in pediatric and adult guidelines ([ISPAD 2022 nutritional management](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36468223/); [Evert, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32425452/)). Their selection and titration are clinical-practice questions outside the scope of this physiology document.

# Part 3: Insulin pharmacology

<a name="insulin-pharmacology"></a>
## 7. Insulin -- from injection to effect

In healthy physiology, the β-cell delivers insulin in 5–8 min secretory bursts directly into the hepatic portal vein, generating a portal-to-peripheral concentration gradient of approximately 2–3:1 and an oscillatory signal that the liver discriminates from sustained background ([Pørksen, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11845219/); [Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/)). Subcutaneous (SC) insulin therapy in T1D inverts this geometry: insulin enters peripheral venous return, bypasses hepatic first-pass extraction, and arrives at the hepatocyte and at peripheral tissues with a pharmacokinetic profile shaped almost entirely by the absorption from the SC depot. The clinical time-to-effect curve of an SC insulin dose is therefore the convolution of three serial processes — molecular dissociation in the depot, capillary uptake and systemic distribution, and intracellular signal transduction at the target cell — each of which has been characterised quantitatively and each of which contributes to the inter- and intra-individual variability that constrains glycemic control in T1D.

### Mechanism

#### Insulin receptor signaling

The insulin receptor (INSR) is a constitutive α₂β₂ tetrameric receptor tyrosine kinase. The *INSR* gene is alternatively spliced to yield two isoforms differing only by the inclusion (IR-B) or exclusion (IR-A) of the 12-residue exon-11 segment in the C-terminal portion of the α-subunit. IR-B predominates in liver, skeletal muscle, and adipose — the principal metabolic targets — whereas IR-A is enriched in fetal tissue, brain, and many tumours. Insulin binds both isoforms with comparable affinity (Kd ≈ 0.9–1.0 nmol/L for insulin), but IR-A binds IGF-2 (insulin-like growth factor 2, a mitogenic ligand with partial IR-A agonism) ~11-fold more tightly than IR-B (Kd ≈ 3.3 vs 36 nmol/L) and binds proinsulin ~7-fold more tightly, conferring its characteristic mitogenic-leaning signaling pattern ([Belfiore et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28973479/)).

Ligand engagement induces trans-autophosphorylation of the activation loop tyrosines (Tyr1158, Tyr1162, Tyr1163). The activated kinase recruits and tyrosine-phosphorylates IRS-1 and IRS-2 (insulin receptor substrate adaptor proteins, obligate metabolic signaling scaffolds) scaffolds; combined skeletal-muscle or hepatic *Irs1*/*Irs2* knockout phenocopies *Insr* knockout, establishing IRS proteins as the obligate metabolic signaling node ([Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). Phosphorylated IRS recruits class IA PI3K (phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase, generates PIP3 (phosphatidylinositol-3,4,5-trisphosphate, membrane lipid second messenger) for Akt activation) (p85 regulatory + p110 catalytic), generating PIP3 at the inner leaflet, which docks PDK1 and Akt (protein kinase B, the central insulin signaling kinase); PDK1 phosphorylates Akt at Thr308, mTORC2 at Ser473.

In skeletal muscle and adipose, activated Akt phosphorylates AS160/TBC1D4, relieving Rab-GAP-mediated restraint and permitting GLUT4 storage vesicle exocytosis to the sarcolemma. Combined *Tbc1d1*/*Tbc1d4* germline deletion in mice abolishes insulin-stimulated muscle glucose uptake ([Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). In hepatocytes, Akt phosphorylates FoxO1 at Thr24/Ser256/Ser319, causing nuclear exclusion and reduced activation of gluconeogenic transcription (G6PC, PCK1) — sufficient on its own, in *Foxo1*-ablated mice, to normalise fasting hepatic glucose production. The parallel mitogenic arm (Shc → Grb2/SOS → Ras → Raf → MEK → ERK) is activated at higher insulin concentrations than the metabolic arm and is of secondary relevance in T1D therapy ([Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). Tissue-level dose–response is graded. In 15 healthy participants, half-maximal suppression of glucose production occurred at 29±2 μU/mL and half-maximal stimulation of utilization at 55±7 μU/mL ([Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7018254/), abstract-supported; full-text appraisal pending). The inspected abstract does not establish a lipolysis threshold or Hill coefficient. See §25 for the distinction between measured endpoints and model parameters.

#### Subcutaneous depot kinetics and analogue chemistry

Soluble insulin in pharmaceutical formulation exists as a concentration-dependent mixture of zinc-coordinated hexamers (~36 kDa), dimers (~12 kDa), and monomers (~6 kDa). The capillary endothelium of subcutaneous tissue is permeable to monomers and dimers but excludes hexamers; absorption is therefore rate-limited by the hexamer → dimer → monomer dissociation that follows dilution of the depot in interstitial fluid. Oligomer distributions depend on formulation and concentration. The exact concentration cut-offs and oligomer-specific absorption times attributed to Søeborg et al. are not retained because the full paper was not acquired; the general association mechanism is discussed in [Gradel et al. (2018)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/).

Rapid-acting analogues are engineered to destabilise the hexamer:
- **Lispro (Humalog):** B28 Pro→Lys and B29 Lys→Pro reverse the canonical dimer-interface charge pattern.
- **Aspart (NovoRapid/NovoLog):** B28 Pro→Asp introduces a negative charge that destabilises the hexamer.
- **Glulisine (Apidra):** B3 Asn→Lys plus B29 Lys→Glu, formulated zinc-free and stable as a dimer; absolute SC bioavailability ~70%, apparent SC half-life ~42 min vs ~86 min for regular human insulin ([Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/)).
- **Faster aspart (Fiasp):** insulin aspart formulated with niacinamide (an absorption-accelerating excipient acting via local vasodilation) and L-arginine; in pooled clamp data (n=218 T1D adults, euglycemic clamp, 0.2 U/kg fasting), onset of appearance ~5 min earlier and tmax ~7 min shorter vs aspart, with early exposure (AUC₀–₃₀ min) approximately doubled ([Heise et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27878566/); [Heise et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31667789/)).

Long-acting basal analogues use the inverse strategy of stabilising or extending the depot:
- **Glargine (Lantus):** A21 Asn→Gly plus two C-terminal B-chain arginines shift the isoelectric point toward neutral pH; the formulation is acidic (pH 4) and precipitates as microcrystals on injection into neutral interstitial pH, then re-dissolves slowly. The active species in plasma is the proteolytically processed M1 metabolite (21A-Gly-human insulin). Steady-state plasma concentration is reached after ~2 days for U100 glargine and ~4 days for U300 ([Evans et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21410860/)).
- **Detemir (Levemir):** B30 Thr-deletion plus a C14 myristic-acid acylation at Lys B29; reversibly binds plasma albumin (>98% bound), buffering free-insulin fluctuations.
- **Degludec (Tresiba):** B30 Thr-deletion plus C16 hexadecandioic-acid acylation at Lys B29 via a γ-glutamic acid linker. After SC injection the molecule self-assembles into soluble multi-hexamer chains in the depot, from which monomers separate slowly and continuously; plasma half-life ~25 h (vs ~12 h for glargine U100), steady state within 3 days, and within-subject variability of the 0–24-hour glucose infusion rate area under the curve (GIR AUC, a pharmacodynamic glucose-lowering endpoint) was lower than glargine U100 at steady state (CV 20% vs 82%; daily 0.4 U/kg dosing in the reviewed T1D clamp trial) ([Haahr & Heise, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25179915/)).

Absolute subcutaneous bioavailability must be distinguished from relative exposure between two subcutaneous formulations. The approximately 70% estimate cited here pertains to glulisine; a near-unity faster-aspart/aspart exposure ratio cannot establish either product's absolute fraction absorbed. A single 70% value should not be assigned to regular insulin and every soluble analogue from these comparisons ([Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/); [Heise et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27878566/), original-study appraisal pending).

In the healthy-subject regular-human-insulin comparison summarised by Gradel and colleagues, thigh and deltoid injection reduced peak concentration by 32% and 42% relative to abdominal injection, with later peak timing but no difference in total exposure. These percentages should not be extended to all rapid-acting analogues. Heating an aspart injection site to 40 °C reduced time to peak concentration by approximately 42% in the reviewed protocol. The intramuscular comparison concerned a shorter time to 50% absorption, not a universal halving of time to peak plasma concentration ([Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/), §§4.2–4.2.1).

#### Insulin clearance

Endogenous insulin secreted into the portal circulation is removed predominantly by the liver: per-pass hepatic extraction of insulin in healthy humans is ~50%, with reported values up to ~80% in some receptor-mediated kinetic analyses ([Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/)). Hepatic clearance is receptor-mediated: insulin binds hepatocyte INSR, the complex is internalised via clathrin-coated pits coordinated by phosphorylated CEACAM1, and the internalised insulin is degraded in early endosomes by insulin-degrading enzyme (IDE), a Zn²⁺-dependent metalloprotease — though liver-specific *Ide* deletion in mice does not cause hyperinsulinemia, suggesting IDE has additional regulatory roles in receptor trafficking beyond proteolysis ([Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/)). The kidney contributes ~30–40% of total clearance via glomerular filtration with ~99% tubular reabsorption and degradation; renal clearance falls in chronic kidney disease and prolongs apparent insulin half-life. Plasma half-life of intravenous insulin is ~4–6 min in healthy adults; for SC injection the apparent half-life is dominated by the absorption rate constant (typically 30–90 min for rapid-acting analogues, several hours for basal analogues), not by elimination ([Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/)).

SC injection routes insulin into peripheral venous return, bypassing portal first-pass extraction. The resulting peripheral hyperinsulinemia (relative to portal-vein hyperinsulinemia at matched whole-body insulin action) is the structural pharmacokinetic divergence between exogenous SC insulin therapy and endogenous β-cell secretion, and underlies several characteristic features of T1D management discussed below.

#### Loss of pulsatility

Healthy β-cells co-ordinate insulin secretion in 5–8 min pulses superimposed on a slower 80–120 min ultradian rhythm. Studies of pulsatile vs constant intra-portal insulin infusion in canine and rodent models showed that the pulsatile pattern produced 50–80% higher hepatic IRS-1/IRS-2 phosphorylation, stronger Akt and FoxO1 activation, and approximately doubled hepatic glucokinase mRNA induction relative to constant infusion at matched mean concentration ([Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/)). The clinical magnitude of this effect in human T1D — where pulsatility is irretrievably lost regardless of analogue or pump strategy — has not been precisely quantified, but it is one mechanistic strand contributing to the residual hepatic insulin resistance observed even in T1D patients on optimised SC therapy.

### Quantitative characterization

| Quantity | Value | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Endogenous portal:peripheral insulin gradient (healthy, fasting) | ~2–3:1 | Reflects ~50% per-pass hepatic extraction | [Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/) |
| Hepatic first-pass insulin extraction (healthy) | ~50% (range up to ~80%) | First-pass extraction; receptor-mediated uptake + IDE-dependent degradation | [Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/) |
| Renal clearance contribution | ~30–40% of total clearance | Glomerular filtration + ~99% tubular reabsorption/degradation; falls in CKD | [Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/) |
| Plasma half-life (IV insulin, healthy adults) | ~4–6 min | Dominated by hepatic and renal clearance | [Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/) |
| INSR Kd for insulin (IR-A and IR-B) | 0.9–1.0 nmol/L | Comparable for both isoforms | [Belfiore et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28973479/) |
| Insulin EC50, hepatic EGP suppression | ~29 μU/mL (~174 pmol/L) | Half-maximal suppression of glucose production | [Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7018254/) |
| Insulin EC50, whole-body glucose disposal (Rd) | ~55 μU/mL (~330 pmol/L) | Hierarchy of insulin actions: lipolysis < EGP < Rd | [Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7018254/); [Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/) |
| β-cell secretory pulse interval (healthy) | 5–8 min | Pulsatility lost in T1D and impaired in T2D | [Pørksen, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11845219/); [Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/) |
| Absolute SC bioavailability | Approximately 70% in the cited glulisine account | Not a universal value for regular insulin/all analogues; distinguish absolute from relative bioavailability | [Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/) |
| Tmax aspart (T1D, fasted, 0.2 U/kg, abdominal SC, glucose clamp) | ~50–60 min | Reference rapid-acting analogue | [Heise et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27878566/) |
| Tmax faster aspart (Fiasp), same conditions | ~43–53 min (~7 min earlier than aspart); onset of appearance ~5 min earlier | Niacinamide-accelerated formulation; doubled AUC₀–₃₀ min | [Heise et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27878566/) |
| Tmax lispro (T1D, fasted SC) | ~30–90 min | Pediatric data showed onset ~2× faster than aspart; adult range varies with dose | [Evans et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21410860/) |
| Apparent SC half-life of glulisine | ~42 min | Vs ~86 min for regular human insulin (post-absorptive); zinc-free formulation | [Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/) |
| Glargine U100 time to plasma steady state | ~2 days | U300 ~4 days; M1 metabolite is the active species | [Evans et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21410860/) |
| Degludec plasma half-life (T1D adults 18–65) | ~25.4 h (vs ~12 h for glargine U100) | Steady state within 3 days | [Haahr & Heise, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25179915/) |
| Degludec within-subject variability in glucose-lowering effect (CV of 24-hour GIR AUC) | 20% versus 82% for glargine U100 | Pharmacodynamic endpoint, not insulin-concentration AUC; T1D, 0.4 U/kg daily, clamps on days 6, 9 and 12 at steady state | [Haahr & Heise, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25179915/) |
| Intra-individual CV% of regular SC insulin (controlled clamp, healthy) | 15–25% | Inter-individual CV ~10% higher again; analogues show similar variability under controlled conditions | [Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/) |
| Intra-individual CV% of intermediate/long-acting insulins | 12–45% (intermediate); >50% (long-acting NPH) | Variability rises with depot complexity and mixed-formulation crystals | [Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/); [Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/) |
| Regional peak concentration and timing, regular human insulin | Thigh Cmax 32% and deltoid Cmax 42% lower than abdominal; later Tmax | Healthy-subject comparison summarised in review; total exposure did not differ | [Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/) |
| Effect of 40 °C heat on Tmax (insulin aspart, T1D) | Tmax reduced ~42% | Local vasodilation; reverses with cooling | [Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/) |
| Lipohypertrophic site vs normal-tissue absorption (insulin aspart, T1D) | Cmax −25%, AUC₀–₄ₕ −22%; CV up to ~52% (AUC) and ~55% (Cmax) vs ~11–15% in normal tissue | Erratic and blunted absorption from fibrotic depot | [Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/) |
| GLUT4 surface translocation kinetics on insulin stimulation | Half-maximal in ~5–10 min, full effect in ~20–30 min | Kinetics drawn from secondary reviews; primary canonical source ([Richter & Hargreaves, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23899560/)) is paywalled — see wishlist | [Chadt & Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591906/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Inverted insulin gradient.** Endogenous β-cell insulin establishes a portal:peripheral concentration ratio of ~2–3:1 with ~50% per-pass hepatic extraction. SC-injected insulin enters peripheral venous return, bypasses hepatic first-pass extraction, and reaches the liver only after systemic dilution. To suppress hepatic glucose production by an equivalent amount, peripheral insulin concentrations in T1D must therefore exceed physiological values, producing relative peripheral hyperinsulinemia. Adipose lipolysis is suppressed at lower insulin concentrations than hepatic glucose production (EC50 ~8–11 vs ~29 μU/mL; Rizza et al., 1981), so peripheral hyperinsulinemia preferentially over-suppresses lipolysis at peak action and under-suppresses gluconeogenesis at trough — a pharmacokinetic asymmetry that contributes to fasting hyperglycemia and the difficulty of replicating physiological 24-hour glucose homeostasis with SC therapy ([Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34328272/); [Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)).

2. **Loss of pulsatile signaling.** All current SC regimens (multiple daily injections, CSII (continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion, i.e., pump therapy) basal-rate pumps) deliver continuous or near-continuous insulin appearance rather than the 5–8 min pulses of healthy β-cell secretion. In animal models, replacing pulsatile portal infusion with constant infusion at matched mean concentration reduces hepatic IRS-1/IRS-2 phosphorylation by 50–80% and roughly halves hepatic glucokinase induction ([Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/)). The quantitative contribution to T1D hepatic insulin resistance in humans has not been isolated experimentally.

3. **Inter-injection variability.** Even under controlled glucose-clamp conditions in healthy subjects, intra-individual CV (coefficient of variation, measure of intra-individual variability as %) of regular SC insulin AUC is 15–25%, and inter-individual CV is ~10% higher again ([Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/)). Long-acting NPH and intermediate-acting insulins have CV >50% — depot mixing complexity drives variability. Modern rapid-acting analogues do not substantially reduce this variability under controlled conditions, whereas a separate repeated-clamp comparison found lower within-subject variability in degludec's glucose-lowering effect than glargine U100 (CV of 24-hour GIR AUC 20% versus 82%, after daily 0.4 U/kg dosing at steady state). This pharmacodynamic comparison must not be interpreted as variability in plasma insulin AUC or attributed solely to one molecular feature ([Haahr & Heise, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25179915/)).

4. **Lipohypertrophy.** Injection into lipohypertrophic tissue can reduce insulin exposure and increase within-person variability, but the reported magnitudes come from different preparations and experiments. Gradel's review reports 25% lower peak concentration and 22% lower four-hour exposure for an aspart comparison. The coefficients of variation of 52% for insulin exposure and 55% for peak concentration, versus 11% and 15% in normal tissue, come from Famulla's separate lispro experiment in 13 people with T1D. These studies should not be combined into one universal loss factor; §22 gives the lispro endpoints and access limitation ([Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/), §4.5).

5. **Modifiers of absorption rate.** Anatomical region, local temperature, exercise, injection depth, dose and tissue condition can modify absorption. The magnitude depends on formulation and protocol. In particular, time to 50% absorption, plasma Tmax and time to maximal glucose-lowering effect are different endpoints and should not be substituted for one another ([Gradel et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30116732/)). The exercise interaction is discussed in §8.

6. **Insulin antibodies.** Anti-insulin antibodies (AIAs) are detectable in 5–30% of T1D patients on modern human insulin and analogues — substantially less than the 60–90% prevalence seen with bovine and porcine insulins of the pre-recombinant era. AIAs can buffer free insulin and produce anomalous PK profiles (delayed peak, prolonged tail), but their clinical impact in modern therapy is generally modest; specific quantitative descriptions are beyond the scope of this section and the prevalence ranges are aggregated from heterogeneous assay platforms.

NB: the simulator (a) uses a two-compartment Hovorka 2004 SC absorption cascade (S1 → S2 → plasma I) with a single absorption rate constant per insulin source rather than resolving hexamer → dimer → monomer dissociation kinetics; (b) does not differentiate insulin analogues by molecular chemistry — calibration is via lumped Tmax and bioavailability for each insulin "type" rather than via the analogue-specific dissociation constants; (c) does not model dose-volume-dependent absorption slowing for large boluses; (d) does not model hepatic first-pass extraction (since SC-injected insulin bypasses portal delivery, the simplification is appropriate for the SC-therapy context but excludes the modelling of physiological/intra-portal infusion modes); (e) does not represent insulin antibodies, lipohypertrophy, or regional injection-site variability as state variables; (f) represents insulin signaling at the kinetic-rate level (x1 transport, x2 disposal, x3 EGP suppression remote effects) rather than as explicit IR–IRS–PI3K–Akt–AS160 cascades. The intra-injection variability noted above is partially captured via a CV ≈ 25% on the absorption time constant (see §13 Variability in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md).*
