# Part 4: Physical Activity

<a name="aerobic-exercise"></a>
## 8. Aerobic exercise -- running, cycling, swimming

Aerobic exercise increases glucose uptake by contracting skeletal muscle and can enhance insulin-stimulated uptake during recovery. These are distinguishable processes with overlapping time courses, not three fixed phases shared by all protocols. Human and animal experiments reviewed by [Cartee (2015)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/) show enhanced insulin-stimulated uptake lasting into recovery, sometimes to 24–48 h; they do not establish a universal decay half-life in T1D or definitive causality for every proposed AS160-signalling step. During and after exercise, glucose concentration reflects the balance of endogenous production, peripheral disposal, ingested carbohydrate and circulating exogenous insulin ([Riddell et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28126459/)).

### Mechanism

#### Acute contraction-stimulated glucose uptake

Contraction activates glucose transport via signaling pathways distinct from -- and partially additive with -- those engaged by insulin ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)).

- **AMPK**: AMP/ATP elevation during contraction allosterically activates AMP-activated protein kinase, particularly the α2 isoform in working muscle. AMPK phosphorylates TBC1D1 (and AS160/TBC1D4), inactivating their Rab-GAP activity and permitting GLUT4 vesicle exocytosis. AMPK α2 activity reverses to baseline within ~30-60 min after exercise stops ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)).
- **Ca²⁺/CaMKII**: SR Ca²⁺ release during excitation-contraction coupling activates Ca²⁺/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II independently of AMPK; CaMKII regulates contraction- but not insulin-induced glucose uptake (rodent KO and pharmacological evidence; mechanism inferred to operate similarly in humans).
- **Rac1**: A small Rho-family GTPase activated by mechanical stretch and contraction, Rac1 drives actin cytoskeletal remodeling required for GLUT4 vesicle docking; muscle-specific Rac1 deletion in mice reduces contraction-stimulated glucose uptake by ~30-40% (rodent data; human evidence supportive but indirect).
- **GLUT4 trafficking**: At rest only ~5% of skeletal-muscle GLUT4 resides at the cell surface. Insulin alone or contraction alone each increases surface GLUT4 to ~25-50%, and combined insulin + contraction is more than additive, consistent with at least partially distinct intracellular GLUT4 storage pools ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)). The molecular identity of the contraction-recruited pool remains under investigation.
- **Substrate flux**: Whole-body glucose disposal can rise from ~2 mg/kg/min (basal post-absorptive) to >10 mg/kg/min during moderate aerobic exercise; muscle-specific extraction can rise up to 50-fold over rest (key-points statement, not a single quantified mean; [Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)).
- **Skeletal-muscle blood flow**: Increases from ~3-5 mL/min/100 g at rest to 50-80 mL/min/100 g during moderate cycling and ≥100-150 mL/min/100 g during near-maximal exercise (a 15-30-fold rise), supplying both substrate delivery and mass-transport capacity for glucose extraction.

#### Insulin pharmacokinetic modulation during exercise

Exercise can increase circulating exogenous insulin, but the response varies with formulation, injection site and depth, dose and protocol. [Pitt and colleagues' narrative review (2020)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7609903/) reports heterogeneous findings, including studies with no significant exercise-related increase. Its Table 2 combines labelled-depot disappearance rates and plasma insulin concentrations, not a pooled common endpoint. For example, one healthy-subject experiment reported thigh disappearance rates of 1.12 ± 0.12 versus 0.68 ± 0.15%/min during exercise and rest, whereas the abdominal comparison was not significant; studies of glargine generally found no significant change. These comparisons do not establish a universal 1.5–3-fold acceleration of either appearance or time to peak concentration. Capillary recruitment, local mechanical effects and temperature are proposed contributors, but their independent contributions during exercise remain incompletely resolved. In particular, the review does not establish that warming accelerates in-vivo hexamer dissociation independently of blood flow. The review full text was inspected; its underlying primary experiments were not independently reappraised here.

#### Post-exercise insulin sensitivity -- two kinetic phases

**Early phase (~1-4 h post-exercise) -- enhanced insulin-stimulated glucose uptake without proximal signaling amplification.** In a one-legged exercise paradigm with euglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamp 4 h after exercise, the previously exercised leg activated insulin-stimulated glucose uptake faster (half-time 11 vs 34 min) and 2- to 4-fold more strongly than the rested leg, despite no enhancement of insulin-receptor tyrosine kinase, IRS-1-associated PI3K activity, or proximal Akt activity ([Wojtaszewski et al., 2000](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10868952/)). Enhanced phosphorylation of AS160 (TBC1D4) at several sites is associated with the sensitised state and is a candidate contributor to greater GLUT4 trafficking at submaximal insulin concentrations; the review does not establish every causal step ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)). Glycogen depletion in the working fibers is a permissive condition: refeeding with carbohydrate to restore glycogen rapidly reverses the increment in insulin-stimulated glucose uptake (causal relationship to glycogen depletion is supportive but not conclusively established; [Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)).

**Late phase (~24-48 h post-exercise) -- the classical Mikines/PEIS phenomenon.** A single 60-min bout at ~150 W on a cycle ergometer in untrained men reduced apparent half-maximal insulin concentration (Km) for whole-body glucose uptake from 52 ± 3 μU/mL at rest to 40 ± 3 μU/mL at 48 h post-exercise, while raising V_max from 9.5 ± 0.8 to 10.7 ± 0.8 mg·min⁻¹·kg⁻¹ ([Mikines et al., 1988](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3126668/) -- abstract data; small sample, n = 7, untrained men without T1D, single intensity). The additional five-day experiment included only three men and found no persisting effect. Measurements immediately after exercise, at 48 h and in this smaller five-day sample cannot identify a general decay constant. Maximal glucose-to-glycogen conversion rose from 5.7 ± 0.6 to 7.2 ± 0.2 mg·min⁻¹·kg⁻¹ at 48 h. Other experiments reviewed by Cartee show enhanced insulin-stimulated uptake in healthy and insulin-resistant populations, with duration and magnitude dependent on exercise, feeding and measurement conditions ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)).

#### Substrate utilization and the crossover concept

Across exercise intensities the relative contributions of carbohydrate (CHO) and fatty-acid oxidation shift predictably ([Brooks & Mercier, 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7928844/) -- abstract truncated at 250 words; full text on paywalled wishlist). At low intensity (≤45% V̇O₂max (maximal oxygen uptake, gold-standard measure of aerobic capacity)) lipid oxidation predominates; at high intensity (~75% V̇O₂max) CHO oxidation predominates. The "crossover point" -- the power output at which CHO-derived fuels overtake lipids as the dominant energy source -- typically lies in the moderate-intensity range. Endurance training shifts the crossover rightward (higher absolute intensity) by enhancing mitochondrial fat-oxidation capacity and reducing sympathoadrenal responses to a given submaximal load. The crossover quantitatively depends on training status, fiber-type composition, glycogen availability, and prior nutrition; a single universal threshold percentage should be interpreted cautiously.

In T1D, the substrate-mix shift carries kinetic implications: low-intensity, long-duration aerobic work draws disproportionately on lipid oxidation and glycerol/lactate gluconeogenic precursors and exerts comparatively modest pressure on circulating glucose, whereas high-intensity continuous aerobic exercise rapidly draws muscle glycogen and plasma glucose. Counterregulatory catecholamine rise at higher intensities partially offsets this glucose flux via hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis ([Hatting et al., 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5723278/)).

### Quantitative characterization

|---|---|---|---|

| Skeletal-muscle glucose uptake fold-increase, rest -> exercise | up to ~50x | Skeletal muscle; multiple modalities | [Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162) |
| Skeletal-muscle blood flow, rest | ~3-5 mL·min⁻¹·100 g⁻¹ | Healthy adults | Standard physiology reference |
| Skeletal-muscle blood flow, moderate aerobic | ~50-80 mL·min⁻¹·100 g⁻¹ | Healthy adults | Standard physiology reference |

| Crossover threshold (CHO becomes dominant fuel) | low intensity ≤45% V̇O₂max lipid-dominated; ~75% V̇O₂max CHO-dominated; crossover commonly cited at ~45-55% V̇O₂max but heavily training-status-dependent | Healthy adults; indirect calorimetry | [Brooks & Mercier, 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7928844/) (abstract data; full text paywalled) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Acute hypoglycemia risk during the bout.** In T1D, exogenous insulin cannot be down-regulated in real time. When a bolus or basal insulin remains pharmacologically active during aerobic exercise, contraction-driven GLUT4 translocation is added on top of the insulin-stimulated GLUT4 pool, producing a near-additive (sometimes supra-additive) increase in muscle glucose extraction. This kinetic combination -- working muscle drawing on both pools simultaneously -- is the proximate cause of the rapid BG fall observed during moderate aerobic exercise on active insulin. Empirically, 45 min of treadmill running at 60% V̇O₂max produced a 3.4 mmol/L plasma BG drop in physically active T1D adults, a fall of ~4.5 mmol/L per hour ([Yardley et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/)). In a separate hyperinsulinaemic-euglycaemic clamp, the extra glucose infusion required to prevent BG from falling increased from 1.8 ± 0.4 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 35% V̇O₂peak to 4.2 ± 0.7 at 65%, then remained statistically similar at 3.5 ± 0.7 at 80% ([Shetty et al., 2021](https://academic.oup.com/jcem/article/106/1/e83/5937229)). This small study (n=9, with only two participants completing 40 min at 80%) supports an intensity plateau under high circulating insulin rather than protection from hypoglycaemia at the highest continuous aerobic workload.

2. **Circulating exogenous insulin cannot follow the normal rapid secretory adjustment.** Endogenous insulin secretion normally falls during aerobic exercise, whereas the time course of injected insulin cannot be downregulated at the same speed. Continuing insulin action can restrain endogenous glucose production while contraction increases disposal. A model fitted to labelled-glucose clamp data from six adults with T1D and six without diabetes separated immediate insulin-independent from delayed insulin-dependent effects during 60 min exercise at 65% maximal oxygen uptake under three glucose/insulin conditions. It predicted increases in insulin-independent disposal of 66–82% in T1D and 67–97% without diabetes, and insulin-dependent increases of 81–155% and 10–40%, respectively. These are model-dependent decompositions from a small protocol, not separately measured component fluxes or a population-wide T1D multiplier ([Romeres et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8321821/); indexed abstract plus online Methods inspected; independent full-text appraisal pending). Agreement with measured total disposal does not independently validate the latent component split.

3. **Catecholamine-driven counterregulation is variable and conditional.** Adrenaline and noradrenaline rise at higher exercise intensities and stimulate hepatic glycogenolysis, partially offsetting muscle glucose uptake. However, the response is blunted in (i) patients with hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure, (ii) those with depleted hepatic glycogen reserves from prolonged poor glycemic control or recent fasting, and (iii) sustained moderate-intensity work where catecholamine rise is modest. Reliance on catecholamine-driven hepatic glucose output as a safety mechanism is therefore unreliable for moderate aerobic exercise.

4. **Delayed hypoglycemia and the limits of mode comparisons.** Increased insulin sensitivity can overlap with overnight insulin exposure after afternoon or evening exercise, but neither Cartee's review nor the sparse Mikines sampling establishes a 12–24-hour T1D decay half-life. In [Yardley and colleagues' crossover study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/), 12 physically active people with T1D performed 45 min aerobic exercise, resistance exercise or rest with protocolised insulin adjustment and glucose supplementation when needed. Plasma glucose fell more during aerobic than resistance exercise (9.2 to 5.8 versus 8.4 to 6.8 mmol/L), but mean interstitial glucose was lower after resistance exercise at 4.5–6 h. Nocturnal hypoglycemic excursions numbered 9 after resistance exercise and 4 after either aerobic exercise or rest; differences were not statistically significant. These findings do not establish an overnight safety ranking. Rest sessions occurred first, only the exercise order was randomised, and some post-exercise CGM observations were missing.

5. **Kinetic asymmetry of onset and recovery.** BG falls quickly when aerobic exercise begins on top of active insulin (minutes); BG recovery after the bout is slow, because insulin pharmacokinetics continue to act on a now insulin-sensitized muscle bed and PEIS persists for hours-to-days. Practical implication: pre-exercise insulin reduction or carbohydrate intake must be planned in advance of the bout (the pharmacokinetic delay of even rapid analogs prevents a same-minute correction), and post-exercise carbohydrate / basal-insulin titration must extend through the PEIS window, not just the immediate recovery period ([Riddell et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28126459/)).

*Implementation: see [Activity -- Four Activity Types with Different Physiology in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#activity) for the simulator implementation. NB: the simulator uses the Rashid/Resalat extended Hovorka model with a discrete fast (contraction-mediated) and slow (PEIS) component; substrate switching between CHO and fat oxidation is not represented; AMPK / Ca²⁺ / Rac1 activation is collapsed into a single contraction-effect surrogate; and the SC insulin-absorption acceleration during exercise is approximated by a heart-rate-scaled rate constant rather than mechanistic blood-flow / temperature compartments.*

---

<a name="anaerobic-exercise"></a>
## 9. Anaerobic training -- resistance training and sprinting

Resistance training, sprinting and high-intensity interval training (HIIT) are often grouped as “anaerobic” exercise, but their acute glycemic responses are not interchangeable. Fasted HIIT commonly raises plasma glucose, whereas conventional resistance exercise has produced a smaller fall than aerobic exercise, no mean change under clamp conditions, or a modest rise depending on time of day and circulating insulin ([Yardley et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/); [Toghi-Eshghi & Yardley, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31211392/); [Young et al., 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10511169/); [Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)). The shared mechanism is simultaneous elevation of EGP (endogenous glucose production) and contraction-mediated glucose disposal; net BG direction depends on their balance with circulating insulin, prandial state, exercise structure and time of day.

When EGP exceeds glucose disposal, sympathoadrenally driven glycogenolysis and lactate-supported gluconeogenesis (the Cori cycle; lactate-to-hepatic-glucose recycling pathway) produce acute hyperglycemia. Resistance exercise can instead leave BG stable when the two fluxes rise by similar amounts. The post-exercise phase adds muscle glycogen resynthesis and prolonged insulin sensitization, which can shift the balance toward delayed hypoglycemia. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), the catecholamine response, lactate flux and contraction-mediated GLUT4 translocation are largely intact, but the post-exercise endogenous insulin pulse is absent and glucagon defense against later hypoglycemia is impaired ([Marliss & Vranic, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/); see [§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)). This physiology produces substantial inter- and intra-individual variation rather than a universal “strength raises BG” rule.

### Mechanism

#### Sympathoadrenal activation and the catecholamine surge

High-intensity exercise, heavy resistance training, and the high-intensity intervals of HIIT activate the sympathoadrenal axis far more rapidly and intensely than continuous aerobic work matched for total energy expenditure. In the islet-cell-clamp study of [Sigal et al. (1996)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8549858/), 14 min of cycle ergometry at 87 ± 2% V̇O₂max raised plasma norepinephrine and epinephrine by more than 12-fold above baseline (numbers cited from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). [Bally et al. (2016)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26739816/) confirmed that intermittent high-intensity exercise produces significantly higher catecholamine, growth-hormone and lactate excursions than energy-matched continuous moderate-intensity exercise in adults with T1D, while [Moser et al. (2015)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26317981/) reported peak plasma lactate of 4.93 ± 1.80 mmol/L during HIIT at the second lactate turn point in trained T1D men. Cortisol rises with a delay (~30 min onset) and contributes to the slower-developing component of post-exercise insulin resistance, while pulsatile growth-hormone secretion during and immediately after intense exercise is a third counterregulatory signal acting on hepatic glucose output and peripheral lipolysis ([Marliss & Vranic, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/); [Bally et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26739816/)).

#### Catecholamine-driven hepatic glucose production

The acute hyperglycemic response in intense exercise is driven primarily by a hepatic glucose production (Ra) that outruns peripheral glucose utilization (Rd). [Purdon et al. (1993)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8445012/) measured a 7-fold rise in Ra and 4-fold rise in Rd during exhaustive cycle ergometry (~12 min at 100% V̇O₂max) in both T1D and control subjects (numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). The same Ra/Rd ratio is reported in [Marliss & Vranic (2002)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/). Mechanistically, hepatic glycogenolysis is activated by β2-adrenergic Gαs–cAMP–PKA signaling, with parallel α1-adrenergic IP3/DAG/Ca²⁺ contributions to phosphorylase kinase activation (see [§2](#hepatic-glucose-production) for the molecular cascade). The causal role of catecholamines was directly demonstrated by [Kreisman et al. (2003)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12765943/), who showed that infusion of norepinephrine to 33.1 ± 2.9 nmol/L plus epinephrine to 7.06 ± 0.44 nmol/L during moderate exercise reproduced the high-intensity exercise Ra (12.9 ± 0.8 vs 3.8 ± 0.4 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ in control) and BG rise (numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist).

A countervailing view comes from the [Wasserman (2009)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2636990/) review of canine and human catheterization studies, which concluded that pancreatic-clamp and hepatic-denervation experiments dissociate catecholamine concentrations from hepatic glucose output during low-to-moderate-intensity work, and attribute the dominant exercise Ra signal to a small drop in insulin combined with glucagon. Wasserman explicitly notes that "attempts to directly assess a role for catecholamines in stimulating endogenous glucose production during high-intensity exercise (>80% V̇O₂max) in humans have also been negative." The reconciliation that has emerged is that at intensities ≥80% V̇O₂max, where post-exercise hyperinsulinemia is observed in healthy subjects, a non-pancreatic catecholamine-driven Ra drive becomes detectable; at lower intensities the hepatic insulin–glucagon axis dominates. The disagreement is a live issue in the field rather than a settled question, and the quantitative weight assigned to catecholamines in exercise glucose production should be regarded as intensity-dependent.

#### Lactate, the Cori cycle, and gluconeogenesis

Type II glycolytic muscle fibers, recruited preferentially during heavy resistance work and supramaximal sprints, produce lactate at rates that rapidly exceed local mitochondrial oxidation, raising plasma lactate from a resting ~1 mmol/L to peaks of 3–8 mmol/L during HIIT and resistance protocols ([Bally et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26739816/); [Moser et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26317981/)). Lactate is exported from glycolytic fibers via monocarboxylate transporter MCT4 (lactate efflux transporter in glycolytic fibers) and enters oxidative fibers, the heart and the liver via MCT1 (lactate influx transporter in oxidative tissues) ([Brooks, 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2805372/)). Hepatic uptake of lactate fuels gluconeogenesis along the lactate dehydrogenase → pyruvate → pyruvate carboxylase → PEPCK → fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase → glucose-6-phosphatase pathway (see [§2](#hepatic-glucose-production)). In direct stable-isotope tracer measurements at the lactate threshold ([Emhoff et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8846961/)), gluconeogenesis accounts for ~25% of total hepatic glucose output in trained subjects and ~40% in untrained subjects during exercise, and rises to nearly 60% of total glucose production when arterial lactate is clamped at high concentrations — the mechanistic basis for the lactate-correlated component of the BG rise reported in T1D HIIT studies ([Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)). Most of the lactate produced is oxidized rather than converted to glucose: at sustained exercise of 50–75% V̇O₂max, ~75–80% of lactate disposal is oxidative and only ~20% supplies gluconeogenesis ([Brooks, 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2805372/)). The Cori cycle therefore contributes a consistent but minority share to acute exercise hyperglycemia; the catecholamine-driven glycogenolytic flux dominates.

#### Skeletal-muscle glucose uptake during anaerobic work

Muscle contraction translocates GLUT4 to the sarcolemma via an AMPK/Rac1-dependent pathway that is independent of insulin signaling ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27739515/)). Young et al. (2023) separated glucose utilization into insulin-mediated and non-insulin-mediated components during moderate- and high-intensity resistance exercise in 25 adults with T1D. Non-insulin-mediated utilization increased during exercise and returned toward baseline approximately 30 min after exercise, whereas insulin-mediated utilization remained unchanged; EGP AUC increased by 1.04 mmol/L (95% CI 0.65–1.43) and Rd AUC increased by 1.26 mmol/L (95% CI 0.41–2.10), leaving mean BG unchanged ([Young et al., 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10511169/)). This is distinct from exhaustive or HIIT protocols, in which hepatic production can outpace the concurrent rise in disposal. Bally et al. (2016) similarly found lower exogenous glucose requirements during the final 30 min of HIIT than during continuous moderate exercise of equal mean intensity, consistent with a larger endogenous glucose contribution.

#### Post-exercise glycogen resynthesis and the delayed disposal phase

Resistance exercise depletes muscle glycogen by approximately 24–40% of resting content per session, with type II fibers showing markedly greater depletion than type I fibers (~48% near-depletion of intramyofibrillar pools in type II vs ~33% loss of intermyofibrillar pool in type I) ([Hamidvand et al., 2025](https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.14814/phy2.70683)). HIIT and prolonged sprint protocols produce comparable or greater depletion. Replenishment proceeds biphasically: while glycogen is severely depleted (<25% of resting), local control by glycogen-synthase activation and glucose transport drives resynthesis at high rates (~33 mM·h⁻¹) that are largely insulin-independent; once glycogen recovers above ~35 mM, resynthesis becomes obligatorily insulin-dependent and falls to ~3.5 mM·h⁻¹ ([Price et al., 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7909795/)) (numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). The Price measurements concern six healthy participants studied with magnetic resonance spectroscopy after severe local glycogen depletion; they are not universal rates after ordinary resistance exercise. Increased insulin-stimulated uptake during recovery can persist into the following day in some protocols (see [§8](#aerobic-exercise)), but the cited observations do not establish a fixed 45% sensitivity increase after HIIT, a common 4–12 h hypoglycemia window, or a direct mapping from glycogen depletion to a 24–48-hour risk curve. Feeding, insulin exposure, exercise history and recovery conditions remain important modifiers.

[Yardley et al. (2013)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23172972/) provided comparative T1D data: in 12 active adults undergoing 45 min of resistance training, aerobic running and rest, with rest first and the two exercise conditions in random order, plasma glucose fell from 8.4 ± 2.7 to 6.8 ± 2.3 mmol/L during resistance training (P = 0.008), compared with 9.2 ± 3.4 to 5.8 ± 2.0 mmol/L during running. Young et al. (2023) observed no mean BG change during tracer-clamp resistance exercise, while Toghi-Eshghi and Yardley (2019) observed a rise from 9.5 ± 3.0 to 10.4 ± 3.0 mmol/L during fasted morning resistance exercise but a fall from 8.2 ± 2.5 to 7.4 ± 2.6 mmol/L in the afternoon. Together, these studies define resistance exercise as context-dependent: typically less glucose-lowering than continuous aerobic exercise, but not intrinsically hyperglycemic.

| Rd AUC change during resistance exercise | +1.26 mmol/L (95% CI +0.41 to +2.10) | Non-insulin-mediated utilization rose; insulin-mediated utilization was unchanged | [Young et al., 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10511169/) |
| Plasma glucose change, ~40 min morning fasted resistance exercise | 9.5 ± 3.0 → 10.4 ± 3.0 mmol/L (Δ = +0.9) | n = 12 T1D adults; 3 sets × 8 repetitions, 7 exercises at 8-RM | [Toghi-Eshghi & Yardley, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31211392/) |
| Plasma glucose change, same protocol in afternoon | 8.2 ± 2.5 → 7.4 ± 2.6 mmol/L (Δ = −0.8) | Same crossover cohort | [Toghi-Eshghi & Yardley, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31211392/) |
| Interstitial glucose 4.5–6.0 h post-exercise (resistance vs aerobic, T1D) | Significantly lower after resistance | CGM, same study; magnitude not directly tabulated in abstract | [Yardley et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23172972/) |
| Cori-cycle / gluconeogenic share of hepatic glucose output during exercise at lactate threshold | ~25% (trained) — ~40% (untrained); rises to ~60% with arterial lactate clamp | Healthy adults, ⁶,⁶-²H₂-glucose + ³-¹³C-lactate tracer | [Emhoff et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8846961/) |
| Lactate disposal partitioning during exercise (50–75% V̇O₂max) | ~75–80% oxidation, ~20% gluconeogenesis | Tracer studies, healthy adults | [Brooks, 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2805372/) |
| Muscle glycogen depletion after a single resistance session | ~24–40% (mean Δ = −104 mmol·kg⁻¹ dry mass; 95% CI −138 to −71) | Meta-analysis of 20 studies, 180 participants | [Hamidvand et al., 2025](https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.14814/phy2.70683) |
| Fiber-type glycogen depletion after resistance training | Type II ~48% near-depleted intramyofibrillar; type I ~33% intermyofibrillar | Same meta-analysis | [Hamidvand et al., 2025](https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.14814/phy2.70683) |
| Initial muscle glycogen resynthesis rate (severely depleted, <25% of resting, insulin-independent) | ~33 mM·h⁻¹ | Healthy n = 6, ¹³C MRS gastrocnemius; from open abstract — full text on paywalled wishlist | [Price et al., 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7909795/) |
| Late muscle glycogen resynthesis rate (>35 mM, insulin-dependent) | ~3.5 mM·h⁻¹ | Same study; insulin-dependent phase | [Price et al., 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7909795/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Catecholamine-driven hyperglycemia uncovered by absent post-exercise insulin pulse.** In healthy individuals, intense exercise terminates with a transient hyperinsulinemia (~40–60 min) that suppresses Ra and accelerates glycogen replenishment ([Marliss & Vranic, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/)). In T1D this pancreatic response is absent, so Rd remains relatively elevated through recovery while Ra falls only as catecholamine levels decay; the resulting prolonged hyperglycemia is a kinetic consequence of the route-of-insulin-delivery problem (see [§2](#hepatic-glucose-production), [§7](#insulin-pharmacology)). Additional subcutaneous insulin can remain active after exercise-related glucose production has subsided and insulin sensitivity has increased. This potential temporal mismatch does not imply that every correction over-corrects, nor does it establish one peak-action time or a general dose instruction.

2. **Preserved glucagon response to exercise but lost glucagon defense against hypoglycemia.** The α-cell glucagon response to exercise is largely retained in T1D, in contrast to the rapid loss of the glucagon response to hypoglycemia within the first ~5 years of disease ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/); see [§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)). The exercise-glucagon signal contributes to the acute hepatic glucose output during the bout, but provides little defense against the post-exercise hypoglycemic phase — the most clinically relevant defect.

3. **Delayed glucose responses do not establish a universal hypoglycemia window.** [Yardley et al. (2013)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/) found lower mean interstitial glucose after resistance than aerobic exercise at 4.5–6.0 h. Nocturnal hypoglycemic excursions numbered 9 after resistance exercise and 4 after aerobic exercise or rest, but differences were not statistically significant and some CGM observations were missing. A lower mean glucose concentration at one recovery interval is not itself a measured glucose-disposal flux, an estimate of the typical onset of hypoglycemia, or proof of an overnight safety ranking.

4. **Prandial-state associations depend on the intervention.** [McClure et al. (2023)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36549943/) included 19 interventions from 15 reports. Overall glucose changed by −1.3 mmol/L (95% CI −2.3 to −0.2), with high heterogeneity (I² = 84%). Fasted interventions averaged +1.7 mmol/L (0.4–3.0), whereas postprandial interventions averaged −2.1 mmol/L (−2.8 to −1.4). This is a contrast between heterogeneous interventions, not a randomised comparison of meal state under an identical protocol; it does not independently isolate insulin, catecholamines or feeding as the causal mediator. This appraisal is limited to the indexed abstract. The within-person correlation reported across four HIIT sessions by [Riddell et al. (2019)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/) was moderate (r = 0.58, 95% CI 0.35–0.80); correlation alone does not validate an individual glucose-prediction algorithm.

5. **Elevated baseline gluconeogenesis amplifies the exercise Ra rise.** [Petersen et al. (2004)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15356077/) demonstrated that fasting hepatic glucose production is ~60% higher in moderately controlled T1D than in healthy controls and that the increase is entirely accounted for by elevated gluconeogenesis; the contribution of glycogenolysis to total Ra is reduced (rest 20% vs 32% in controls; high-intensity exercise 32% vs 57%). Because hepatic glycogen stores are smaller in T1D and gluconeogenic flux is already elevated, the exercise Ra response is shifted toward the lactate-driven gluconeogenic component and the Cori cycle assumes a quantitatively larger role than in healthy subjects.

6. **Acute and delayed concentration responses differ between exercise modes.** In Yardley's managed crossover protocol, the within-bout plasma-glucose fall was smaller during resistance than aerobic exercise (−1.6 versus −3.4 mmol/L), whereas later interstitial glucose was lower after resistance exercise. Insulin was adjusted and glucose supplied when required. Thus the comparison concerns glucose concentration under those study conditions, not an untreated physiological response, a directly measured difference in disposal, or a demonstrated failure of a prediction algorithm.

7. **Resistance exercise and HIIT require separate calibration.** Fasted HIIT produced a mean rise of +3.7 ± 1.6 mmol/L across 64 sessions ([Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)), whereas resistance protocols produced −1.6 mmol/L, no mean change, or approximately +0.9 mmol/L across the controlled studies above. Applying the HIIT response magnitude to ordinary resistance exercise therefore overstates acute hyperglycemia and obscures the roles of active insulin and time of day.

*Implementation: see [§Activity — Four Activity Types with Different Physiology in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#activity) for the simulator implementation. The simulator separates contraction-mediated uptake, exercise-specific hepatic glucose production, delayed insulin-mediated sensitivity, perfusion-dependent insulin absorption and muscle-glycogen turnover. Strength training uses a smooth fourth-order Hill latency gate with a 150-minute half-onset time for its insulin-mediated sensitivity response. This keeps the response near baseline during a 45-minute bout and early recovery, consistent with Young et al. (2023), while avoiding an unsupported biological switch at one exact minute. The half-onset time is a conservative implementation parameter rather than an identified molecular constant. The exercise-specific hepatic response is shared by all fictional characters; only the existing weight-based body scale changes. The absolute contraction flux is scaled around the 70 kg reference because the game has no independent lean-mass state. Plasma lactate and MCT1/MCT4 transport are not explicit states; their net hepatic contribution is aggregated in the exercise-specific hepatic-drive term. Prandial state and time of day affect the shared insulin and circadian physiology but are not resistance-specific parameters.*

<a name="exercise-inflammation"></a>
## 10. Exercise-induced inflammation and impaired insulin action

Exercise-associated cytokine release, muscle damage and reduced insulin action are related but distinct observations. An increase in circulating interleukin-6 (IL-6) after exercise does not by itself demonstrate pathological inflammation or increased insulin requirements. Conversely, muscle-damaging exercise can impair insulin-stimulated glucose disposal even when a systemic cytokine measurement does not establish the responsible pathway. Human evidence comes mainly from small experiments in people without diabetes and observational endurance-race cohorts; the magnitude and duration of a corresponding effect in type 1 diabetes (T1D) remain poorly quantified ([Asp et al., 1996](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/); [Philippe et al., 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4885626/); [de Sousa et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8554198/)).

### Mechanisms and what the measurements establish

**Contraction-associated cytokine release is not equivalent to muscle damage.** Contracting muscle releases IL-6 as a myokine, a muscle-derived signalling protein. Its metabolic effects depend on the accompanying physiological state; an acute exercise-associated increase should not be equated with the chronic cytokine profile of obesity or infection. In a non-randomised crossover experiment in seven healthy men, uphill walking increased IL-6 acutely, whereas non-damaging downhill walking did not. Both conditions improved the one-hour oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) value the next day. Thus, neither a detectable IL-6 increase nor muscle damage was necessary for that particular improvement ([Philippe et al., 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4885626/)).

**Damaging eccentric contractions can reduce glucose transport capacity.** Eccentric exercise loads muscle while it lengthens. Asp and colleagues studied seven participants using one-legged exercise, muscle biopsies and clamps with arterial and bilateral femoral-venous sampling two days later. GLUT4 protein content was approximately 39% lower in the exercised thigh. Maximal whole-body insulin action was reduced, but the effect depended on insulin concentration; it was not a uniform reduction at every clamp step. The local leg difference at maximal insulin was 11.9% and did not meet the conventional significance threshold (*P* = .08). The larger whole-body effect suggested an additional systemic contribution, but the experiment did not identify a specific circulating mediator ([Asp et al., 1996](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/), abstract and reported clamp results).

**Cytokine-mediated signalling provides a candidate mechanism, not a demonstrated post-marathon pathway in T1D.** In mouse and cell experiments, suppressor of cytokine signalling proteins SOCS1 and SOCS3 inhibited insulin-receptor-substrate phosphorylation and downstream metabolic responses. The work involved endotoxaemia, obesity models and experimental overexpression, rather than a T1D exercise intervention. It supports the biological possibility of cytokine-related insulin resistance but does not specify its size or time course after a given exercise session ([Ueki, Kondo and Kahn, 2004](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC419873/), abstract and experimental model description).

**Blood markers do not identify the rate-limiting physiological defect.** Creatine kinase (CK) reflects leakage of a muscle enzyme, not a measured insulin-sensitivity coefficient. Likewise, a cytokine concentration reflects production, distribution and clearance; return towards baseline is not itself an estimated plasma half-life. In 57 male amateur marathon finishers, IL-6, IL-10 and tumour necrosis factor alpha (TNF-α) increased approximately fourfold, elevenfold and 1.5-fold immediately after the race. Sampling continued at 24 and 72 hours, but available observations differed by marker, and insulin sensitivity was not measured. These results describe an inflammatory and repair response, not a conversion from cytokine concentration to insulin dose ([de Sousa et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8554198/)).

### Quantitative evidence and contrasting protocols

| Study and population | Exposure and comparison | Measured finding | Interpretive boundary |
|---|---|---|---|
| Asp et al. (1996), seven participants without T1D | One-legged eccentric exercise; exercise and control clamps in random order, two days after exercise | Maximal whole-body glucose infusion rate 81.3 ± 3.2 versus 96.4 ± 8.8 µmol·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹, a reported 15.7% reduction, *P* < .05 | Maximal, not submaximal, insulin response; the exercised-leg difference was smaller and *P* = .08. Dispersion is reproduced from the accessible abstract without assuming it is a standard deviation |
| Philippe et al. (2016), seven sedentary healthy men | One hour uphill versus downhill at equal walking speed, in fixed order; assessment after 24 h | One-hour OGTT glucose decreased 10.12 ± 3.22% after uphill and 5.03 ± 5.48% after downhill; both within-condition comparisons significant | No significant between-condition difference in the post-exercise metabolic outcomes; metabolic energy expenditure was not matched; no significant CK increase |
| de Sousa et al. (2021), 57 male amateur marathon finishers | Before, immediately after, 24 h and 72 h after a marathon | Cytokine-specific changes, with substantial immediate IL-6 and IL-10 increases | Observational race cohort; complete-case samples differed across assays; no clamp or T1D insulin-requirement endpoint |

Sources: [Asp et al. (1996)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/), [Philippe et al. (2016)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4885626/) and [de Sousa et al. (2021)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8554198/). Philippe reports means ± standard deviations. The Asp HTML contains the original abstract but not machine-readable full methods; its original PDF remains an acquisition/appraisal priority.

The apparently different results are physiologically compatible. Asp examined damaging exercise and maximal insulin responsiveness; Philippe deliberately used a non-damaging downhill protocol and measured oral glucose tolerance. The latter integrates insulin secretion, glucose appearance and disposal, whereas a clamp tests responses under imposed insulin and glucose conditions. Contraction type alone therefore cannot classify a session as insulin-sensitising or insulin-resistance-inducing.

The systematic review by [Alves et al. (2022)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8893166/) included 76 reports, of which 29 contributed to meta-analyses. IL-6 increased after half-marathons, marathons and ultramarathons, but the meta-regression did not establish a relationship between race category and effect size (*P* = .864). Standardised pre/post differences are not fold changes, and sparse follow-up plus high heterogeneity prevent extraction of a universal dose-dependent recovery curve. The review's stronger narrative claims about distance-dependent recovery should therefore not replace its more qualified quantitative result.

### Implications and unresolved questions in T1D

These experiments support the possibility that muscle damage can oppose the insulin-sensitising effects discussed in §8. They do not establish a standard sequence of increased sensitivity during the first six hours, resistance at 24–48 hours and recovery by 72 hours in T1D. Nor do they validate a fixed percentage increase in insulin requirement after unfamiliar exercise.

A useful physiological investigation would distinguish damaging from non-damaging exercise and measure the competing processes over recovery: insulin exposure, glucose appearance and disposal, muscle glycogen, soreness or damage markers, and subsequent glycaemia. Cytokine sampling alone cannot decide whether impaired peripheral disposal, increased hepatic production, altered food intake or insulin-delivery changes explain a glucose rise. The main translational gap is therefore a measured T1D time course, not an additional unverified cytokine multiplier.

<a name="relaxation-yoga"></a>
## 10b. Relaxation, yoga and mindfulness: autonomic and glycaemic outcomes

Relaxation-based practices are relevant to diabetes through psychological coping, health behaviour, physical activity and stress physiology. These routes should be distinguished because the interventions differ: yoga can involve substantial movement and instructor contact, mindfulness programmes train attention and responses to distress, and paced breathing directly manipulates respiratory timing. A glycaemic change after a combined programme cannot identify which component produced it ([Hamasaki, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37755243/); [Maurya et al., 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12168419/)).

The evidence is uneven. Reviews predominantly concern type 2 diabetes (T2D), whereas direct type 1 diabetes (T1D) evidence includes a small adolescent yoga trial with substantial attrition. Autonomic experiments in healthy adults provide evidence for short-term physiological responses, not a numerical conversion from relaxation duration to a fall in glucose.

### Mechanistic routes and measurement boundaries

**Stress-endocrine regulation.** Activation of the hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal (HPA) axis and sympathoadrenal pathways affects hepatic glucose production, substrate mobilisation and insulin action. Cortisol modifies gluconeogenic gene expression and has permissive effects on other counterregulatory hormones; this is not equivalent to an immediate fixed glucose-lowering response when perceived stress falls ([Hatting et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28868790/); see §§2 and 20).

[Pascoe et al. (2017)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28963884/) reviewed 42 randomised trials comparing yoga-containing interventions with active controls and reported changes in several stress-related physiological measures, including waking and evening cortisol. The accessible abstract does not supply a transferable percentage reduction in cortisol exposure. It also groups heterogeneous interventions and populations. It supports investigating the endocrine pathway, not assigning an eight-week programme a universal 15–25% cortisol reduction.

**Respiratory manipulation and cardiac autonomic indices.** Heart rate variability (HRV) measures variation between successive heartbeats. Its interpretation depends on respiration and the selected metric. At six breaths per minute, the respiratory oscillation is approximately 0.1 Hz, within the conventional low-frequency band rather than the 0.15–0.40 Hz high-frequency band. An increase in high-frequency power therefore cannot simply be assumed to represent the response to this breathing rate.

In a randomised-order crossover experiment, 59 healthy adults aged 18–26 completed 5-, 10-, 15- and 20-minute paced-breathing conditions and a neutral-documentary control. Root mean square of successive differences (RMSSD), a time-domain HRV index, was higher during all breathing conditions than during control; reported condition contrasts had absolute effect sizes *d* = 1.14–1.26. Longer sessions did not increase the RMSSD response. After cessation, the breathing conditions did not differ from control under the study's multiplicity-adjusted criterion, although spontaneous respiratory frequency remained lower. Diabetes was an exclusion criterion, and glucose, insulin and cortisol were not outcomes ([Laborde et al., 2021](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8656666/fullTextXML), Methods and Tables 1–3).

**Behavioural and movement components.** Improved mood, reduced distress, greater physical activity or more consistent self-management could mediate a glycaemic effect without a large direct autonomic action on glucose disposal. These remain competing explanations unless the intervention measures them and tests mediation. For example, the adolescent yoga trial combined postures, breathing, relaxation and meditation with instructor-led sessions; it did not isolate a breathing-only component or measure the proposed hormonal pathway ([Maurya et al., 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12168419/)).

### What the outcome literature shows

| Evidence | Population and comparison | Finding | Main limit on inference |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hamasaki (2023), overview of five systematic reviews | Predominantly T2D; mindfulness and related programmes with varying controls | Several reviews estimated HbA1c differences around −0.25 to −0.35 percentage points; another reported 0.05 points, 95% CI −0.22 to 0.32 | Reviews overlap in their primary trials and differ in intervention eligibility; these are not five independent replications or a T1D effect estimate |
| Innes and Selfe (2016), 25 controlled trials, 2,170 participants | Adult yoga studies: 12 randomised and 13 non-randomised trials; T1D-only studies excluded | Generally favourable glycaemic findings, but ten trials did not report between-group comparisons | This review did not itself provide a new pooled 0.5-point estimate; that figure was discussed from another meta-analysis |
| Maurya et al. (2025), 92 adolescents randomised, 46 completers | Three months of yoga plus usual care versus usual care | Among completers, HbA1c changed from 9.33% to 7.96% in yoga and from 8.06% to 9.10% in control | Major attrition, baseline imbalance, unblinded bundled intervention and inconsistent imputation reporting |
| Laborde et al. (2021), 59 healthy young adults | Four single-session breathing durations versus documentary control | Increased RMSSD during breathing, without an additional duration effect | Autonomic endpoint only; neither a T1D study nor a glycaemic intervention |

Sources: [Hamasaki (2023)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37755243/), [Innes and Selfe (2016)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4691612/), [Maurya et al. (2025)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12168419/) and [Laborde et al. (2021)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8656666/fullTextXML).

The overview illustrates why the comparator and follow-up matter more than counting favourable reviews. In its account of Ngan and colleagues' T2D synthesis, the pooled HbA1c difference was −0.35 percentage points (95% confidence interval −0.67 to −0.04) up to one month after intervention, but the benefit was not evident at three to six months. Other included reviews used overlapping trials and combined mindfulness with acceptance-based approaches or eating-awareness programmes. Pooling these review estimates again would double-count participants and obscure intervention differences ([Hamasaki, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37755243/), Results).

The T1D trial requires separate appraisal. Maurya and colleagues randomised 46 participants per group, but only 25 yoga and 21 control participants completed follow-up. Baseline HbA1c was already higher in yoga, and the completer groups differed in socioeconomic characteristics and diabetes duration. The within-yoga reduction of 1.37 percentage points is not an adjusted between-group treatment effect. The authors also report an intention-to-treat analysis with imputation, contrary to a description of the paper as completer-only; however, Methods describe 50 imputations and a 12-month follow-up, while Results describe ten imputations in a three-month trial. These discrepancies limit independent reconstruction of the analysis.

The same trial found no significant between-group difference in insulin requirement. Table 3 labels values near 52 as “units/kg/d”, an implausible unit for the reported daily requirement; those values should not be reused as physiological dosing data without clarification. Cortisol, HRV and continuous glucose outcomes were not measured, so the study cannot establish an autonomic mechanism or a direct reduction in counterregulatory hormone activity ([Maurya et al., 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12168419/), Methods, Tables 2–3 and Discussion).

### Interpretation for T1D

The strongest defensible distinction is between a measured autonomic response, an improvement in psychological or behavioural outcomes, and a sustained glycaemic benefit. Evidence for the first does not quantify the third. Neither a T2D meta-analytic effect nor reduced baseline HRV establishes an upper bound on benefit in T1D.

Repeated stress, sleep and self-management measurements alongside glycaemic outcomes would help separate the proposed routes. The studies do not establish an independent, predictable glucose-lowering response to a single relaxation session.

### References

> - Innes KE, Selfe TK. (2016). “Yoga for adults with type 2 diabetes: a systematic review of controlled trials.” *Journal of Diabetes Research*, 2016:6979370. [Full text](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4691612/).

# Part 5: Hormones and Circadian Rhythm

<a name="counterregulatory-hormones"></a>
## 11. Counterregulatory hormones — the body's defense against hypoglycemia

Glucose counterregulation comprises suppression of endogenous insulin secretion, stimulation of endogenous glucose production, limitation of insulin-mediated glucose disposal, and behavioral responses to falling glucose. Glucagon and sympathoadrenal responses have rapid metabolic effects, whereas cortisol and growth hormone contribute over longer intervals. In T1D, exogenous insulin is not subject to rapid pancreatic feedback, glucagon responses can be impaired, and antecedent hypoglycaemia can attenuate autonomic responses ([McCrimmon and Sherwin (2010)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC3279554/fullTextXML)). The resulting risk depends on insulin exposure, counterregulatory capacity and recognition of the event, not on a single universal glucose threshold.

### 11.1 The Cryer hierarchy: glycaemic thresholds and the three lines of defense

Suppression of endogenous insulin secretion ordinarily precedes the glucose-raising response to hypoglycaemia. Exogenous insulin cannot be withdrawn by this feedback mechanism, although residual endogenous secretion varies between people with T1D. Glucagon stimulates hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis; adrenaline becomes particularly important when the glucagon response is impaired. These mechanisms should be distinguished from a fixed numerical hierarchy of hormone and symptom thresholds.

Experimental thresholds depend on the population, antecedent hypoglycaemia, awareness status, glucose-sampling method and clamp protocol. In the systematic review by [Verhulst et al. (2022)](https://discovery.dundee.ac.uk/ws/files/77405021/Verhulst2022_Article_GlycaemicThresholdsForCounterr.pdf), counterregulatory hormone and symptom responses generally appeared at lower glucose concentrations in T1D than in people without diabetes. The pooled median adrenaline threshold was 3.8 mmol/L without diabetes and 3.4 mmol/L in T1D. The estimates and their interquartile ranges are summarized in §11.6.

The review found no significant separation between autonomic and neuroglycopenic symptom thresholds within either population. Symptom reporting and objectively measured cognitive impairment are distinct outcomes. A response threshold measured during a stepped hypoglycaemic clamp is not a clinical action threshold and does not establish that warning symptoms will precede impairment in an individual.

### 11.2 Glucagon and the paracrine biology of the pancreatic islet

Glucagon, a 29-amino-acid peptide secreted by α-cells of the islet of Langerhans, is the single most important acute glucose-raising hormone in non-diabetic humans. The classic dual-blockade study by [Rizza, Cryer, Haymond and Gerich (1979)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36413/) showed in healthy adults (n=12) that combined α- and β-adrenergic blockade alone did not impair glucose recovery from insulin-induced hypoglycemia, but somatostatin-induced glucagon suppression did, and combined glucagon-plus-adrenergic blockade nearly abolished recovery — establishing glucagon as the primary driver and adrenaline as the principal back-up.

The α-cell is not a freestanding glucose sensor. It sits embedded in the islet, where it is exposed to a locally extreme paracrine and juxtacrine environment from neighbouring β- and δ-cells. β-cells comprise ~60–80% of islet endocrine cells and, in human islets, are *intermingled* with α- and δ-cells rather than confined to a strict core as in rodents ([Brissova et al., 2005](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2493924/) — autopsy study, n=14 non-diabetic donors of mixed European ancestry, laser-scanning confocal microscopy; results may not generalise to all populations). This intermingled architecture amplifies paracrine cross-talk:

- **Insulin** is released from β-cells at concentrations 50–100× higher in islet capillary blood than in the systemic circulation ([Unger & Orci, 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2941311/)). At these concentrations insulin binds α-cell insulin receptors and tonically suppresses glucagon secretion.
- **GABA** (γ-aminobutyric acid) is co-stored in β-cell secretory granules and activates GABA<sub>A</sub> receptors on α-cells, hyperpolarising them and inhibiting exocytosis ([Gromada et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17213323/)).
- **Zinc** ions co-released with insulin from β-cell granules close α-cell K<sub>ATP</sub> (ATP-sensitive potassium channels governing α-cell membrane potential and exocytosis)-independent channels and contribute to glucagon suppression at high β-cell activity. (The relative contributions of insulin, GABA and zinc remain debated; Unger & Orci 2010 emphasise insulin, while Gromada 2007 weights GABA and zinc more heavily.)
- **Somatostatin** from δ-cells acts as a tonic brake on both α- and β-cells, providing fine local damping.

Unger and Orci (2010) framed this as a "Sherringtonian" (mutual inhibition between adjacent cell populations, by analogy with Sherrington's spinal motor inhibition) reciprocal arrangement: high β-cell activity actively *inhibits* α-cells, and falling β-cell activity removes that inhibition — the so-called **"switch-off hypothesis"**. In healthy humans, falling glucose causes β-cell insulin (and GABA, zinc) output to drop; the resulting *rate of decrease* in local insulin paracrine tone is itself a primary stimulus that releases α-cells to secrete glucagon. Direct α-cell glucose sensing (via K<sub>ATP</sub> channels and metabolism) contributes but is now thought to be secondary to this paracrine switch-off signal ([Gromada et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17213323/)).

### 11.3 Why the glucagon defense fails in T1D

In T1D, β-cell mass is reduced by 70–95% at diagnosis and approaches zero in C-peptide-negative long-standing disease. α-cells, in contrast, *survive* the autoimmune attack ([Brissova 2005](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2493924/)) and even hypertrophy in some studies. They retain the molecular machinery to produce and release glucagon, and they continue to respond to non-glucose stimuli — most importantly to amino acids (arginine, alanine), as Gerich demonstrated in his landmark 1973 *Science* paper ([Gerich et al., 1973](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4581053/)). What they lose is the ability to mount a glucose-specific glucagon response to falling plasma glucose.

The mechanism is most parsimoniously explained by loss of paracrine input. Without juxtaposed functioning β-cells, α-cells lack (i) the tonic insulin/GABA/zinc suppression that "primes" the switch-off response, and (ii) the falling local insulin gradient that physiologically signals hypoglycemia from within the islet.

The glucagon response to hypoglycaemia often becomes impaired in T1D, while responses to other stimuli may persist. The pace and extent of impairment vary with disease state and residual secretion. [Verhulst et al. (2022)](https://discovery.dundee.ac.uk/ws/files/77405021/Verhulst2022_Article_GlycaemicThresholdsForCounterr.pdf) could not reliably estimate a pooled T1D glucagon threshold because responses were frequently absent or insufficiently reported; its threshold analysis therefore does not establish a universal five-year cutoff. Postprandial glucagon secretion and the glucagon response to falling glucose are different physiological outcomes and should not be described as one uniformly absent hormone response.

### 11.4 Adrenaline, autonomic symptoms, and hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF)

When the glucagon response is impaired, adrenaline provides an important remaining hormonal contribution to recovery. It stimulates hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, promotes lipolysis and limits peripheral glucose uptake. Autonomic activation also generates warning symptoms, including sweating, tremor and palpitations, but their presence and glucose threshold vary.

**Hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF)** links antecedent hypoglycaemia to reduced counterregulation and impaired awareness. Response amplitude and the glucose threshold for initiating a response are separate properties: attenuation may involve either or both. The pooled median adrenaline threshold was lower in T1D than in participants without diabetes (3.4 versus 3.8 mmol/L), and impaired awareness was associated with responses at still lower glucose levels ([Verhulst et al. (2022)](https://discovery.dundee.ac.uk/ws/files/77405021/Verhulst2022_Article_GlycaemicThresholdsForCounterr.pdf)).

Prior hypoglycaemia can therefore increase susceptibility to subsequent episodes. Central adaptation is implicated, but the specific contributions of altered glucose sensing, neurotransmission and fuel availability remain incompletely resolved; much mechanistic evidence comes from animal preparations rather than direct experiments in T1D. Avoidance of recurrent hypoglycaemia can improve awareness and counterregulation in selected patients, but neither a universal recovery interval nor a fixed glucose target follows from these mechanistic observations ([McCrimmon and Sherwin (2010)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC3279554/fullTextXML); [Cryer, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23883381/)).

### 11.5 Cortisol and growth hormone — the slow secondary defenses

Cortisol and GH thresholds should not be inferred from the speed of their downstream actions. [Verhulst et al. (2022)](https://discovery.dundee.ac.uk/ws/files/77405021/Verhulst2022_Article_GlycaemicThresholdsForCounterr.pdf) reported median thresholds of 3.5 and 3.8 mmol/L, respectively, without diabetes, and 2.8 and 3.2 mmol/L in T1D. Their slower metabolic actions distinguish them from the rapid glucagon and sympathoadrenal contributions; a lower numerical threshold in one population does not establish delayed secretion in every individual.

Cortisol acts through glucocorticoid-receptor-mediated changes in gene expression and other actions that modify gluconeogenesis and insulin action. GH can increase lipolysis and impair insulin-mediated glucose utilization. Their effects depend on exposure duration and metabolic context, rather than on the plasma hormone threshold alone. Exact peak times, plasma half-lives and effect delays require independently matched kinetic sources and are not combined into a universal counterregulatory timeline here.

### 11.6 Quantitative summary of counterregulatory hormone parameters

The following are pooled **median thresholds (interquartile ranges)** from stepped hypoglycaemic clamps in [Verhulst et al. (2022)](https://discovery.dundee.ac.uk/ws/files/77405021/Verhulst2022_Article_GlycaemicThresholdsForCounterr.pdf). They are not individual biological switches, clinical treatment cutoffs, or effect-site time constants.

| Response | Without diabetes, mmol/L | T1D, mmol/L |
|---|---:|---:|
| Glucagon | 3.8 (3.0–3.8) | Not reliably estimable |
| Adrenaline | 3.8 (3.2–4.2) | 3.4 (2.8–3.9) |
| Noradrenaline | 3.2 (3.2–3.7) | 3.0 (2.8–3.1) |
| Cortisol | 3.5 (3.2–4.2) | 2.8 (2.8–3.4) |
| Growth hormone | 3.8 (3.3–3.8) | 3.2 (3.0–3.3) |
| Autonomic symptoms | 3.4 (3.4–3.4) | 3.0 (2.8–3.4) |
| Neuroglycopenic symptoms | 3.4 (2.8–not estimable) | 3.0 (3.0–3.1) |

A non-estimable interval boundary reflects limitations of the available stepped-clamp data, not an absence of physiological variability. Neuroglycopenic symptom scores should not be equated with an objective cognitive-function endpoint.

### 11.7 Integration and clinical implications

Exogenous insulin exposure, impaired glucagon responses and HAAF can jointly limit recovery from hypoglycaemia. Their relative importance varies; residual endogenous secretion and awareness are not uniformly absent in all T1D. Cortisol and GH cannot be assumed to compensate for the loss of rapid responses on the same timescale.

These observations support prevention of recurrent hypoglycaemia and preservation of effective recognition and response. They do not specify a universal glucose floor, duration of treatment or emergency dosing regimen. Exogenous glucagon acts on hepatic glucose production, but its effect also depends on substrate availability and the prevailing metabolic state. Clinical management requires population-appropriate guidance rather than a dose inferred from the threshold table.

### 11.8 Controversies and uncertainties

Several tensions in the field remain unresolved and should be flagged when this physiology is invoked quantitatively. The **relative contribution of insulin vs GABA vs zinc to α-cell paracrine suppression** is contested: Unger & Orci (2010) emphasise insulin; Gromada et al. (2007) and Rorsman's group emphasise GABA and zinc; recent optogenetic work in mouse islets supports a multi-factor model. The "switch-off" mechanism is therefore better viewed as a *system property* of intact β-cell juxtaposition than as a single signal. The balance between **direct α-cell glucose sensing and paracrine sensing** is unsettled — α-cells express K<sub>ATP</sub> channels and metabolise glucose, suggesting some intrinsic capacity, but their behaviour in dispersed culture differs sharply from in-islet behaviour, and whether intact T1D α-cells retain any preserved direct glucose sensing in vivo is unknown. The **mechanism of HAAF** remains hypothetical: cortisol-mediated central adaptation, opioid signalling, increased brain glucose transport and altered hypothalamic glucose sensing have all been proposed but none is definitively established. **Threshold variability** is large: pooled thresholds in [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/) carry inter-quartile ranges of ~0.6–1.0 mmol/L and describe population behaviour, not individual responses; individual thresholds shift with antecedent glycaemia, antecedent exercise, sleep, sex (women have somewhat blunted responses), age (children retain partially preserved glucagon longer), and pregnancy. Finally, **most threshold data come from White European or North American adult cohorts**; pediatric, pregnant, elderly, and non-European populations are under-studied, and quantitative thresholds should not be assumed identical.
