<a name="dawn-phenomenon"></a>
## 12. The dawn phenomenon — pre-awakening glucose rise

The dawn phenomenon is a circadian rise in fasting plasma glucose and in the insulin requirement to maintain euglycaemia, occurring during the late nocturnal and pre-awakening hours (approximately 03:00–08:00) in the absence of intervening hypoglycaemia, food intake, or waning exogenous insulin. It was first described in T1D by Schmidt and colleagues, who reported a reproducible night-to-morning blood glucose elevation that was not preceded by a nocturnal nadir below 3.5 mmol/L and therefore could not be attributed to counter-regulatory rebound (Somogyi effect; see §13) ([Schmidt et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7318489/)). Bolli and Gerich subsequently demonstrated that an analogous, but quantitatively much smaller, dawn rise in endogenous glucose production (EGP) and insulin secretion occurs in healthy non-diabetic adults — establishing the phenomenon as a normal physiological adaptation to the awakening state, rather than a diabetes-specific abnormality ([Bolli et al., 1984 — *Diabetes* 33:1150–1153, n = 8 healthy volunteers, half-hourly sampling 01:00–09:00](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)). The clinical relevance for T1D arises because endogenous beta-cell compensation is absent: the pre-dawn rise in hepatic insulin requirement is unmet, and fasting morning hyperglycaemia, post-breakfast excursions, and the empirical need for a higher breakfast insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio follow directly. Recent CGM analyses suggest that approximately 50% of well-treated T1D pump users show a clear dawn phenomenon when defined by a rise in basal insulin requirement of more than 20% sustained for at least 90 minutes ([Carlson et al., 2019 — IQR 28.8–110.6%, n = 33 T1D pump users](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31630987/)), and the original cohort study by Perriello and colleagues found the phenomenon present in 89% of T1D subjects (101/114) when assessed by overnight Biostator-controlled euglycaemic insulin titration ([Perriello et al., 1991 — *Diabetologia*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)).

### Hormonal drivers — cortisol, growth hormone, and the awakening pulse

Two hormone systems with distinct circadian profiles converge in the pre-awakening hours and together produce the dawn signal.

**Cortisol** follows a robust ACTH-driven circadian rhythm anchored to the suprachiasmatic nucleus. Plasma cortisol reaches its nocturnal nadir near sleep onset (around 23:00–01:00, ~50–80 nmol/L) and rises through the second half of the night to a peak between approximately 06:00 and 08:00 (~400–550 nmol/L in healthy adults), with the steepest rate of rise in the final two hours before awakening; the cortisol awakening response adds a further short-lived 38–75% increment in the first 30–45 minutes after waking ([Pruessner et al., 1997 — cited in §11; healthy cohorts](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9415946/)). Cortisol acts genomically through the glucocorticoid receptor on glucocorticoid-response elements in *PCK1* and *G6PC*, upregulating phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase and glucose-6-phosphatase and increasing hepatic gluconeogenic capacity over a 30–120 min window (see §2). It also induces post-receptor peripheral insulin resistance and is permissive for catecholamine and glucagon action. Bolli's original work in healthy controls showed that plasma cortisol, epinephrine, and norepinephrine all rose significantly from their nocturnal nadirs between 04:00 and 06:30, preceding by roughly 30–60 minutes the measurable rise in glucose production ([Bolli et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)) — temporally consistent with cortisol-mediated transcriptional priming of hepatic gluconeogenesis.

**Growth hormone** is secreted in pulses tied to slow-wave sleep (SWS), with the largest pulses concentrated in the first half of the night (typically 23:00–03:00) but with smaller, late-night pulses persisting into the early-morning REM-dominated period in many individuals. GH peaks reach 5–30 ng/mL during pulses (versus inter-pulse troughs near 0.1–0.5 ng/mL); the integrated 24-hour GH output is markedly higher in adolescents than in adults, and is amplified by puberty. GH lowers insulin sensitivity in liver and skeletal muscle through post-receptor IRS-1 serine phosphorylation, increased FFA flux from lipolysis, and induction of hepatic IGF-binding protein responses; the insulin-antagonistic effect lags the GH pulse by 3–5 hours, which places its peak metabolic action squarely in the 04:00–08:00 window even when the inciting pulse occurred earlier in the night ([Møller et al., 1990 — somatostatin GH-suppression abolishes the dawn rise in T1D](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2406181/)). Two converging T1D experiments are mechanistically decisive. Perriello and colleagues showed that suppressing nocturnal GH pulses with somatostatin abolished the pre-dawn rise in insulin requirement in T1D adults, while GH replacement during somatostatin infusion restored it ([Perriello et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2406181/)). Edge and colleagues demonstrated in pubertal T1D adolescents that the magnitude of the dawn rise in pre-breakfast insulin demand correlated with mean overnight GH concentration, and that pharmacological GH suppression reduced the pre-breakfast insulin requirement ([Edge et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2096008/)). Cortisol and GH thus contribute through distinct, non-redundant pathways — cortisol via gluconeogenic transcription and permissive action, GH via delayed peripheral and hepatic insulin resistance — and explain why the dawn phenomenon is most pronounced during adolescence, when GH output is highest, and is reduced (but not abolished) in older T1D patients ([Perriello et al., 1991 — inverse correlation with diabetes duration, r = −0.72](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)).

### Hepatic insulin resistance and the rise in EGP

The mechanistic core of the dawn phenomenon is a transient increase in hepatic glucose output driven by reduced hepatic insulin sensitivity. In healthy controls, EGP rises by approximately 30–50% above its pre-dawn nadir between 05:00 and 08:00, and is matched by a parallel rise in endogenous insulin secretion (C-peptide rises significantly after 05:30) so that fasting glucose increases only modestly (typically ≤0.5–1.0 mmol/L) ([Bolli et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)). In T1D, the hepatic insulin demand rises by a similar 30–60% — Perriello reported a mean ~33% rise in intravenous insulin infusion required to maintain euglycaemia, from ~0.102 mU·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 02:40 to ~0.135 mU·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 06:40 ([Perriello et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)) — but, in the absence of adaptive endogenous insulin secretion, the unmet demand translates directly into fasting hyperglycaemia. Both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis contribute: cortisol-driven transcriptional upregulation of *PCK1* and *G6PC* primarily augments gluconeogenesis, while GH-driven lipolysis raises FFA flux and substrate-couples gluconeogenesis through increased acetyl-CoA. The net hepatic effect is consistent across studies regardless of insulin-treatment modality, but the *manifest* glucose rise depends critically on the overnight insulin-delivery profile (see below).

### Peripheral insulin resistance

Changes in peripheral insulin action can extend into the post-breakfast period, while ongoing hepatic glucose production also affects the meal excursion. The relative contributions and magnitudes vary between protocols and individuals ([Carroll and Schade, 2005](https://www.endocrinepractice.org/article/S1530-891X(20)39957-2/fulltext); [Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/)). Insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio terminology must state its units: more insulin per gram means a higher ratio in units/g but a lower ratio in g/unit. A breakfast insulin requirement cannot be inferred solely from fasting glucose or a general morning sensitivity percentage.

### Magnitude — healthy versus T1D, and the "extended dawn" / "dawn-of-the-dawn" phenomenon

In people without diabetes, a compensatory change in endogenous insulin secretion generally limits an early-morning glucose rise. In T1D, the observed glucose trajectory depends on both changing insulin action and the overnight delivery profile. [Porcellati et al. (2013)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/) describe a 15–25 mg/dL rise when waning insulin is prevented, but their Figure 1 is an illustration involving three groups of six participants, not a pooled estimate: the depicted rise is 55 mg/dL with NPH, 17 mg/dL with fixed-rate CSII, and absent in the glargine example, which is explicitly identified as unpublished observation. T2D findings in the same perspective concern a different population and should not define a T1D magnitude.

A separate phenomenon must be distinguished. The classical dawn phenomenon refers to the pre-breakfast rise in hepatic insulin demand driven by endogenous hormonal mechanisms and is anchored to the awakening transition. Carroll and Schade introduced the term **"extended dawn phenomenon"** (sometimes informally "dawn-of-the-dawn") to describe a *post-breakfast prolongation* of hyperglycaemia in which the morning meal's glycaemic excursion is exaggerated and prolonged by the residual hormonal milieu ([Carroll & Schade, 2005](https://www.endocrinepractice.org/article/S1530-891X(20)39957-2/fulltext)). The mechanism is the persistence of GH-driven peripheral insulin resistance, ongoing FFA flux, and slowed hepatic glucose disposal during the first 1–3 hours after breakfast, producing a higher and later post-breakfast peak than is seen after lunch or dinner with comparable carbohydrate loads. Quantitatively, post-breakfast peak glucose has been reported at 191–208 mg/dL (10.6–11.6 mmol/L) versus lower peaks after lunch and dinner in T2D cohorts on identical meals ([Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/)). The extended dawn phenomenon explains why a pre-breakfast bolus correction alone is often insufficient: even after fasting glucose is normalised, the breakfast bolus dose and timing must independently account for reduced post-breakfast insulin sensitivity. Caveat: some authors use "dawn phenomenon" inclusively to encompass both the pre- and post-breakfast components, and the literature is not uniform on terminology — readers should attend to whether a quoted magnitude is fasting-only or includes the post-breakfast tail.

### Clinical implications

A dawn-related rise is not a behavioral failure. It can reflect a mismatch between the time course of insulin availability and changing metabolic demand, but it does not identify a universal basal-rate increase or breakfast bolus adjustment. Basal pharmacokinetics and the post-breakfast response need separate evaluation. The physiological distinction is between insulin delivery, hepatic glucose production and peripheral insulin action; individualized treatment decisions require clinical assessment ([Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/)).

CGM has transformed the differential diagnosis of morning hyperglycaemia. A gradual, monotonic rise from a stable nocturnal plateau beginning at ~03:00 is characteristic of dawn phenomenon; a fall to <3.5 mmol/L overnight followed by a rebound to hyperglycaemia is the Somogyi pattern (see §13); and a flat or rising trace from bedtime onward without a clear pre-dawn inflection points to insufficient basal insulin rather than dawn-driven hyperglycaemia. CGM-based prevalence estimates in T1D range from approximately 50% (well-treated pump cohorts, strict thresholds) to approaching 90% (overnight Biostator titration, looser thresholds) — the discrepancy reflecting threshold and methodology rather than true biological variability ([Carlson et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31630987/); [Perriello et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)).

### Controversies and limitations

Three issues remain unsettled. (i) The relative contributions of cortisol and GH to the manifest T1D dawn signal are debated. Selective somatostatin GH-suppression studies make GH the dominant pulsatile driver in adolescent and adult T1D, but cortisol's contribution is harder to isolate experimentally because adrenocortical suppression with metyrapone introduces confounding compensatory ACTH responses; the consensus position is that GH provides the proximate insulin-resistance trigger while cortisol provides permissive transcriptional priming. (ii) Diurnal duration. Whether a true "dusk phenomenon" — a smaller pre-dinner rise in hepatic insulin demand — exists as a separate physiological entity is contested; the §14 evidence on diurnal ISF variation suggests it is more likely a continuum of circadian insulin-sensitivity variation than a discrete dawn-mirroring event. (iii) Reproducibility. Within-person night-to-night variability complicates interpretation of a single glucose trajectory. Sleep, prior activity, meal composition and insulin delivery can all affect the observed pattern. The available appraisal does not establish a universal 40–60% coefficient of variation or a fixed number of nights sufficient for a stable estimate.

> **Sources:**

> - Bolli GB, De Feo P, De Cosmo S, et al. (1984). "Demonstration of a dawn phenomenon in normal human volunteers." *Diabetes*, 33(12):1150–1153. [PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)

<a name="somogyi-effect"></a>
## 13. The Somogyi effect — rebound hyperglycemia after nocturnal hypoglycemia

The Somogyi hypothesis proposes that nocturnal hypoglycaemia can produce subsequent hyperglycaemia through counterregulatory responses. A low-to-high glucose sequence and an endogenous hormonal cause are separate claims. Modern monitoring can document the sequence, but morning hyperglycaemia alone identifies neither a preceding low nor its mechanism. Insulin delivery, carbohydrate treatment, late meals, activity and sensor artifacts must also be considered.

Historical experiments questioned a routine causal link between nocturnal hypoglycaemia and subsequent hyperglycaemia. More recent observational evidence establishes that low-to-high nocturnal sequences can occur, while leaving their causes unresolved. The distinction matters because a physiological explanation cannot be inferred solely from the shape of a sensor trace.

### 13.1 Classical mechanism: counterregulation as a glucose-raising engine

The proposed sequence is compatible with glucose-raising counterregulatory actions described in [§11](#counterregulatory-hormones): falling glucose can elicit sympathoadrenal responses, modify endogenous glucose production and alter peripheral glucose utilization. In T1D, impaired glucagon responses and persistent exogenous insulin exposure modify this response. Hormone thresholds, response amplitudes and duration vary; the mechanism does not imply an inevitable overshoot.

A hormonal explanation of morning hyperglycaemia requires more than temporal ordering. It must account for insulin exposure throughout the night, endogenous glucose flux and any carbohydrate used to treat the low. A recurring pattern also requires reproducibility across nights rather than a single episode. These conditions distinguish a causal hormonal rebound from the broader descriptive category of post-hypoglycaemic nocturnal hyperglycaemia.

### 13.2 Historical evidence: clinical observation, not controlled trials

Somogyi's 1938–1959 case series were uncontrolled, non-blinded, single-center clinical observations on a small number of patients receiving regular soluble insulin (animal-derived; human recombinant insulin did not arrive until the early 1980s). His glucose measurements were intermittent capillary samples; no overnight glucose tracing existed. The dose reductions that "improved" fasting glucose in his patients were applied without controls for regression to the mean, observer bias, or the confounding effect of better daytime glycemic awareness ([González-Vidal et al., 2024](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39718705/)). The hypothesis spread rapidly through clinical teaching despite this thin evidentiary base, in part because the underlying counterregulatory physiology (independently established by Cryer and colleagues from the late 1970s onward) made the mechanism sound mechanistically inevitable.

The first quantitative challenge came from [Gale, Kurtz, and Tattersall (1980)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6105438/), whose *Lancet* study sampled overnight blood glucose, free insulin, GH, cortisol, and glucagon in 17 episodes of asymptomatic nocturnal hypoglycemia in patients with T1D. They found that fasting glucose at 07:00 ranged from 0.7 to 17 mmol/L after these episodes, and — critically — that the patients with apparent rebound hyperglycemia did **not** have higher counterregulatory hormone concentrations than those whose glucose remained low. The dominant correlate of morning glucose was the late-night free insulin level: hyperglycemia appeared when overnight insulin was waning, not when counterregulation was peaking. Their interpretation reframed the morning rise as relative late-night insulin deficiency, with counterregulation playing at most a permissive role.

### 13.3 The Hirsch and Perriello experiments: controlled tests

Controlled experiments have tested whether induced nocturnal hypoglycaemia increases subsequent glucose under specific insulin regimens. [Hirsch et al. (1990)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2190769/) studied 10 adults with IDDM in three crossover conditions on otherwise identical regimens: control nights, prevented nocturnal hypoglycemia (intravenous dextrose clamp keeping plasma glucose >5.6 mmol/L), and induced nocturnal hypoglycemia (stepped intravenous insulin lowering plasma glucose to <2.8 mmol/L for ~1 hour). Mean fasting and breakfast plasma glucose after induced hypoglycemia (1.9 ± 0.2 mmol/L nadir) were statistically indistinguishable from the prevention arm. Daytime glucose excursions did not correlate with nocturnal counterregulatory hormone peaks. The investigators concluded that "nocturnal hypoglycemia does not appear to cause clinically important daytime hyperglycemia" in typical IDDM. Perriello and colleagues separately studied a GH-associated dawn phenomenon in the **absence of insulin waning**. This is a mechanism that does not require preceding hypoglycaemia, rather than evidence that GH acts only when insulin wanes ([Perriello et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2178966/)).

### 13.4 The CGM era: trajectories and denominators

Earlier reports examined the relationship between nocturnal lows and fasting glucose and challenged the assumption that a high morning value usually signals a preceding hypoglycaemic episode ([Høi-Hansen et al., 2005](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00125-005-1946-5); [Choudhary et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23672623/)). Their fasting-glucose comparisons should not be equated with every subsequent definition of a low-to-high sensor sequence.

[González-Vidal et al. (2025)](https://d-nb.info/1373067012/34) retrospectively examined 14-day sensor records from 755 adults with T1D at one Spanish centre. At least one nocturnal value below 70 mg/dL followed by a value above 180 mg/dL before 06:00 occurred in 248 participants (32.8%). The sequence occurred on 405 of 10,268 observed nights (3.9%), or 20.2% of the 2,009 nights containing hypoglycaemia. These denominators answer different questions. Of the 405 low-to-high nights, 326 still had hyperglycaemia at 06:00.

The study required at least 70% sensor use but no minimum duration for the nocturnal low. It used a fixed 00:00–06:00 window rather than individual sleep times, and did not include closed-loop insulin delivery. Carbohydrate treatment, activity and hormonal responses were not measured sufficiently to distinguish mechanisms. The findings establish that post-hypoglycaemic nocturnal hyperglycaemia can occur; they do not establish endogenous hormonal overshoot as its cause. A universal estimate that fewer than 5–10% of nocturnal lows produce this pattern is not supported by the current appraisal.

### 13.5 Distinguishing Somogyi, dawn phenomenon, and waning basal

Continuous monitoring can describe overnight patterns, but their mechanisms are not uniquely identifiable from glucose alone.

| Observed pattern | Possible contributors | Information needed to distinguish them |
|---|---|---|
| Early-morning rise without a documented low | Dawn-related change in insulin action, waning delivery, late nutrient absorption | Insulin delivery and exposure, meals, repeated timing |
| Rise over a longer overnight interval | Insufficient insulin availability, ongoing nutrient absorption, illness or other context | Delivery record, intake and clinical context |
| Low followed by a high value | Counterregulation, carbohydrate treatment, altered insulin delivery, sensor artifact | Confirmation of the low, treatment record and accompanying exposures |

These are competing or interacting explanations, not automatic dose-adjustment categories. Morning hyperglycaemia alone does not justify increasing or decreasing insulin, and a low-to-high sequence alone does not prove a hormonal rebound.

### 13.6 Controversies and current consensus

Counterregulatory mechanisms are real, but their existence does not establish how frequently they cause an untreated overshoot. Conversely, observing post-hypoglycaemic hyperglycaemia does not validate every element of the historical Somogyi narrative. Differences in patient population, insulin regimen, sensor use, episode duration and whether the outcome is fasting glucose or any later high value can change reported frequencies.

The principal unresolved question is causal attribution: how much of the observed rise reflects endogenous glucose production, carbohydrate treatment, changes in insulin availability or measurement error? The 2025 cohort could not separate these explanations. Direct measurement or adequately recorded interventions would be required to do so. Hypotheses about children, residual endogenous secretion or awareness status require population-specific evidence rather than extrapolation from adult observational patterns.

Accordingly, the physiological synthesis is conditional: nocturnal hypoglycaemia can be followed by hyperglycaemia, but the trace itself is insufficient to identify a hormonal Somogyi mechanism or prescribe a treatment change.

<a name="diurnal-variation-isf"></a>
## 14. Diurnal variation in insulin sensitivity

Time-of-day variation in postprandial glycaemia can involve insulin action, endogenous insulin secretion and hepatic glucose production. These contributions overlap with the dawn phenomenon (§12), but a breakfast-to-dinner comparison does not isolate any one of them. In 14 healthy adults, aligned and 12-hour-inverted behavioural schedules separated circadian phase from behavioural timing: biological-evening two-hour glucose area under the curve was 12% higher overall than in the biological morning, accompanied by lower early-phase insulin secretion ([Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/)). This was not a comparison at matched insulin secretion. In 19 adults with C-peptide-negative T1D receiving their individualised insulin regimens, a mixed-meal study found no statistically significant population-wide diurnal insulin-sensitivity pattern, although some glucose and hepatic endpoints differed by meal ([Hinshaw et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3712033/)). Healthy-adult glucose-tolerance rhythms therefore cannot directly specify either a universal T1D insulin-sensitivity rhythm or a glucose increment per gram of carbohydrate.

### Mechanism

#### Circadian organisation: SCN, peripheral clocks, and the BMAL1/CLOCK transcription–translation feedback loop

The mammalian circadian system is a hierarchy of self-sustained molecular oscillators. The master pacemaker resides in the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) of the anterior hypothalamus, where ~20,000 neurons entrain to environmental light via the retinohypothalamic tract and impose a coherent ~24-h phase on the rest of the organism through autonomic outflow, body-temperature rhythms, and the cortisol and melatonin axes ([Dyar et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24567902/)). Virtually every peripheral tissue — liver, skeletal muscle, adipose, pancreatic islet, gut — contains its own cell-autonomous clock based on the canonical BMAL1/CLOCK (core circadian transcription factor heterodimer driving 24-h gene expression oscillations) transcription–translation feedback loop: BMAL1::CLOCK heterodimers drive transcription of *Per1/2/3*, *Cry1/2*, and *Rev-erbα/β*, whose protein products feed back to repress BMAL1::CLOCK activity, generating ~24-h oscillations in thousands of downstream "clock-controlled genes". In liver and skeletal muscle, 5–20% of the transcriptome is rhythmic, including transporters (GLUT4/SLC2A4), glycogen-handling enzymes (glycogen synthase, glycogen phosphorylase), gluconeogenic enzymes (PEPCK, G6Pase), mitochondrial biogenesis regulators, and components of insulin signalling itself ([Harfmann et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25512305/); [Harfmann et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486508/)).

Peripheral clocks are entrained by the SCN through neural and hormonal cues but are also strongly responsive to feeding. In rodent restricted-feeding paradigms the liver clock can uncouple from the SCN within days, demonstrating that nutrient timing is itself a *zeitgeber* (environmental time-giver that entrains biological clocks) for peripheral oscillators independent of the central pacemaker ([Reutrakul & Van Cauter, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24628249/)). This dual entrainment — light to SCN, food to peripheral tissues — is the substrate for circadian misalignment when shift work, jet lag, or late-night eating drive the two systems out of phase.

#### Tissue-specific contributions to the 24-h ISF curve

The 24-h ISF rhythm is not a single phenomenon but a temporally coordinated sum of contributions from skeletal muscle, liver, adipose, and the β-cell:

- **Skeletal muscle (~80% of postprandial glucose disposal in healthy adults).** Muscle-specific *Bmal1* knockout in mice abolishes the diurnal rhythm in muscle glucose uptake and produces glucose intolerance with normal insulin secretion, identifying the intrinsic muscle clock — not systemic hormones alone — as a determinant of insulin-stimulated disposal ([Dyar et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24567902/); [Harfmann et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486508/)). The mechanism is multi-layered: GLUT4 mRNA and protein abundance oscillate ~2-fold across 24 h; AS160/TBC1D4 phosphorylation, which gates GLUT4 vesicle exocytosis, is rhythmic; mitochondrial respiratory capacity peaks during the active phase and falls overnight; and glycogen synthase activity is highest in the early active phase, favouring storage of incoming glucose at breakfast.
- **Liver.** Hepatic insulin sensitivity (suppression of EGP per unit insulin) is highest in the late biological day and lowest in the late biological night/early morning, with the latter contributing — together with the cortisol/GH dawn pulse — to dawn fasting hyperglycaemia. Hepatic glycogen synthesis, gluconeogenesis (PEPCK, G6Pase), and bile acid handling are all under strong clock control.
- **Adipose tissue.** Lipolytic rhythmicity tracks the cortisol axis (peak FFA flux in late night/early morning), generating an early-morning rise in plasma free fatty acids that promotes hepatic gluconeogenesis and acutely impairs muscle insulin signalling — a mechanism that overlaps with the §22-style intracellular DAG/PKC-θ pathway and contributes to morning peripheral insulin resistance.
- **Pancreatic β-cell.** In the oral minimal-model analysis used by [Saad et al. (2012)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC3478548/fullTextXML), disposition index is the **product** of insulin sensitivity and an index of β-cell responsiveness. Among 20 healthy participants studied with identical meals, both insulin sensitivity and β-cell responsiveness were generally higher at breakfast than later meals; individual patterns varied. The result does not describe two opposing rhythms or establish a fixed diurnal insulin requirement in T1D.

#### The breakfast–dinner asymmetry: clock effect, "second-meal" effect, and pre-load confounds

Morning–evening meal comparisons can be influenced by prior food intake, fasting duration, activity and preceding insulin exposure as well as circadian phase. A beneficial second-meal effect can partially oppose a deterioration in evening glucose tolerance; its magnitude depends on the protocol. Therefore, neither ordinary daytime feeding nor matched preloads imply a universal direction of bias. Controlled circadian designs distinguish these exposures more directly ([Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/); [Qian et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29862620/)). A single percentage should not combine biological time, meal history and circadian misalignment.

#### Shift work and circadian misalignment

In simulated shift-work protocols where the behavioural cycle is inverted by 12 h relative to the endogenous circadian phase, healthy young adults show a 6% rise in postprandial glucose despite a 22% rise in postprandial insulin — i.e. acutely induced peripheral insulin resistance ([Scheer et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2657421/)). Three of eight subjects in that study reached a postprandial glucose response in the prediabetic range during misalignment, despite normal tolerance at baseline. Long-term observational data are consistent: meta-analyses of cohort studies estimate a 9–37% relative excess of incident type 2 diabetes in rotating-shift workers, with a dose-response relation to years of exposure ([Gao et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31684766/)). Mechanistic interpretation is constrained by the fact that shift work is bundled with sleep restriction, late-night eating, and altered physical activity — but laboratory misalignment studies that hold sleep duration constant still demonstrate independent metabolic effects, supporting a primary circadian contribution.

### Quantitative characterization

| Quantity | Value | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Two-hour postprandial glucose AUC: biological evening vs morning | 12% higher overall; 17% on test day 1 and 7% on day 3 | 14 healthy adults; aligned and 12-hour-inverted behavioural schedules; endogenous insulin secretion also varied | [Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/) |
| Glucose rise above morning levels under continuous enteral nutrition | +17 ± 5% glucose; +49 ± 8% calculated insulin secretion | Sleep-isolated circadian rhythmicity; healthy adults | [Van Cauter et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1885778/) |
| Postprandial glucose AUC: dinner vs breakfast (healthy, isocaloric) | ~30% higher at dinner | Reproducible in multiple meal-test cohorts; partly confounded by pre-load | [Saad et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22751690/); [Owens et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8974189/) |
| Disposition index (β-cell responsivity × insulin sensitivity), breakfast vs dinner | Highest at breakfast, ~25–35% lower at dinner | Healthy adults, triple-tracer mixed-meal | [Saad et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22751690/) |
| Postprandial glucose during 12-h circadian misalignment vs alignment | +6% glucose, +22% insulin | Forced-desynchrony, simulated shift work | [Scheer et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2657421/) |
| Type 2 diabetes risk in rotating-shift workers | RR ~1.09–1.37 vs day workers | Dose-response with years of shift exposure | [Gao et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31684766/) |
| Fraction of muscle transcriptome under circadian control | ~5–20% | Includes GLUT4, glycogen-handling, mitochondrial biogenesis | [Harfmann et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25512305/); [Dyar et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24567902/) |
| Effect of muscle-specific Bmal1 KO | Abolished diurnal muscle glucose uptake; whole-body glucose intolerance with normal insulin secretion | Mouse model; identifies intrinsic muscle clock as causal | [Harfmann et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486508/) |
| Population-level diurnal SI curve in T1D adults | Not statistically significant on average; large inter-individual variability | n=19 adults, triple-tracer; morning EGP suppression weaker than later in day | [Hinshaw et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3712033/) |
| Real-world ISF in T1D children/adolescents on ultra-long-acting basal | Lower morning ISF, higher evening ISF | Single-centre paediatric cohort; not generalised to adults | [Hegab, 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8957904/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Two overlapping morning effects, not one.** Early-morning hyperglycaemia in T1D reflects (a) the §12 dawn phenomenon — acute cortisol/GH-driven hepatic glucose output rising from ~04:00 — and (b) the trough of the broader 24-h ISF rhythm in muscle, liver, and adipose. Clinically, the two are difficult to separate from CGM alone; pump basal-rate profiling typically captures the *summed* effect rather than parsing it mechanistically.
2. **Population-level evidence in T1D is weaker than in healthy adults.** Hinshaw and colleagues studied 19 adults with long-standing T1D under triple-tracer mixed-meal protocols and did not find a statistically significant population-wide diurnal SI rhythm; the more robust signal was that endogenous glucose production was less suppressed at breakfast despite higher insulin concentrations, supporting a hepatic component rather than a clean peripheral SI curve in adult T1D ([Hinshaw et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3712033/)). Paediatric real-world data show clearer time-of-day ISF differences (lower morning, higher evening), but external validity to adults is limited ([Hegab, 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8957904/)).
3. **Why the T1D signal is harder to detect.** Subcutaneous basal insulin pharmacokinetics impose their own time-varying plasma insulin profile that is convolved with the underlying circadian SI rhythm; missing endogenous β-cell pulsatility removes a feedback loop that healthy individuals use to absorb part of the rhythm; and inter-individual variability in pump basal-rate profiling and meal habits adds noise that obscures population-level patterns.
4. **Practical implications.** Time-segmented basal rates, breakfast-specific carb ratios, and dinner-specific bolus advancement are standard pump-therapy responses to the joint dawn + ISF effect. Closed-loop and hybrid closed-loop systems can compensate reactively for a fraction of the rhythm, but pre-emptive feed-forward correction (e.g., temporary basal increases before breakfast) is still commonly used and reflects the persistent residual error of purely reactive control.
5. **Shift work and extrapolation to T1D.** Controlled studies in healthy adults establish that circadian misalignment can impair glucose tolerance, but they do not define the magnitude or an upper bound of this effect in T1D. Measured endogenous insulin concentrations in healthy participants are not equivalent to exogenous insulin requirements in T1D ([Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/)).

<a name="carbohydrate-sensitivity-time-of-day"></a>
### Does the glucose response per gram of carbohydrate vary with time of day?

#### Terminology and interpretation

**Carbohydrate sensitivity factor (CSF)** denotes the glucose concentration increase associated with one gram of carbohydrate, expressed in mmol/L/g or mg/dL/g. Liu et al. (2020) explicitly use this term in a clinically evaluated rescue-carbohydrate recommender. It identifies an operational response parameter, not a universal physiological constant: their system updates CSF from glucose outcomes after carbohydrate treatment, and its feasibility evaluation was not a circadian physiology experiment ([Liu et al., 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7189144/)).

CSF differs from the **insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio (ICR)**, expressed here as grams per insulin unit, and the **insulin sensitivity factor (ISF)** or insulin correction factor, expressed as glucose reduction per insulin unit. If the abbreviation *ICF* denotes an insulin correction factor, it refers to the latter quantity rather than carbohydrate sensitivity. ICR and ISF are treatment-derived estimates, not direct measurements of the glucose rise caused by carbohydrate ([Hegab, 2022](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/pediatrics/articles/10.3389/fped.2022.854972/full)).

Under a simplified linear cancellation assumption, an implied factor is `CSF_implied = ISF / ICR`: the units reduce to mmol/L/g when ISF is expressed in mmol/L/U and ICR in g/U. This is an algebraic approximation, not independent evidence of physiological equivalence. A lower breakfast ICR alone cannot establish a higher breakfast CSF because ISF may change concurrently. Ratios of population medians also do not establish individual response ratios.

A measured carbohydrate response needs a defined endpoint. The glucose change after 15 minutes, peak increment and incremental area under the curve (iAUC; integrated glucose elevation above baseline) describe different quantities; iAUC per gram also has a time dimension. Dividing a response to a larger dose by its gram content gives an average dose-normalised response under those conditions, not necessarily the marginal effect of adding exactly 1 g. Furthermore, the observed change includes ongoing insulin action and background glucose fluxes; it is not the isolated carbohydrate effect relative to an otherwise identical no-carbohydrate condition.

#### Direct human evidence and its limits

Hinshaw et al. studied 19 C-peptide-negative adults with T1D using insulin pumps and with normal gastric emptying. Matched mixed meals containing approximately 50 g carbohydrate were served at 07:00, 13:00 and 19:00; labelled meals contained 50 g glucose. A triple-tracer method distinguished meal-derived glucose appearance from endogenous glucose production. Participants retained individualised basal insulin and premeal boluses. Dinner peak plasma glucose was lower than breakfast peak glucose, whereas 0–360-minute glucose iAUC did not differ significantly. Meal-derived glucose appearance and whole-body insulin sensitivity also showed no statistically significant meal differences. Because insulin exposure was not held constant, these results neither establish an independent circadian CSF nor demonstrate its absence ([Hinshaw et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3712033/)).

Hegab studied 93 participants aged 6–18 years using multiple injections with degludec or glargine U300. Clinically adjusted correction factors and carbohydrate ratios differed by time of day. However, finger-stick monitoring and treatment adjustment were not standardised oral-glucose challenges at matched insulin exposure. These results support time-dependent insulin requirements, not an independently measured carbohydrate-response rhythm ([Hegab, 2022](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/pediatrics/articles/10.3389/fped.2022.854972/full)).

| Study and source location | Quantitative finding | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| Hinshaw et al. (2013), Results; n=19 adults with T1D | Peak glucose: overall meal effect P=0.010; dinner versus breakfast P<0.01. Glucose iAUC: P=0.15; insulin sensitivity: P=0.34 | Peak and integrated response give different answers; individualised insulin treatment was retained |
| Hegab (2022), Table 3; n=93 children/adolescents with T1D | Morning/afternoon/evening ISF medians: 50/60/75 mg/dL/U; corresponding IQRs: 40–75/40–75/45–77.5 | Correction-factor estimates, not CSF measurements |
| Taleb et al. (2023), Table 2; n=32 adults with T1D | At starting glucose 3.0–3.5 mmol/L, 16 versus 32 g glucose produced 15-minute changes of 0.1 ± 0.8 versus 0.6 ± 0.9 mmol/L (mean ± SD) | Response during insulin-induced hypoglycaemia; no time-of-day comparison |
| Morris et al. (2015), circadian-effects Results; n=14 healthy adults | Biological-evening two-hour glucose AUC 12% higher overall; 17% on test day 1 and 7% on day 3 | Endogenous insulin secretion varied; not a T1D per-gram factor |

#### Why hypoglycaemia treatment and healthy circadian studies do not supply a universal CSF

Taleb et al. induced hypoglycaemia with subcutaneous insulin in fasting adults. At starting glucose below 3.0 mmol/L, 16 and 32 g glucose produced identical mean 15-minute increases of 0.8 mmol/L, with SDs of 0.9 and 1.0 mmol/L respectively (n=29). This lack of a proportional net response at a short endpoint does not mean the additional glucose was unabsorbed. Continuing insulin action, starting glucose and its preceding trajectory affect the observed change. The trial did not compare morning and evening treatment ([Taleb et al., 2023](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/endocrinology/articles/10.3389/fendo.2023.1186680/full)).

Morris et al. separated circadian and behavioural influences with two eight-day laboratory protocols using aligned versus 12-hour-inverted behavioural schedules. Their healthy-adult evening response included reduced early-phase endogenous insulin secretion. This mechanism cannot directly specify the magnitude or an upper bound of a carbohydrate-response rhythm in C-peptide-negative T1D ([Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/)).

**Evidence boundary:** A targeted literature search on 2026-09-06, covering carbohydrate sensitivity terminology, diurnal/circadian T1D meal studies and oral hypoglycaemia treatment, identified no direct within-subject T1D experiment quantifying a circadian CSF independently of changing insulin exposure. This was not an exhaustive systematic review, and failure to identify such a study does not establish absence of an effect. Repeated carbohydrate challenges would need defined observation windows and control of insulin exposure, baseline glucose trajectory, preceding meals, activity and sleep. Existing evidence supports context-dependent glucose responses but does not justify a separate universal morning-to-evening multiplier per gram of sugar, or a diurnal change in glucose distribution volume as its established cause.

### Controversies and limits of the evidence

The field is in a curious position: the *qualitative* picture (breakfast > dinner glucose tolerance in healthy adults; intrinsic muscle clock gates diurnal disposal in mice) is robust, but the *quantitative* T1D-specific 24-h ISF curve that pump algorithms or closed-loop systems should embed is not yet definitively established. Three open issues are worth flagging. (a) Pre-load confounds are pervasive: many morning-vs-evening tolerance studies do not match prior caloric intake, and the second-meal effect partly cancels the circadian effect. (b) Many model-based diurnal-ISF curves used in algorithm design (e.g. Toffanin and colleagues) are *synthetic constructions* fitted within virtual-patient simulators and validated by hypoglycaemia reduction in those same simulators — useful as engineering heuristics but not direct human validation of the underlying physiological curve ([Toffanin et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23911174/)). (c) Inter-individual variability in T1D appears to be at least as large as the population-mean rhythm itself, meaning a fixed time-of-day ISF correction is unlikely to capture the relevant dynamics for any given patient — chronotype, sleep schedule, meal timing, and basal-insulin choice all modulate the effective curve.

The simulator uses a ~50% peak-to-trough ISF amplitude (1.20 → 0.70 → 1.20) based on the Toffanin (2013) virtual-patient curve, dampened relative to algorithm-design references. This is an engineering heuristic for educational realism — it is *not* a population-validated T1D physiological curve (Hinshaw 2013 found no statistically significant population-wide diurnal SI rhythm in T1D adults). The choice should be revisited if better quantitative human T1D data become available.

<a name="menstrual-cycle"></a>
## 15. Menstrual cycle and insulin requirements

Ovarian hormone variation can coincide with changes in glucose exposure and insulin action in T1D, but phase-related effects differ between individuals and cycles. These changes occur alongside variation in food, activity, sleep, insulin delivery and self-management. [Gamarra and Trimboli (2023)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9962060/fullTextXML) conclude that a subset experiences luteal worsening, while the prevalence and a common effect size remain uncertain.

### Endocrinology of the cycle and metabolic correlates

The menstrual cycle is conventionally divided into four functional phases. The **menses** (cycle days 1-5) coincide with the lowest steroid levels: estradiol typically <100 pmol/L, progesterone <2 nmol/L. The **follicular phase** (days 1-13) is dominated by rising estradiol from the developing dominant follicle, peaking at 800-1500 pmol/L just before the LH surge (luteinizing hormone surge triggering ovulation at cycle day ~14). **Ovulation** (around day 14) is followed by formation of the corpus luteum (post-ovulatory ovarian structure secreting progesterone throughout the luteal phase), which during the **luteal phase** (days 15-28) produces progesterone in concentrations 10-40 nmol/L (a 10- to 30-fold rise over follicular baseline) along with a secondary estradiol peak of 400-800 pmol/L. If implantation does not occur the corpus luteum involutes, steroid concentrations collapse over 24-48 h, and menstruation ensues.

Each steroid has at least one biologically plausible mechanism for altering insulin action. **Progesterone** in supraphysiologic doses produces peripheral insulin resistance in animal models and humans, attributed to (i) impaired post-receptor insulin signaling in skeletal muscle, (ii) increased lipolysis with elevated circulating free fatty acids and consequent FFA-induced insulin resistance via the Randle cycle, and (iii) reduced GLUT4 translocation. **Estradiol** has bidirectional effects: physiologic concentrations enhance hepatic and peripheral insulin sensitivity, but supraphysiologic levels (as in pharmacological combined oral contraceptives) impair it. The luteal phase combines high progesterone with moderately elevated estradiol — net effect is typically a small reduction in whole-body insulin-mediated glucose disposal.

### Quantitative evidence in healthy women

Euglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamp data in non-diabetic women are remarkably consistent in showing **little or no whole-body insulin sensitivity change** across the cycle. Yki-Järvinen ([1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6376531/)) clamped seven healthy women at follicular and luteal phases and found glucose disposal of 9.0 ± 1.3 vs. 9.2 ± 1.6 mg/kg/min (NS), despite progesterone rising from 1.2 to 24.2 nmol/L. Diamond and colleagues likewise reported no significant change with hyperinsulinemic clamps in non-diabetic women. The BioCycle longitudinal study using HOMA-IR (homeostatic model assessment of insulin resistance, fasting-based surrogate) across multiple cycles in 257 healthy women detected statistically significant but small variation, with HOMA-IR peaking around the late follicular phase and the magnitude of change <10% — well within day-to-day biological noise. The take-home is that in metabolically intact women, ovarian counter-regulation, beta-cell adaptation and hepatic insulin clearance buffer most of the steroidal effect on circulating glucose. *(Caveat: clamp studies have small samples — typically n = 5-15 — and are powered to detect only large effects; subtle phase effects in subgroups would be missed.)*

### T1D-specific evidence: clamp and pump-based studies

Because women with T1D lack endogenous insulin and beta-cell counter-regulation, any phase-dependent change in insulin sensitivity translates more directly into glucose excursions. The literature here is genuinely heterogeneous.

The most-cited mechanistic study is **Widom, Diamond & Simonson ([1992](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1547678/))**, who performed hyperglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamps (glucose 11.7 mmol/L, insulin ~24 mU/L) in 16 IDDM women at follicular and luteal phases. Seven subjects ("deteriorators") showed luteal-phase glucose disposal falling from ~30.2 to 24.5 µmol/kg/min — a ~19% reduction — and this group also had a larger luteal-phase rise in estradiol (164 → 352 pmol/L). The remaining nine subjects ("non-deteriorators") had unchanged or improved disposal. This within-study heterogeneity does not establish the prevalence of a corresponding phenotype in the wider T1D population.

**Trout ([2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17425444/))** used frequently-sampled IVGTT (intravenous glucose tolerance test with minimal-model analysis) in five T1D pump users and found three of five with reduced luteal-phase insulin sensitivity, with no significant group-level effect. **Goldner ([2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15321002/))** used early-generation CGM over three consecutive cycles in four T1D women and found two with reproducible luteal-phase hyperglycemia patterns and two without — again a roughly 50-50 split. **Brown ([2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4667305/))** estimated nocturnal insulin sensitivity using Kalman-filtered CGM and pump data in 12 T1D women across 3 cycles each (33/36 ovulatory). Group-mean insulin sensitivity index fell from 4.6 ± 0.5 (early follicular) to 4.0 ± 0.9 (early-mid luteal, P = 0.05) and 4.0 ± 1.0 (late luteal, P = 0.03) — a ~13% reduction. Across the 36 cycles, 42% showed >5% luteal sensitivity reduction, 36% showed increased sensitivity, and 22% were unchanged; 9/12 subjects had a *consistent* pattern across at least two of their cycles. The latter is the strongest evidence to date that cyclic effects are individually reproducible even when the population mean is modest.

### CGM-era observational data

Cross-sectional CGM studies confirm a small but consistent group-level luteal-phase deterioration. **Tatulashvili ([2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35869507/))** prospectively monitored 24 T1D women over 62 cycles and observed time-in-range falling progressively from 61 ± 18% (early follicular) to 55 ± 20% (late luteal, P < 0.001), with significantly higher time-above-range late-luteal vs early-follicular (P = 0.003). The absolute TIR decrease (~6 percentage points) is real but small compared with intra-subject day-to-day variability of 10-20 percentage points. **Closed-loop cohorts attenuate this further:** Levy ([2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35099294/)) studied 16 T1D women across ≥3 cycles each on the Tandem Control-IQ system and found insulin delivery and CGM metrics statistically unchanged across phases. Monroy and colleagues ([2025, "780MENS"](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39898554/)) reported 12 T1D women on the MiniMed 780G with total daily insulin rising from 33.6 ± 12.2 IU (early follicular) to 37.2 ± 11.9 IU (late luteal, +11%, P < 0.001) and mean glucose rising from 7.3 to 7.7 mmol/L (P = 0.002), but with TIR essentially preserved (85% vs 83%, NS). The implication is that hybrid closed-loop algorithms compensate adequately for typical luteal increases in insulin demand.

### Perimenstrual hypoglycemia risk

Changes around menstruation may alter an individual's previously observed insulin requirement, but the literature does not establish a universal 10–20% decline over 24–48 hours. Insulin exposure carried over from an earlier phase and concurrent food or activity changes can modify hypoglycaemia risk. The pattern requires individual observation rather than an automatic phase-based dose escalation and taper ([Gamarra and Trimboli (2023)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9962060/fullTextXML)).

### PCOS, contraception and pregnancy interactions

**Polycystic ovary syndrome** (PCOS) is markedly over-represented in T1D, with prevalence depending on criteria: 12-18% by NIH 1990, 31% by Androgen Excess Society, and up to 40% by Rotterdam ([Codner & Escobar-Morreale, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17284617/)). Mechanistically the supraphysiologic peripheral insulin doses required in T1D drive ovarian theca-cell androgen production, mimicking the hyperinsulinemia of T2D-PCOS. Clinical features (oligomenorrhea, anovulation, hyperandrogenism) are typically milder than in T2D-PCOS but confound any cycle-tracking attempt: anovulatory cycles lack the luteal progesterone surge entirely, while irregular cycles produce timing uncertainty that defeats phase-based dose adjustments.

**Hormonal contraception** changes ovarian hormone exposure, but whether a particular preparation reduces cycle-associated glycaemic variability in T1D remains insufficiently established. [Gamarra and Trimboli (2023)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9962060/fullTextXML) identify oral contraception as a research gap. A preferred contraceptive formulation, a predictable insulin increment or abolition of glycaemic cycling cannot be inferred from this review.

The **transition into pregnancy** is signaled by failure of the corpus luteum to involute, sustained progesterone elevation and exponentially rising hCG, with insulin requirements typically rising 50-100% by mid-second trimester (covered separately).

### Inter-individual variability and clinical implications

[Gamarra and Trimboli (2023)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9962060/fullTextXML) included 14 studies with individual sample sizes of 4–124 participants. Definitions of menstrual phase, glucose outcomes and insulin sensitivity varied, risk of bias was generally high, and the authors did not perform a quantitative meta-analysis. Several studies found higher glucose exposure or reduced insulin sensitivity in the luteal phase, whereas others found no significant phase difference. The review does not establish a population-wide prevalence, a common percentage change in insulin requirement, or a fixed rate of change at menstruation. Fasting and postprandial effects may differ, and individual patterns require observation across cycles. A universal phase-based dose template cannot be inferred from this evidence; absence of evidence for a template is not proof that a particular intervention was tested and found ineffective.

<a name="seasonal-variation"></a>
## 16. Seasonal variation in glycemia and insulin requirements

Glycemic control in T1D is not a stationary signal. Repeated cross-sectional and longitudinal analyses of large registries demonstrate that mean HbA1c oscillates predictably with the calendar, with a winter peak and a late-summer trough in the northern hemisphere. The amplitude is small in absolute terms (≈0.2–0.7 percentage points in most cohorts) but exceeds the typical month-to-month within-subject coefficient of variation in stable adult T1D and is therefore detectable at population level. The phenomenon matters for three reasons: (i) it is a confounder in clinical trials and HbA1c-based diagnostic thresholds when enrollment or assay timing is not balanced across seasons; (ii) it shifts the operating point of automated insulin delivery (AID) algorithms and predictive bolus advisors that assume stationary insulin needs; (iii) it points to modifiable upstream drivers — temperature, activity, daylight, infections — each with mechanistic links covered elsewhere in this document.

### 16.1 Magnitude and timing in T1D registries

The most rigorous T1D-specific dataset is from the Lodz pediatric clinic (central Poland, 51°N): 3,935 HbA1c measurements in 677 children showed a sine-wave with peak in February (and secondary peaks in November–December) and trough in August–September; the winter–summer difference was 0.44–0.67 percentage points across consecutive years (max single-month delta 0.72 pp), against a cohort mean HbA1c of 7.65 ± 1.12% [Mianowska 2011]. The signal was confined to schoolchildren (≥7 y); preschoolers showed white-noise variability, attributable to caregiver-controlled rather than child-driven activity and meal patterns. Swedish pediatric registry data (n ≈ 8,000) reproduce a seasonal HbA1c pattern with similar phasing [Hanberger 2014]. Adult T1D data are sparser; a Japanese tertiary-care series (n = 88 with severe-hypoglycemia events) reported median HbA1c 9.1% (winter) vs 7.7% (summer), though the comparison did not reach significance given small subgroup sizes (P = 0.13) [Tsujimoto 2014]. As a working figure for adult T1D, an amplitude of ≈0.2–0.5 pp is consistent with the published data, with the larger end of that range applying to high-latitude, lifestyle-active subgroups; pediatric amplitudes are typically larger.

### 16.2 Mechanism 1 — temperature and insulin absorption

Ambient temperature correlated negatively with HbA1c in Lodz schoolchildren at R = −0.56 (p = 0.0002), and hours of sunshine and solar irradiance similarly with R ≈ −0.52 [Mianowska 2011]. Mechanistically, warmer ambient and skin temperature raises subcutaneous blood flow and accelerates insulin absorption from the depot; this is treated quantitatively in §21 (temperature/climate effects on insulin absorption). The same physics produces the paradoxical Tsujimoto finding of *more* severe hypoglycemia in summer in T1D (35.2% vs 18.2% of cases, p = 0.01) despite *lower* mean HbA1c [Tsujimoto 2014]: a faster, taller insulin-action curve sharpens both the average effect and the tail risk of overshoot, particularly when meal-bolus timing or basal rates set in cooler months are not retitrated. *Caveat:* the temperature contribution cannot be cleanly separated from concurrent changes in physical activity and clothing-mediated heat exposure at the injection site, and most observational studies do not adjudicate these.

### 16.3 Mechanism 2 — physical activity seasonality

Daylight hours and ambient temperature jointly drive an outdoor-activity gradient that is steep in temperate latitudes. Pedometer and accelerometer data in non-diabetic populations show 20–40% higher daily step counts in summer than winter at latitudes >40°. Exercise increases insulin sensitivity acutely (next 24–48 h) and chronically (training adaptation), and this is the dominant pathway by which behavior couples to seasonal HbA1c (mechanism quantified in §10, exercise). The pediatric school-vs-preschool dissociation in [Mianowska 2011] is the strongest single piece of evidence that activity, not photoperiod or vitamin D *per se*, drives the bulk of the signal: preschoolers experience the same temperature, daylight and vitamin D as schoolchildren but lack the autonomous outdoor-play exposure, and they show no seasonal HbA1c pattern. *Caveat:* activity is partially confounded with school-holiday meal patterns (different carbohydrate loads, snacking frequency), which the registry data cannot resolve.

### 16.4 Mechanism 3 — vitamin D and insulin sensitivity

25-hydroxyvitamin D follows a strong seasonal cycle at temperate latitudes (winter trough roughly 30–50% below summer peak), and observational studies link low 25(OH)D to higher HbA1c and lower insulin sensitivity. However, Mendelian randomization in 443,734 Europeans found no causal effect of genetically lowered vitamin D on T1D risk [Manousaki 2021], and randomized supplementation trials in established T1D have not produced clinically meaningful HbA1c reductions. The most defensible position is that vitamin D is a *marker* of light exposure and outdoor activity rather than a major independent driver of seasonal glycemia in T1D; any direct β-cell or muscle insulin-signaling effect is small relative to the activity and absorption mechanisms above.

### 16.5 Mechanism 4 — winter respiratory infections

Winter respiratory virus seasons (influenza, RSV, rhinovirus, since 2020 SARS-CoV-2) drive transient insulin resistance via cytokine release (TNF-α, IL-6) and counter-regulatory hormone activation (mechanism in §17, illness/infection). Infection episodes are typically days-to-weeks long, which is short relative to the 8–12 week HbA1c integration window, but their winter clustering biases the integrated signal upward. The SWEET registry showed that pandemic-era disruption changed not only T1D incidence seasonality but the timing of glycemic control nadirs, supporting infection load as a real contributor [SWEET 2022]. *Caveat:* corticosteroid prescriptions for respiratory illness amplify the effect and are not consistently captured in registry data.

### 16.6 Mechanism 5 — diet, light exposure and circadian alignment

Northern-hemisphere winter is associated with higher caloric and carbohydrate intake (holidays, comfort foods, reduced fresh produce), shorter photoperiods, and circadian phase-delay in late chronotypes. Each of these has plausible mechanistic links to insulin sensitivity (circadian misalignment lowers peripheral insulin sensitivity by 10–15% in controlled lab studies), but the specific contribution to seasonal HbA1c in T1D has not been quantified. Holiday-period (Dec–early Jan) glucose excursions are clearly visible in CGM cohort data but represent a minority of the integrated winter excess.

### 16.7 Latitude, hemisphere asymmetry and equatorial cohorts

Seasonal amplitude is not universal. The T1D incidence-seasonality literature, which is methodologically simpler than HbA1c-seasonality, found that of 105 worldwide centers only 42 showed significant seasonality in incidence; latitude largely accounted for the difference, with southern-hemisphere centers showing inverted (July–September peak) patterns consistent with their winter [Moltchanova 2009]. For HbA1c specifically, Scandinavian, German, Polish and UK studies show clear winter peaks; equatorial and low-latitude cohorts show attenuated or absent patterns; a North Indian study found a *monsoon* peak rather than a thermal-winter peak, attributed to monsoon-related activity restriction and festival-period dietary patterns [Sapra 2024]. The takeaway: the *direction* and *magnitude* of seasonal HbA1c variation in any given clinic depends on local climate amplitude, cultural activity patterns, and infection ecology — temperate-zone Northern Hemisphere data should not be extrapolated to equatorial care.

### 16.8 Open questions and climate-change implications

Whether climate-change-driven warming and milder winters will compress the seasonal HbA1c amplitude in temperate latitudes is unresolved. A naive prediction from the Mianowska temperature correlation (R = −0.56) is that winter HbA1c would fall as winters warm, narrowing the seasonal swing; this is plausible but unproven and ignores compensating shifts in infection seasonality and indoor heating practices. Whether AID systems should incorporate seasonal priors on basal rate and ICR/ISF is an open algorithmic question — current commercial systems adapt within days-to-weeks but do not encode an annual cycle.

# Part 6: Lifestyle and External Factors

<a name="illness-infection"></a>
## 17. Acute illness and infection — sick-day physiology

Acute intercurrent illness is one of the most common precipitants of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) in established type 1 diabetes (T1D). In international pediatric registries combining data from England, Wales, the U.S., Austria, and Germany, intercurrent infection is consistently among the leading identifiable triggers of DKA episodes, alongside missed insulin and pump failure ([Maahs 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26055086/); [Wolfsdorf 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29900641/)). The mechanistic problem is that infection can alter insulin requirements while disrupting intake, absorption and insulin delivery. Holt and colleagues' 2024 review estimates that people with diabetes carry a 1.5- to 4-fold increased risk of infection, with hyperglycemia itself impairing neutrophil function, complement activation, and adaptive responses, creating a positive-feedback loop where poorly controlled BG worsens the very infection driving the loss of control ([Holt 2024 — Diabetologia, OA](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11153295/)).

### Cytokine biology of acute infection

Within hours of pathogen recognition, innate immune cells (macrophages, monocytes, dendritic cells) and tissue-resident sentinels release a coordinated wave of pro-inflammatory cytokines: TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6. IL-6 is the dominant systemic signal — its plasma concentration tracks the magnitude and severity of infection more reliably than TNF-α or IL-1β, both of which act largely paracrine and have shorter half-lives. In moderate viral or bacterial infection (e.g. influenza, community-acquired pneumonia, pyelonephritis), circulating IL-6 typically rises from a baseline of <5 pg/mL to 20–200 pg/mL, with peaks of 1000+ pg/mL in severe sepsis. TNF-α rises more modestly in plasma (often only 2- to 10-fold) but exerts strong local effects on insulin-target tissues. Caveat: cytokine concentrations vary widely between infections, between individuals, and between assay platforms — the absolute numbers above are order-of-magnitude clinical orientation, not precise thresholds.

### Cytokine-induced insulin resistance — molecular mechanism

The signaling pathway by which TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6 blunt insulin action is the same set of serine kinase cascades described in detail in [§10 Exercise-induced inflammation](#exercise-inflammation): cytokine receptor engagement activates JNK and IKK-β, both of which phosphorylate IRS-1 on inhibitory residues including Ser307, while IL-6 in particular induces SOCS3, which directly competes with IRS-1 for receptor binding and targets it for proteasomal degradation. The downstream consequence is impaired tyrosine phosphorylation of IRS-1, blunted PI3K/Akt signaling, reduced GLUT4 translocation in skeletal muscle, and attenuated insulin suppression of hepatic glucose production. The acute-illness version of this pathway is essentially the same biology operating with steeper kinetics — hours rather than days — and with a stronger systemic component because IL-6 spillover from the infection site reaches all insulin-target tissues simultaneously ([Senn 2002 — Diabetes](https://diabetesjournals.org/diabetes/article/51/12/3391/34282/); [Senn 2003 — JBC, SOCS3](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12560330/); [de Alvaro 2004 — JBC](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15199052/); [Pickup 2004 — Diabetologia](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00125-004-1447-y)). Caveat: IL-6 has dual roles — chronic elevation drives insulin resistance, while transient exercise-induced IL-6 from contracting muscle is insulin-sensitizing; the difference appears to lie in source, duration, and co-secretion of IL-1ra versus TNF-α.

### Counterregulatory hormone activation

Pro-inflammatory cytokines do not act in isolation. IL-6, IL-1β, and TNF-α each independently stimulate the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis, with synergistic effects when combined; IL-6 is the most potent of the three and can drive ACTH and cortisol release even when CRH signaling is blocked, indicating direct pituitary action ([Bethin 2000 — PNAS](https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.97.16.9317)). Plasma cortisol typically doubles to triples during moderate febrile illness, with loss of the normal diurnal rhythm — sustained elevation through the night is part of why nocturnal hyperglycemia is a hallmark of sick days. Glucagon also rises, partly via cytokine-stimulated α-cell secretion and partly via baroreflex/dehydration-driven sympathetic activation. The combined cortisol + glucagon + catecholamine signature drives hepatic gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis, increases lipolysis (raising FFA delivery to liver), and antagonizes peripheral insulin action — the same counterregulatory profile described in [§11 Counterregulatory hormones](#counterregulatory-hormones), now layered on top of cytokine-induced IR.

### Fever as multiplier of metabolic demand

Fever is energetically expensive. Classical indirect calorimetry studies (DuBois; Roe & Kinney; reviewed in [Manthous 1995](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3621073/)) show that resting metabolic rate rises approximately 10–13% per °C above 37 °C, driven by Q10 effects on enzyme kinetics and the energetic cost of generating and defending the new hypothalamic setpoint (shivering thermogenesis at fever onset, then increased substrate cycling). A 2 °C fever therefore lifts basal energy demand by ~20–25%. In T1D this matters because the substrate fueling that demand must come from somewhere — and with insulin signaling already blunted by cytokines, the body increasingly mobilizes hepatic glycogen and fatty acids rather than glucose uptake into peripheral tissues. Caveat: fever's direct contribution to hyperglycemia is smaller than the cytokine + counterregulatory hormone effect; the dominant clinical signal is insulin resistance, not heat-driven gluconeogenesis.

### The gastrointestinal-illness paradox

Gastrointestinal illness may lower glucose through reduced intake, delayed gastric emptying or impaired absorption, whereas febrile and inflammatory responses can increase insulin requirements. Requirements may therefore increase or decrease; continued need for insulin is not evidence that basal requirements invariably rise. The pediatric ISPAD guidance emphasizes ongoing insulin delivery together with glucose, ketone, hydration and clinical assessment ([Phelan et al. (2022)](https://www.ispad.org/static/6551d05a-156c-4772-a32a6d663fcfc2d4/2022CGCh13PediatricDiabetes-2.pdf), sections 3–4).

### Accelerated ketogenesis in T1D illness

Insufficient insulin action permits adipose lipolysis and increased hepatic ketone production. Counterregulatory drive and reduced nutrient intake can both contribute during illness, including when glucose is not markedly elevated. Ketonaemia with low or normal glucose may reflect reduced intake and insulin deficiency, but ketone concentration alone does not diagnose euglycaemic DKA: metabolic acidosis and the diabetes context must also be established ([Phelan et al. (2022)](https://www.ispad.org/static/6551d05a-156c-4772-a32a6d663fcfc2d4/2022CGCh13PediatricDiabetes-2.pdf); [Glaser et al., 2022](https://www.ispad.org/static/6dd62eae-c8cb-4b4a-84e1efc768505746/Ch11PediatricDiabetes.pdf)). The distinction between ketosis and ketoacidosis is developed in §23.

### Specific infection profiles

- **Influenza and other respiratory viruses.** Strong systemic IL-6 response, prolonged fever (3–7 d), often modest GI involvement. Insulin requirements typically up 20–50% during the febrile phase, declining over the subsequent week. Influenza vaccination is recommended annually for T1D, and observational data suggest reduced hospitalization rates in vaccinated individuals.
- **COVID-19 / SARS-CoV-2.** Particular concern in T1D because of (a) ACE2 expression on β-cells raising theoretical risk of further islet injury, (b) profound IL-6 storm in severe cases, and (c) early pandemic data showing increased presentation of new-onset T1D in DKA. The Unsworth multicenter U.K. cohort reported 70% DKA at presentation in new-onset pediatric T1D during the first COVID-19 wave (vs ~30–40% baseline), with 52% of DKA cases severe ([Unsworth 2020 — Diabetes Care, OA](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/43/11/e170/35903/)). Causality for new-onset T1D induction by SARS-CoV-2 remains debated; what is clear is that the pandemic's healthcare-access disruption raised DKA-at-diagnosis rates substantially.
- **Gastroenteritis.** As above — high pediatric DKA risk despite often modest fever, due to the eating/vomiting/insulin paradox.
- **Urinary tract infection (UTI).** Particularly relevant in adults with longer-duration T1D; glycosuria provides bacterial substrate and partial immune impairment in poorly-controlled diabetes raises pyelonephritis risk. UTI is a classical "occult" precipitant of DKA in patients without overt febrile symptoms.
- **Dental abscess and skin/soft-tissue infection.** Often underestimated as DKA precipitants because patients may not recognize a dental or cellulitic source as systemic illness. Clinically important to ask explicitly during DKA workup.

### Sick-day rules — ISPAD 2022 framing

Sick-day care is a clinical response to changing insulin need, intake, hydration and ketone production. The 2022 pediatric guideline recommends preparation, repeated assessment and access to diabetes-team support; its recommendations are not fixed physiological multipliers ([Phelan et al. (2022)](https://www.ispad.org/static/6551d05a-156c-4772-a32a6d663fcfc2d4/2022CGCh13PediatricDiabetes-2.pdf)).

The carbohydrate content of fluids and adjustments in insulin depend on glucose, intake, ketones and clinical condition. A ketone value alone does not justify a universal instruction to give carbohydrate with every correction dose. Likewise, the need to continue insulin does not imply keeping an unchanged basal dose during every gastrointestinal illness. Pediatric, adult and device-specific guidance must remain distinct.

Blood β-hydroxybutyrate can diverge from urinary acetoacetate. Persistent urine ketones during recovery can mislead interpretation, and a glucose sensor does not measure ketosis. Detailed treatment schedules belong in the relevant clinical guideline rather than in a physiological parameter table.

### Controversies and uncertainties

- **Pediatric vs adult differences.** Children show faster ketogenic flux per unit illness severity, smaller glycogen buffer, and higher dependence on caregiver decisions — combining to give substantially higher gastroenteritis-associated DKA rates than adults. Adult sick-day rules drawn directly from pediatric ISPAD frameworks may under-emphasize the role of comorbid conditions (cardiovascular disease, renal impairment) that matter more in older T1D populations.
- **Fixed multipliers vs glucose-driven titration.** Older sick-day frameworks specified fixed percentage increases (e.g. "+20% basal during illness"). Modern ISPAD guidance moves toward ketone- and glucose-driven titration because the insulin-resistance magnitude varies enormously between infections and patients — a fixed multiplier under-doses severe sepsis and over-doses mild rhinovirus.
- **COVID-era data.** The 2020–2022 COVID-19 pandemic generated a large but heterogeneous body of data on T1D-infection interaction. Genuine signals (DKA-at-diagnosis spike, IL-6 storm in severe cases) coexist with confounded signals (healthcare-access disruption, delayed presentation, behavioral change) that are difficult to disentangle. Long-term incidence data for whether SARS-CoV-2 itself accelerates T1D onset are still maturing.
- **Sick-day BOHB thresholds.** The 1.5 mmol/L action threshold is operationally validated but mechanistically it is a continuum — ketogenesis is already accelerated at 0.6 mmol/L. Some frameworks (notably newer pump-loop algorithms) initiate intervention at lower thresholds.
