<a name="sleep-deprivation"></a>
## 18. Sleep and sleep deprivation

Sleep is an active neuroendocrine state, not a passive interlude, and its architecture is tightly coupled to glucose homeostasis. A normal adult night cycles four to six times through ~90-minute periods of non-rapid-eye-movement (NREM) sleep — divided into N1, N2, and slow-wave sleep (SWS, N3) — alternating with rapid-eye-movement (REM) sleep. SWS dominates the first third of the night and coincides with the nocturnal nadir of cortisol, the sympathetic nervous system, and core body temperature, together with the largest spontaneous growth-hormone (GH) pulse of the 24-hour cycle. REM dominates the last third and is accompanied by autonomic instability, rising adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)/cortisol secretion, and a partial uncoupling of cerebral glucose utilisation from systemic glycaemia. Disturbance of either the duration or the internal architecture of sleep alters the magnitude and timing of these endocrine signals, with quantifiable downstream effects on insulin sensitivity, hepatic glucose output, appetite hormones, and counterregulation. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), the picture is layered: the same physiology applies, but the night is also when most exogenous-insulin overdoses, fear-of-hypoglycaemia, CGM/pump alarm awakenings, and nocturia from osmotic diuresis converge to fragment sleep in ways healthy controls do not experience. Sleep biology is therefore a clinically and physiologically relevant axis for T1D — and one of the most under-studied.

### Normal sleep architecture and endocrine timing

A consolidated 7–9 h adult night opens with a rapid descent through N1 and N2 into SWS within ~15–25 min. The first NREM-REM cycle ends ~90 min after sleep onset and is followed by progressively shorter SWS episodes and longer, denser REM episodes towards morning. Two endocrine systems are anchored to this architecture. First, the somatotropic axis: ~70–80 % of the daily GH pulse mass is released during the first SWS episode in young adults, and SWS suppression abolishes this pulse without compensatory daytime release. Second, the hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal (HPA) axis: cortisol secretion is actively inhibited during early-night SWS, reaches its nadir around sleep onset, then rises through the late night to peak shortly after waking. Sympathetic outflow shows a similar pattern — low during SWS, rising during REM and the second half of the night. (*Caveat:* these patterns are best characterised in young, healthy men sleeping in laboratory conditions; women, older adults, and individuals with chronic disease show attenuated SWS and a flatter cortisol nadir, complicating extrapolation.)

### The Spiegel–Van Cauter sleep-restriction series

The classical evidence base for "sleep loss as a metabolic stressor" comes from a tightly controlled series at the University of Chicago. In the landmark 1999 *Lancet* report, 11 healthy young men were studied across 6 nights of severe restriction (4 h time-in-bed) followed by 6 nights of recovery (12 h time-in-bed). After the restriction phase, intravenous glucose-tolerance-test (ivGTT) glucose clearance fell ~40 % and the acute insulin response to glucose fell ~30 %, yielding a glucose-tolerance pattern resembling impaired glucose tolerance in older adults. Evening cortisol concentrations rose markedly (P = 0.0001) — a "flattening" of the diurnal cortisol slope rather than a uniform increase — sympathetic heart-rate-variability indices increased (P < 0.02), and thyrotropin fell (P < 0.01). A 2004 *Annals of Internal Medicine* brief communication extended the work to appetite regulation: 2 nights of 4 h sleep versus 2 nights of 10 h, in a controlled-feeding crossover, reduced circulating leptin by ~18 %, increased ghrelin by ~28 %, and increased self-rated hunger and appetite by ~24 % and ~23 %, respectively, with the largest effect on calorie-dense, carbohydrate-rich foods (+33–45 %). (*Caveat:* the Spiegel cohorts were small (n = 11–12), exclusively young, lean men, studied under fixed isocaloric feeding and bed-rest conditions; the effect sizes are likely upper bounds, and free-living adults compensate partially through behaviour.)

### Replication and dose–response in independent cohorts

Subsequent independent groups have largely replicated the direction of effect with smaller magnitudes under more moderate restriction. Buxton et al. (2010, *Diabetes*) restricted 20 healthy men to 5 h/night for 7 nights after an 8-night 10 h baseline and observed a 20 ± 24 % fall in ivGTT-derived insulin sensitivity (P = 0.001) and an 11 ± 5.5 % fall in clamp-measured insulin sensitivity (P < 0.04), without proportionate changes in salivary cortisol or catecholamines — implicating mechanisms beyond classical stress hormones. Nedeltcheva et al. (2009, *J Clin Endocrinol Metab*) showed that even in middle-aged adults, 14 nights of 5.5 h time-in-bed (versus 8.5 h) reduced glucose tolerance and the disposition index, raised evening cortisol, and produced sustained increases in hunger ratings. Selective SWS suppression — sparing total sleep time but eliminating slow-wave activity using acoustic stimuli — independently impaired insulin sensitivity by ~25 % over only 3 nights (Tasali 2008, *PNAS*), demonstrating that sleep *quality*, not just total duration, is metabolically relevant. These different interventions and populations do not establish a common insulin-sensitivity reduction per hour of lost sleep. Chronic restriction, circadian misalignment and selective slow-wave suppression require separate interpretation.

### Molecular and circadian mechanisms

Several non-mutually-exclusive pathways connect sleep loss to insulin resistance. (i) **Sympathetic overdrive:** restriction shifts heart-rate variability towards low-frequency (sympathetic) dominance and raises plasma noradrenaline, suppressing β-cell insulin secretion and stimulating hepatic glucose output. (ii) **HPA-axis flattening:** the loss of the early-night cortisol nadir produces a sustained mild hypercortisolaemia that, over days, induces hepatic and adipose insulin resistance through classical glucocorticoid-receptor signalling. (iii) **Adipokine perturbation:** the leptin fall and ghrelin rise increase energy intake and shift substrate selection towards carbohydrate, exacerbating postprandial glycaemia. (iv) **Peripheral clock disruption:** the molecular clock genes BMAL1, CLOCK, PER1–3, and CRY1–2 oscillate in liver, muscle, adipose, and pancreatic β-cells; misalignment between the central suprachiasmatic clock and peripheral oscillators (as in shift work, jet lag, or social jet lag) reduces β-cell insulin response and skeletal-muscle GLUT4 translocation independently of sleep duration. (v) **Mitochondrial and inflammatory signalling:** sleep restriction raises IL-6, TNF-α, and CRP and impairs muscle mitochondrial respiration, contributing to insulin resistance through the JNK/IKK-β pathway. Cross-link [§12 (dawn phenomenon)](#dawn): the cortisol-rise that produces dawn hyperglycaemia in T1D is the *normal* end of the sleep-locked cortisol curve, and sleep loss broadens and amplifies it — but the two phenomena are mechanistically distinct (dawn = circadian cortisol/GH timing; sleep loss = sustained HPA/SNS activation).

### Sleep restriction in T1D specifically

[Donga et al. (2010)](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC2890361/fullTextXML) obtained paired clamp data in seven adults with T1D after normal sleep and sleep restricted to four hours. Nine participants enrolled; two were excluded from analysis because of nocturnal hypoglycaemia/hyperglycaemia or previously undetected sleep apnea. Glucose disposal fell from 25.5 ± 2.6 to 22.0 ± 2.1 μmol·kg lean body mass⁻¹·min⁻¹ (approximately 14%; P=0.04). Glucose infusion required to maintain euglycaemia fell from 19.0 ± 2.9 to 14.9 ± 2.1 in the same units (approximately 21%; P=0.04). Values are means ± SEM. Basal endogenous glucose production and its suppression during the clamp did not differ significantly. The experiment supports an acute reduction in peripheral insulin action under its conditions; it does not establish a proportional change in daily insulin dose, an effect per hour of lost sleep, or chronic-restriction kinetics.

### Counterregulatory blunting during sleep

Healthy sleep itself attenuates the counterregulatory response to hypoglycaemia. In landmark clamp work by Jones et al. (1998) and Banarer & Cryer (2003), nocturnal hypoglycaemia during sleep produced markedly lower epinephrine and symptom responses than identical hypoglycaemia while awake, and crucially failed to wake most subjects. In T1D this defect is compounded by the broader hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF) phenomenon (cross-link [§11](#hypoglycemia-haaf)): a single antecedent nocturnal hypoglycaemic event blunts daytime symptom recognition and adrenergic response for 24–48 h, creating the well-described vicious cycle of nocturnal hypoglycaemia → impaired awareness → further nocturnal events. Sleep is thus both a permissive state for prolonged hypoglycaemia and a trigger for next-day HAAF.

### Real-world overnight glycaemia and sleep fragmentation in T1D

CGM-era observational data describe a heterogeneous picture. Well-controlled adult T1D cohorts on hybrid closed-loop or sensor-augmented pumps achieve ~50–70 % overnight time-in-range (3.9–10 mmol/L), with progressively higher overnight TIR than daytime TIR as algorithms have matured; multiple-daily-injection cohorts perform less well. Reutrakul et al.'s 2016 systematic review and meta-analysis (22 studies) found that adults with T1D self-report poorer subjective sleep quality than controls, that those sleeping > 6 h have lower HbA1c than those sleeping ≤ 6 h, and that children with T1D sleep ~26 min less per night than controls. CGM alarm awakenings are a substantial and under-quantified contributor to fragmentation: published series report median 1–4 alarm-driven awakenings per night in unoptimised settings, with high-tone glucose alarms producing sleep-stage transitions even when the patient does not consciously wake. Fear of nocturnal hypoglycaemia is reported by 50–75 % of T1D adults and parents of children with T1D, and predicts intentional pre-sleep hyperglycaemia targeting that elevates HbA1c independently of objective glycaemic exposure. (*Caveat:* these are observational, with strong selection bias towards motivated CGM users; the "fragmentation burden" of T1D in unselected populations is largely unmeasured.)

### Obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) in diabetes

OSA — recurrent upper-airway collapse producing intermittent hypoxia and arousals — is causally linked to insulin resistance through sympathetic activation, oxidative stress, and HPA-axis dysregulation. The prevalence in type 2 diabetes is well-characterised at 50–70 %. In T1D the data are sparser but increasingly suggest a similar burden: Reutrakul's meta-analysis estimated a pooled OSA prevalence of ~52 % in adults with T1D, far above general-population rates of ~10–25 %, with moderate-to-severe OSA trending towards higher HbA1c. Mechanisms include obesity (increasingly common in T1D), autonomic neuropathy (which may impair upper-airway tone and ventilatory drive), and possibly diabetes-specific changes in pharyngeal soft-tissue glycation. (*Caveat:* heterogeneity in diagnostic methods (polysomnography vs home testing vs questionnaires) inflates the confidence interval, and selection bias in sleep-clinic cohorts is large.)

### Shift work, social jet lag, and chronic circadian misalignment

Forced desynchrony protocols — in which subjects live on a 28-h "day" so their behavioural schedule rotates against the central clock — show that circadian misalignment alone, with adequate total sleep, raises postprandial glucose ~6 % and reduces insulin response to a meal ~32 % (Scheer 2009, *PNAS*; Buxton 2012, *Sci Transl Med*). Real-world shift workers have a ~40 % relative-risk increase for type 2 diabetes; in T1D, observational data link rotating shifts and large weekday–weekend bedtime shifts ("social jet lag" (chronic misalignment between social schedule and internal circadian clock) > 2 h) to higher HbA1c and more glycaemic variability (Larcher 2016, *Eur J Endocrinol*). Mechanistically, peripheral-clock misalignment with the central clock is the leading hypothesis, with direct β-cell clock disruption demonstrated in murine models.

### Sleep extension as an intervention

Pilot trials of sleep extension (typically increasing time-in-bed by 60–90 min/night for 1–6 weeks) in habitually short-sleeping adults without diabetes show modest improvements in fasting glucose, HOMA-IR, and appetite-hormone profiles, and meaningful reductions in spontaneous energy intake (~270 kcal/day in So-ngern 2019). Direct extension trials in T1D are essentially absent; the available evidence supports sleep hygiene and device-appropriate management of alarm burden without compromising hypoglycaemia detection as plausible — but unproven — adjuncts to glycaemic management.

<a name="alcohol"></a>
## 19. Alcohol -- ethanol metabolism, gluconeogenesis, and delayed hypoglycemia

Alcohol can increase the risk of hypoglycaemia in insulin-treated T1D, but the glucose response depends on nutritional state, insulin exposure, activity and the composition of the drink. Ethanol metabolism, endogenous glucose production, glucose disposal and symptom recognition are distinct processes. Their effects should not be represented as a fixed percentage suppression or an obligatory nocturnal sequence. The mechanistic evidence reviewed below includes healthy-human, animal and isolated-tissue experiments; it does not provide a single quantitative response for T1D ([Steiner et al., 2015](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC4693236/fullTextXML)).

### Mechanism

#### Hepatic ethanol metabolism and the NADH/NAD+ ratio

Alcohol dehydrogenase oxidises ethanol to acetaldehyde while reducing NAD⁺ to NADH; aldehyde dehydrogenase then oxidises acetaldehyde to acetate. These reactions alter cytosolic and mitochondrial redox states. The microsomal CYP2E1 pathway also contributes to ethanol oxidation, particularly with chronic exposure, but its cofactor stoichiometry is not identical to that of alcohol dehydrogenase. Increased NADH/NAD⁺ favours lactate relative to pyruvate and changes the availability of gluconeogenic intermediates. A fixed fold-change in redox state or glucose production cannot be assigned across nutritional states ([Steiner et al., 2015](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC4693236/fullTextXML)).

#### Inhibition of hepatic gluconeogenesis

The lactate–pyruvate and related redox equilibria connect ethanol oxidation to gluconeogenic substrate use. Inhibition is substrate- and protocol-dependent: findings from lactate-supported gluconeogenesis cannot be generalized to near-complete inhibition from every precursor. Gluconeogenesis, glycogenolysis, hepatic glucose output and whole-body glucose disposal must also remain separate endpoints. Steiner et al. (2015) summarize human studies in which glucose production is unchanged or decreases together with disposal, alongside animal and isolated-tissue experiments examining individual substrate pathways.

Siler et al. (1998) studied 48 g oral alcohol in overnight-fasted normal men, not an intravenous ethanol clamp in T1D. The indexed abstract reports a 45% lower gluconeogenic flux relative to placebo and a 12% reduction in hepatic glucose output within the alcohol condition, from 2.03 ± 0.21 to 1.79 ± 0.21 mg/kg/min, without a change in plasma glucose. These abstract-level data support inhibition of gluconeogenesis with compensating changes elsewhere in glucose flux; they do not support a 50% suppression of total endogenous glucose production or a near-complete blockade in T1D. Detailed protocol appraisal requires the original full text.

#### Glycogen availability and nutritional state

Glycogen availability modifies the consequences of inhibited gluconeogenesis. In healthy humans, alcohol after feeding or a relatively short fast can leave plasma glucose unchanged, whereas prolonged fasting increases vulnerability when glycogen availability is limited. This is not evidence that feeding guarantees protection in insulin-treated T1D. The contribution of glycogenolysis changes with insulin, prior intake and exercise, and total glucose output may be offset by changes in disposal. An obligatory overnight depletion time or a fixed 6–12-hour delay cannot be inferred from these mechanisms ([Steiner et al., 2015](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC4693236/fullTextXML)).

#### Counterregulation and symptom recognition

Hormonal responses and recognition of hypoglycaemia can also be affected by alcohol. These effects must be separated from inhibition of gluconeogenesis and from the pre-existing counterregulatory abnormalities of T1D ([§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)). The available mechanistic review does not establish a common percentage reduction in each hormone or in symptom scores for all insulin-treated adults. Nor should an alcohol-associated impairment in recognition be equated with structural, irreversible unawareness or presumed autoimmune destruction of α-cells. The persistence of exogenous insulin action and the individual's awareness status influence the consequences of an impaired response ([Steiner et al., 2015](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC4693236/fullTextXML)).

#### Carbohydrate-containing beverages and glucose trajectories

Carbohydrate-containing drinks or accompanying food can raise glucose while ethanol metabolism affects endogenous production. An early rise therefore does not establish the direction of the later glucose response. Conversely, the absence of carbohydrate does not prove that a particular late hypoglycaemic episode will occur. Beverage composition, meal timing and continuing insulin absorption must be considered separately; it is not valid to assume that only basal insulin remains active at a predetermined hour.

#### Exercise + alcohol

Recent exercise can alter glucose uptake, insulin action and glycogen availability ([§8](#aerobic-exercise)). These changes may coincide with alcohol-related inhibition of gluconeogenesis. The size and time course of their combined effect require direct study; separate mechanisms do not establish mathematical additivity or a 30–60% frequency of severe episodes. A low measured glucose value alone is also not equivalent to severe hypoglycaemia requiring external assistance.

### Quantitative characterisation

| Endpoint in Siler et al. (1998) | Reported observation | Interpretation boundary |
|---|---|---|
| Gluconeogenic flux after 48 g oral alcohol | 45% lower relative to placebo | Overnight-fasted normal men; abstract-level appraisal |
| Hepatic glucose output within the alcohol condition | 2.03 ± 0.21 to 1.79 ± 0.21 mg/kg/min, approximately 12% lower | Not a 50% suppression of total production; plasma glucose did not change |
| T1D glucose response or severe-hypoglycaemia rate | Not estimated by this experiment | No T1D multiplier, fixed lag or event frequency is assigned |

The contrast with placebo and the within-condition change are different comparisons. The original article is needed to appraise the complete tracer protocol, uncertainty estimates and time course.

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Insulin exposure persists during falling glucose.** Exogenous insulin cannot be rapidly switched off through β-cell feedback. The implications of inhibited glucose production therefore differ from those in healthy volunteers.
2. **Counterregulation varies between individuals.** Loss of the glucagon response to hypoglycaemia and impaired awareness can increase vulnerability, but neither is universal in the same form or at a fixed duration of T1D.
3. **Glucose and symptom trajectories are not interchangeable.** Intoxication can complicate recognition of altered cognition; the glucose response nevertheless requires measurement rather than inference from symptoms or from the carbohydrate content of a drink.
4. **Acute and habitual exposure answer different questions.** A short metabolic experiment cannot establish the alcohol-attributable fraction of severe events, the causes of nocturnal deaths or the effects of habitual intake on HbA1c. Such estimates require separately appraised T1D outcome studies.

### Sources

- Siler SQ, Neese RA, Christiansen MP, Hellerstein MK. (1998). "The inhibition of gluconeogenesis following alcohol in humans." *American Journal of Physiology*, 275(5):E897–E907. DOI: 10.1152/ajpendo.1998.275.5.E897. PMID: 9815011. Indexed abstract appraised; original full text not obtained.
- Steiner JL, Crowell KT, Lang CH. (2015). "Impact of alcohol on glycemic control and insulin action." *Biomolecules*, 5(4):2223–2246. [Full text](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC4693236/fullTextXML). Human, animal and isolated-tissue evidence are distinguished in the review.

<a name="psychological-stress"></a>
## 20. Psychological stress

Psychological stress -- examinations, work conflict, anticipatory anxiety, public speaking, bereavement -- engages the same neuroendocrine effectors that defend against hypoglycaemia (sympathoadrenal medulla, hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis), and an effect on circulating glucose is therefore mechanistically anticipated in type 1 diabetes (T1D), where exogenous insulin cannot dynamically compensate. The clinical reality is more nuanced than the popular shorthand "stress raises blood glucose": ambulatory studies in T1D consistently show heterogeneous, individual-specific glycaemic reactivity, with positive responders (BG rises with hassles) outnumbering negative responders but a large fraction showing no detectable association ([Riazi et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15536020/); [Gonder-Frederick et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2226381/)). The chronic-stress and behavioural pathways -- diabetes distress, depression, sleep disruption, missed boluses, comfort eating -- are at least as relevant for HbA1c trajectories as direct hormonal effects, and they interact bidirectionally with glycaemic control ([Snoek, Bremmer & Hermanns, 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25995123/)).

### Acute stress biology

#### Sympathoadrenal axis

A salient psychogenic stimulus activates the locus coeruleus and rostral ventrolateral medulla, driving preganglionic sympathetic outflow to the adrenal medulla and to peripheral postganglionic terminals. Within seconds to minutes, plasma adrenaline (epinephrine) rises 5- to 10-fold above resting (~30-50 pg/mL → 200-500 pg/mL during a moderately intense laboratory stressor) and noradrenaline (norepinephrine) rises 2- to 4-fold ([Goldstein, 2010 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19698447/)). Adrenaline binds β2-adrenoceptors on hepatocytes (activating glycogen phosphorylase via cAMP/PKA) and α-adrenoceptors on pancreatic islets, simultaneously stimulating hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis and -- in non-diabetic subjects -- suppressing insulin secretion. In skeletal muscle, β2 stimulation acutely activates glycogen phosphorylase locally but reduces insulin-mediated glucose uptake. The integrated effect in healthy subjects is a transient 1-2 mmol/L rise in plasma glucose blunted within 30-60 min by a counter-regulatory insulin response (see §11). In T1D the brake on glucose efflux is missing, so the same catecholamine surge produces a larger and more sustained glycaemic excursion.

#### HPA axis

Within the same minutes-to-hours window, hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus neurons release corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH); CRH stimulates anterior-pituitary corticotrophs to secrete adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH); ACTH drives adrenal-cortical synthesis of cortisol. Salivary or plasma cortisol typically rises 30-50% above baseline 20-40 min after a standardised psychosocial stressor, with the Trier Social Stress Test as the canonical paradigm ([Kirschbaum, Pirke & Hellhammer, 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8255414/); [Dickerson & Kemeny, 2004 -- meta-analysis](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15122924/)). Cortisol acts genomically through the glucocorticoid receptor at glucocorticoid-response elements in the *PCK1* and *G6PC* promoters (PEPCK and glucose-6-phosphatase induction; cf. §2), increasing hepatic gluconeogenic capacity over 30-120 min, and post-receptor in muscle and adipose tissue to attenuate insulin signalling. The cortisol contribution to a single stress episode is therefore delayed relative to catecholamines but more durable.

### Acute glycaemic effects in T1D

Wiesli et al. (2005) administered the Trier Social Stress Test to 20 T1D patients in two states. In the **fasting state**, neither plasma glucose nor counter-regulatory hormones changed significantly during stress despite robust cardiovascular activation (heart rate 80 → 99 bpm, systolic blood pressure 122 → 152 mmHg). In the **postprandial state** (test administered after a standardised meal and matched insulin bolus), the same stressor produced a clinically meaningful 45-min delay in postprandial glucose decline, with a cumulative incremental AUC ~30% higher than the non-stress condition ([Wiesli et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16043731/)). The differential is consistent with adrenaline's known suppression of insulin-mediated glucose disposal acting on top of an already-elevated nutrient load. The same mechanism is implicated in stress-related delayed gastric emptying and in transient β-adrenergic insulin resistance ([Goldstein, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19698447/)).

Earlier laboratory work ([Gonder-Frederick et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2226381/); [Halford, Cuddihy & Mortimer, 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2226382/)) found inconsistent direct stress-glucose effects in basal conditions, mirroring the fasting arm of Wiesli. The empirical pattern most defensible from these studies is: in T1D, acute psychological stress reliably perturbs glycaemia mainly when it coincides with active nutrient absorption or active insulin action, where the catecholamine-mediated reduction in tissue insulin sensitivity has a substrate to act on.

### Variable directionality -- the autonomic-profile caveat

The cliché "stress raises BG" is wrong often enough in T1D that it should not be relied on for self-management heuristics. Riazi, Pickup and Bradley (2004) followed 54 T1D adults over 21 days with daily hassle reports and four-times-daily capillary glucose. Within-subject stress-glucose correlations spanned r = -0.79 to +0.58, with about 20% of participants showing a positive same-day association (BG rises with hassles), about 4% a negative association, and the majority no detectable signal. "Stress-reactive" individuals (significant within-person correlation in either direction) had significantly higher HbA1c (P < 0.05) than non-reactive participants ([Riazi et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15536020/)). Direction and magnitude of stress-reactivity track autonomic phenotype ("fight" vs "freeze" responder profiles), coping style, and likely the hormonal balance between catecholamines (hyperglycaemic in T1D) and stress-induced behavioural changes -- reduced food intake, restlessness, increased physical activity -- some of which lower BG. The clinical implication is that stress-glycaemia must be characterised individually before any management response is justified.

A separate confound is **reverse causality**: hyperglycaemia itself induces irritability, fatigue, cognitive slowing, and low mood, while hypoglycaemia produces anxiety and dysphoria ([Sommerfield et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15226212/)). Cross-sectional studies that report a BG-mood correlation cannot distinguish the direction of effect, and longitudinal CGM-mood studies that adjust for activity, meals and time-of-day generally find smaller stress-glycaemia coefficients than naive analyses suggest.

### Chronic stress and T1D

#### Hormonal pathway

Sustained activation of the HPA axis -- weeks to months of work overload, caregiving burden, chronic illness, financial strain -- produces moderately elevated daytime cortisol, flattened diurnal rhythm, and (in some phenotypes) eventual hypocortisolism. Chronically elevated cortisol increases hepatic gluconeogenic capacity and post-receptor insulin resistance (see §2 and §11), shifts visceral adipose distribution, and elevates fasting glucose and HbA1c. In T1D the dominant glucose-lowering signal remains exogenous insulin, so the practical effect of chronic cortisol elevation is an upward drift in basal-insulin and total-daily-insulin requirements during prolonged stress periods ([Surwit, Schneider & Feinglos, 1992 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1425110/)).

#### Behavioural pathway

The behavioural arm is empirically larger than the direct hormonal arm. Chronic stress disrupts sleep (see §18), erodes adherence to bolus timing and dose calculation, increases reliance on convenience foods with poor postprandial profiles, and reduces planned physical activity. In adolescents and emerging adults the loss of structure is particularly costly: missed boluses are the single largest modifiable contributor to elevated HbA1c in this group ([Hagger et al., 2016 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26748793/)).

### Diabetes distress and burnout

**Diabetes distress** describes the affective burden specifically arising from living with diabetes -- the unrelenting demands of carbohydrate counting, dosing decisions, fear of hypoglycaemia, fear of long-term complications, perceived clinician unsupportiveness, and family or interpersonal frictions. The 17-item Diabetes Distress Scale (DDS-17) operationalises four subscales: emotional burden, physician-related distress, regimen-related distress, and interpersonal distress ([Polonsky et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15735199/)). In adult T1D cohorts, ~40% report moderate diabetes distress (DDS-17 mean ≥2) and ~20% report high distress (≥3) at any given time, with substantial within-person variation across the disease course ([Fisher et al., 2016 -- T1D longitudinal study](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27118163/)). Distress is statistically and conceptually distinct from clinical depression: in the 3D study, 84% of patients with moderate-to-high diabetes distress did not meet criteria for major depressive disorder, and 67% of those with major depressive disorder also had diabetes distress ([Fisher, Gonzalez & Polonsky, 2014 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24606397/)).

Diabetes distress correlates with HbA1c at small-to-moderate effect sizes (r ≈ 0.2-0.3 cross-sectionally) and longitudinally predicts HbA1c trajectory more reliably than depression scores do, even at sub-clinical distress levels ([Fisher et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19837786/)). "Diabetes burnout" -- a clinical descriptor partially overlapping high distress, characterised by detachment, exhaustion, and disengagement from self-care -- is increasingly recognised but lacks a fully validated separate instrument; current measurement leans on the high end of the DDS-17 and qualitative criteria ([Hilliard et al., 2016 -- T1D burnout review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27086134/)).

### Depression and the bidirectional T1D-depression link

Adults with diabetes have approximately twice the odds of clinical depression compared with non-diabetic adults (OR ≈ 2.0, 95% CI 1.8-2.2; Anderson et al. 2001 meta-analysis of 42 studies, mixed T1D and T2D; [PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11375373/)); estimates restricted to T1D suggest a 2- to 3-fold elevation versus background population, though prevalence figures vary widely with the assessment instrument used ([Roy & Lloyd, 2012 -- T1D depression review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22858441/)). A persistent measurement caveat is that several Diagnostic and Statistical Manual depression items (fatigue, weight change, sleep disturbance, concentration deficit) overlap with symptoms of unrecognised hyperglycaemia or hypoglycaemia, leading to over-diagnosis when self-report instruments are used without glycaemic context ([Snoek, Bremmer & Hermanns, 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25995123/)).

The depression-glycaemia link is bidirectional. Depression predicts subsequent worsening of glycaemic control via reduced self-care, missed appointments, and altered eating and activity patterns; glycaemic deterioration in turn predicts subsequent worsening of depression scores via direct mood effects of dysglycaemia and via the demoralising experience of perceived treatment failure. Severe hypoglycaemic episodes are independently associated with subsequent depressive symptoms and with fear-of-hypoglycaemia anxiety that constrains insulin dosing.

### CGM-era observational data: small effect sizes after adjustment

Continuous glucose monitoring combined with ecological momentary assessment of mood and stress has refined the older cross-sectional picture. When meals, insulin doses, physical activity and time-of-day are adjusted for, the residual within-person association between perceived stress and CGM-derived glucose is small (typical effect size corresponding to a ~0.2-0.5 mmol/L shift per standard-deviation increase in same-hour stress), and is not consistently positive across individuals. The take-away is that population-level "stress raises BG" coefficients in T1D are real but quantitatively modest once the confounders that travel with stress (skipped meals, comfort eating, missed boluses, sedentary behaviour) are accounted for. Most of the clinical impact of stress on T1D glycaemia operates through these behavioural channels rather than through unmediated hormonal flux.

### Interventions

Behavioural interventions targeting the cognitive-emotional layer of T1D self-management have small-to-moderate effects on HbA1c and larger effects on distress and quality-of-life outcomes. Cognitive-behavioural therapy adapted for diabetes reduces distress and depressive symptoms (mean reduction ~0.4-0.6 SD) and produces small HbA1c reductions (~0.3 percentage points) sustained at 6-12 months ([Uchendu & Blake, 2017 -- meta-analysis](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27933671/)). Mindfulness-based interventions reduce salivary cortisol output and perceived stress at moderate effect size, with small HbA1c effects in mixed T1D/T2D samples; T1D-specific evidence is sparse and is summarised under [§10b -- Relaxation, yoga, and meditation](#relaxation-yoga). Structured problem-solving and motivational-interviewing protocols, peer-support and group-based approaches, and integrated behavioural-medicine co-management within diabetes clinics are increasingly recommended in international guidelines.

<a name="temperature-climate"></a>
## 21. Temperature and climate

Ambient and tissue temperature is one of the most clinically conspicuous yet under-quantified modifiers of subcutaneous (SC) insulin pharmacokinetics in T1D. Patients routinely report patterns that are reproducible across decades of physiology: a hot shower or sauna shortly after a meal bolus precipitates hypoglycemia within 30-60 min; injecting insulin into a chilled abdomen on a winter morning blunts and delays peak action; a febrile illness amplifies the unpredictability of basal-bolus dosing; and an insulin pen left on a car dashboard at 45 °C silently loses potency, presenting later as unexplained hyperglycemia or even ketoacidosis. The physiological substrate is simple - SC insulin must traverse interstitium, dissociate from hexameric/dimeric storage forms, and reach capillary beds before entering the systemic circulation - and every step is sensitive to local blood flow, tissue temperature, and the chemical stability of the depot. *Caveat: Most quantitative human evidence dates from regular human insulin and NPH (1979-1995); modern rapid analogs (lispro, aspart, glulisine, faster aspart) and ultra-long basals (degludec, glargine U300) carry smaller absolute temperature effects because their kinetics are less limited by hexamer dissociation and SC blood flow, but the directionality and clinical relevance persist ([Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/); [Pitt et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33193089/)).*

### Subcutaneous blood flow as the rate-limiting step

Healthy human skin and SC adipose tissue exhibit one of the body's widest dynamic ranges in regional perfusion. Dermal blood flow can vary roughly 10-fold between full vasoconstriction (~0.1-0.5 mL/100 g/min in cold-stressed acral skin) and full thermoregulatory vasodilation (4-7 mL/100 g/min during heat stress, with peaks of >150 mL/100 g/min in glabrous skin via arteriovenous anastomoses). SC adipose perfusion has a narrower but still meaningful range (1-10 mL/100 g/min). Because the absorption half-time of soluble (regular) insulin in steady-state conditions is ~50-90 min and is dominated by transport from interstitium to capillary, even modest perfusion changes translate directly into changes in plasma insulin onset, peak, and duration ([Berger et al., 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/488572/); [Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/)). Rapid analogs are less perfusion-limited because their dissociation from hexameric form is faster, but at the population level absorption variability remains 15-25 % CV (coefficient of variation, intra-individual variability %) intra-individually and >50 % inter-individually for any given SC insulin in any given site - and acute thermal perturbations can exceed that baseline noise.

Cold exposure adds two further mechanisms beyond vasoconstriction. First, local tissue temperature directly slows insulin diffusion and dissociation kinetics (a typical Arrhenius effect of ~2-3 % per °C over the physiological range). Second, shivering thermogenesis recruits skeletal-muscle glucose uptake (insulin-independent but additive with circulating insulin) and elevates catecholamines, producing a paradoxical mixed glycemic response in which a cold-induced absorption delay can be partly offset by enhanced peripheral glucose disposal once the insulin finally arrives.

### Heat → vasodilation → faster absorption: the sauna paradigm

The classical demonstration is [Koivisto's 1980 sauna study](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7000239/) (BMJ 280:1411-1413), in which ten insulin-dependent patients received their morning soluble (regular) insulin and were then exposed to two 25-min sessions in a Finnish sauna at 85 °C dry heat. The mean insulin disappearance rate from the SC depot rose by ~110 % vs. the room-temperature control day (i.e., approximately 2-fold faster, p<0.01), and plasma glucose 2-3 h after injection was 3.0-3.3 mmol/L (54-60 mg/dL) lower on the sauna day (p<0.05). The fall in glucose was tightly proportional to the increase in absorption, implicating a pharmacokinetic - not pharmacodynamic - mechanism. A confirmatory chamber study at moderate heat ([Koivisto et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7010077/), Metabolism 30:402-405) found that simply raising ambient temperature from 20 °C to 35 °C accelerated SC insulin disappearance by 50-60 % over 4 h, with plasma glucose 1-2 mmol/L lower at the warmer temperature - a dose-response relationship between skin/SC temperature and absorption rate. Local heating without systemic heat stress (e.g., warm packs over the injection site, warm-water immersion of the limb) reproduces the effect, and modern infusion-set studies confirm a ~30-60 % acceleration of basal insulin delivery from CSII (continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion, i.e., insulin pump) pumps with skin warming. Hot showers and warm baths within 30-60 min of a bolus are therefore a real, reproducible hypoglycemia trigger.

### Cold injection and reduced ambient temperature

The cold side of the curve is less well-quantified but consistent in direction. Reducing skin temperature from 32 °C to ~22 °C decreases SC insulin disappearance by ~30-50 % in human and porcine models ([Berger et al., 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/488572/); reviewed by [Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/)), prolongs time-to-peak, and lowers peak plasma concentration with a corresponding loss of postprandial control. A second practical phenomenon is "cold-pen sting": insulin from a refrigerator-fresh pen (4-8 °C) is biologically intact but the cold bolus locally vasoconstricts the depot, transiently delays absorption, and is more painful at injection. Letting pens reach room temperature before use (~20 min) eliminates this effect. In outdoor winter exposure, peripheral skin in the abdomen or arm can drop to 18-25 °C even under clothing during prolonged cold; switching the rotation from a chilled limb to a warmer covered site (e.g., abdomen vs. exposed thigh) is the simplest mitigation.

### Fever as endogenous heat - amplifying §17 illness risk

Febrile illness combines two independent BG perturbations. The illness itself drives counterregulatory hormones, ketogenesis, and insulin resistance (see §17). Independently, the rise in core and SC temperature accelerates SC insulin absorption: a 1-2 °C fever produces a measurable (~10-25 %) increase in disappearance rate, narrowing the time window between insulin onset and peak. The clinical consequence is that the same dose-correction algorithm that worked at baseline can now both peak earlier (raising hypo risk in the 1-2 h post-bolus window) and run out faster (raising hyper risk later). This is one mechanism by which sick-day glycemia becomes "all-or-nothing": small dose changes are amplified through faster, sharper kinetics on top of an already hormonally perturbed background.

### Insulin product thermal stability and storage

Insulin is a small protein (5.8 kDa) stabilized by hexameric Zn²⁺ coordination and a small number of disulfide bonds, and its in-vial chemistry is sensitive to both heat and freeze damage. Manufacturer labeling for unopened cartridges and vials is 2-8 °C until the printed expiry; once in use, all major rapid analogs (lispro, aspart, glulisine, faster aspart) are labeled stable for ≤28 days (some up to 42 days for specific products) at room temperature ≤30 °C. Above ~30-32 °C, both potency and chemical purity deteriorate progressively. The Vimalavathini-Gitanjali human-insulin study ([Indian J Med Res, 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19797814/), 130:166-169) showed a 14-18 % loss of potency by HPLC after 28 days at 32-37 °C across three formulations. The 2023 Cochrane systematic review ([Richter et al., Cochrane Database Syst Rev, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37930742/), CD015385) consolidated manufacturer and independent data and concluded that unopened vials and cartridges retain clinically acceptable potency for up to 6 months at 25 °C and up to 2 months at 37 °C, with oscillation between 25 °C and 37 °C tolerated for up to 3 months - findings highly relevant to storage in low-resource and tropical settings (e.g., the unglazed-clay-pot work). Freezing irreversibly disrupts hexameric structure: a single freeze-thaw cycle can produce visible turbidity and substantial activity loss, and any insulin that has frozen must be discarded. The functional clinical signature of degraded insulin is unpredictable, often blunted glycemic response despite apparently correct dosing - a pattern that should prompt a fresh vial/pen before a major dose escalation.

### Exercise-induced muscle warmth and absorption from active limbs

Local hyperthermia is also an automatic byproduct of exercise. Working muscle generates heat at 5-15 W/kg, raising muscle and overlying SC temperature by 1-3 °C within 20-30 min and increasing local SC blood flow several-fold. The classic injection-site work from [Koivisto and Felig, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6444267/) (Ann Intern Med 92:59-61) showed that under resting conditions, abdominal absorption of regular insulin was ~86 % faster than thigh and ~30 % faster than arm; running converts the resting-thigh depot into the body's fastest depot, and arm exercise does the same for an arm depot. Reported absorption acceleration from exercise of the limb containing the depot is in the range of ~50-100 % above the resting rate for soluble insulins, with a smaller but still significant effect (~20-50 %) for rapid analogs. This is one component of the widely observed hypoglycemia risk in the 60-90 min after a meal bolus in patients who exercise the injected limb (see §10).

### Hot weather, sweating, and dehydration

Beyond the depot, sustained hot-weather exposure drives behavioral and circulatory changes that distort BG. Heat-induced sweat losses of 0.5-1.5 L/h reduce plasma volume; the resulting hemoconcentration produces an apparent rise in measured glucose for a fixed glucose mass and concentrates ketones if insulin is also under-delivered. Heat exposure also reduces appetite, alters meal timing, and increases the appeal of sugar-containing rehydration fluids - all hard for fixed insulin algorithms to track. People with T1D have measurably impaired thermoregulation: lower skin blood flow and sweating responses during heat exposure, especially with longer disease duration, autonomic neuropathy, or poor glycemic control ([Kenny et al., Temperature, 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4861190/)). The combination raises both heat-illness risk (impaired heat dissipation) and BG instability (faster SC absorption + dehydration concentration effect + behavioral disruption).

### Climate adaptation - tropical and arctic acclimatization

Chronic acclimatization to heat (≥10-14 days) increases plasma volume by ~10-15 %, lowers the sweating threshold, and improves cardiovascular tolerance, partly normalizing the acute heat response. Cold acclimatization is more modest in humans but includes reduced shivering threshold and improved peripheral vasoconstrictor tone. Long-stay travelers and migrants typically require an iterative basal/ICR/ISF re-tune over 1-3 weeks rather than a single day-1 adjustment; the evidence base is largely observational and case-series.

### CGM and pump hardware in extreme climates

Sensor and infusion hardware add a further layer of climate sensitivity. Common CGM platforms (Dexcom G6/G7, Libre 2/3) carry an operating range of approximately 10-42 °C; below ~10 °C the electrochemistry of the sensor wire slows and reported glucose lags or reads low, and above ~42 °C drift and signal loss become common. Adhesive failure is the dominant practical mode of failure in heat: sweat and SC oil reduce hold time from 7-14 days to 2-4 days, and overpatching, antiperspirant skin-prep, or cyanoacrylate-style adhesives are necessary for endurance, tropical, or military use. Insulin pump reservoirs are subject to the same thermal stability constraints as vials and pens; in addition, insulin in tubing exposed to direct sunlight on a beach can exceed 40 °C within minutes, with both potency loss and air-bubble formation that disrupts delivery. Cold has the inverse profile: sub-zero exposure can freeze the insulin in pump tubing and sensor connectors, and operating below ~5 °C is outside specification.

<a name="injection-site"></a>
## 22. Injection site, technique, and lipohypertrophy

The anatomical location and depth of injection, insulin formulation, and condition of the receiving tissue all influence subcutaneous insulin absorption. Regional comparisons, inadvertent intramuscular delivery and lipohypertrophy address different sources of variability and should not be summarised as a universal day-to-day percentage change. Evidence from regular human insulin also requires qualification before extrapolation to modern analogues.

### Subcutaneous depot anatomy

The intended target tissue is the subcutaneous adipose layer beneath the dermis but superficial to the deep fascia and skeletal muscle. Skin thickness in adults is remarkably constant across body regions and across BMI -- approximately 1.9--2.4 mm at the abdomen, thigh, arm, and buttock -- whereas overlying subcutaneous fat thickness varies dramatically with sex, BMI, and anatomical region (typically 5--35 mm at the abdomen but as little as 3--8 mm at the thigh in lean adults and children) ([Gibney et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20602605/)). Once delivered, insulin forms a roughly spherical depot at the needle tip; absorption proceeds by diffusion of monomer/dimer species across the capillary endothelium of the surrounding subcutaneous microvasculature, with absorption affected by depot geometry, insulin association or formulation-specific release, diffusion and local microvascular transport ([Hildebrandt, 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1914533/); see also [§7](#insulin-pharmacology) for hexamer dissociation kinetics and [§21](#temperature-climate) for thermal modulation of regional blood flow).

### Site comparison: abdomen, arm, thigh, buttock

Classic comparative pharmacokinetic studies, predominantly performed with regular human insulin, demonstrated a clear hierarchy: abdomen > upper arm > thigh ≈ buttock for both rate of absorption and peak concentration. With regular human insulin, the difference in time-to-peak between abdomen and thigh was on the order of 30--60 min, and the abdominal site cleared the depot 86% faster than the thigh and 30% faster than the arm ([Koivisto and Felig, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6444267/); [Bantle et al., 1993](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/16/12/1592/17362/)). Vora and colleagues quantified the underlying mechanism using simultaneous measurement of ¹²⁵I-soluble-insulin absorption and laser-Doppler subcutaneous blood flow: blood-flow rates were highest at the abdominal wall, intermediate at the upper arm, and lowest at the anterior thigh, and the rank order of absorption rate matched the rank order of blood flow ([Vora et al., 1992](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/15/11/1484/21271/)).

With rapid-acting analogs (lispro, aspart, glulisine), regional differences are smaller but still measurable. In a 6-way crossover comparison of abdominal, deltoid, and femoral injection of insulin lispro and regular insulin, abdominal administration produced the highest C_max at the earliest t_max for both formulations; for lispro the t_max difference between abdomen and thigh was approximately 10--20 min, vs. ~45 min for regular insulin in the same study ([Ter Braak et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8941480/)). The flatter absorption curves of analogs partly reflect their faster monomerization, which makes the rate of absorption less dependent on local blood flow. Long-acting analogs (glargine, detemir, degludec) show minimal regional dependence in registration studies, consistent with their depot stability being driven by formulation-specific microprecipitation or albumin binding rather than vascular kinetics. *Caveat:* a substantial fraction of widely-cited site-comparison data was generated with regular human insulin, which is now rarely used for prandial dosing; readers should not extrapolate the magnitude of "abdomen vs. thigh" effects from 1980s literature directly to modern analog therapy. These regional comparisons do not establish one optimal prandial or basal injection region across formulations; clinical site guidance requires the preparation and technique to be specified.

### Inadvertent intramuscular injection

Because subcutaneous fat thickness is highly variable but skin thickness is not, needles longer than the local subcutis depth deliver insulin into skeletal muscle. Intramuscular insulin is absorbed substantially faster than subcutaneous insulin (NPH T_50% 5.3 h IM vs. 10.3 h SC, with day-to-day coefficient of variation rising from 18% to 30%) and is associated with hypoglycemia, particularly when followed by exercise of the injected limb ([Vora et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2404719/); [Frid et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2190773/)). Ultrasound studies confirm that with an 8 mm needle inserted at 90° without a skin fold, IM injection rates are ~15--20% in lean adult men and rise to ~30--40% in lean children, especially in the thigh and male deltoid; with a 12.7 mm needle the IM rate in lean children reaches 86% ([Tubiana-Rufi et al., 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10526725/)). The 4--5 mm pen needle, inserted at 90° without a skin fold in adults, reduces IM injection rates to ≤2% across BMI strata while maintaining backflow rates equivalent to longer needles ([Hofman et al., 2010](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/33/9/1940/27008/); [Birkebaek et al., 2008](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/31/9/e65/28003/)). The cited 2016 Forum for Injection Technique and Therapy Expert Recommendations (FITTER) endorse 4 mm pen needles as first-line for nearly all patients regardless of age or BMI ([Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/)). *Clinical-technique boundary:* needle length, skinfold and angle jointly determine intramuscular or intradermal risk. A specific 45° recommendation for a 4-mm needle is not retained because the original guidance was not independently available for this appraisal. Technique should be checked against the original clinical guidance and device specification, not inferred from subcutis thickness alone.

### Lipohypertrophy: biology

Repeated injection of insulin into the same focal area drives both adipocyte hypertrophy and adipocyte hyperplasia mediated by the lipogenic, anti-lipolytic, and insulin-like growth factor 1 receptor cross-reactivity of insulin signalling, compounded by a low-grade fibrotic response to mechanical needle trauma ([Barola et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6218430/)). The resulting lipohypertrophic (LH) lesion is clinically detectable as a thickened, rubbery, often hypoesthetic subcutaneous nodule -- frequently more conspicuous on palpation than on inspection, and consistently underdiagnosed when palpation is omitted from routine review. Ultrasound visualizes LH as hyperechoic dermal-subcutaneous thickening with disorganized fat-lobule architecture and reduced microvascular density. Because LH tissue is hypoesthetic and absorbs unpredictably (i.e., often less painfully but less effectively), patients preferentially re-inject into established lesions, perpetuating a self-reinforcing cycle.

### Lipohypertrophy and absorption variability

The prevalence of lipohypertrophy varies with population and ascertainment, including clinical examination versus ultrasound ([Deng et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5934253/); [Blanco et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23886784/)). This observational evidence is distinct from controlled studies of absorption.

In Famulla and colleagues' crossover study, 13 people with T1D received 0.15 U/kg lispro into lipohypertrophic and normal abdominal tissue. Four-hour insulin exposure was 131 versus 165 h·mU/L and peak concentration 61 versus 79 mU/L, approximately 21% and 23% lower in lipohypertrophic tissue. Coefficients of variation were 52% versus 11% for exposure and 55% versus 15% for peak concentration. Four-hour glucose-infusion-rate AUC was 625 versus 775 mg/kg, a distinct pharmacodynamic endpoint. Five-hour post-meal glucose AUC was 731 versus 513 mg·h/dL, approximately 43% higher; the smaller 26% contrast concerned peak glucose. Insulin Tmax did not differ. These results were verified against the indexed abstract; full-text appraisal remains pending ([Famulla et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27411698/)). Observational associations with unexplained hypoglycaemia and higher insulin requirements should not be interpreted as identical to this acute crossover comparison.

### Rotation strategy

Systematic within-region rotation is intended to reduce repeated injury and local overuse while limiting arbitrary changes between anatomical regions with different absorption characteristics. Blanco and colleagues' cross-sectional findings associate incorrect rotation with lipohypertrophy; they do not demonstrate prospective incidence. The reported 5% of correct rotators with lipohypertrophy and 98% of people with lipohypertrophy rotating incorrectly have different denominators and are not a risk ratio ([Blanco et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23886784/)). Precise spacing and region schedules belong to clinical-technique guidance and should be checked in the original consensus rather than inferred from that association ([Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/), full-text appraisal pending).

### Pump cannula sites

Continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion adds an indwelling cannula and possible inflammation, leakage, occlusion or tissue injury to the determinants of insulin delivery. Mechanical set failure and changes in absorption kinetics are different phenomena. The cited material here does not establish a uniform progressive 10–30% reduction in insulin AUC by day 3; a numerical wear effect requires the insulin, device, wear protocol and measured endpoint to be specified. Repeated use of the same region may also produce lipohypertrophy, as discussed above.

### Other modulators

Massage of the injection site, exercise of the underlying muscle group (e.g., running after a thigh injection), and elevated ambient or local skin temperature each accelerate absorption via increased local blood flow (cross-link [§21](#temperature-climate) for thermal effects); cold skin and dehydration slow it. Insulin formulations vary in injection-site pain, which patients may misattribute to "bad sites" -- low-pH formulations (historically glargine U100, pH 4) cause more transient stinging than neutral-pH analogs.

### Modern guidance and controversies

The 2010 FIT and 2016 FITTER statements are important historical sources for injection technique ([Frid et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20933208/); [Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/)). This targeted appraisal is not a comprehensive update of clinical guidance. Comparative evidence is still needed for particular rotation patterns, the time course of lesion regression, routine ultrasound screening and device-specific wear intervals. The inherited universal 6–12-month regression and 48-hour variability claims are not retained without directly applicable original evidence.

# Part 7: Complications and Warning Signs

<a name="ketones-dka"></a>
## 23. Ketone bodies and diabetic ketoacidosis

Ketogenesis (mitochondrial production of ketone bodies from acetyl-CoA when beta-oxidation flux exceeds the capacity of the tricarboxylic acid cycle to oxidize acetyl-CoA) is a conserved mammalian survival mechanism that provides an oxidizable fuel for the brain and periphery during carbohydrate scarcity. Under physiological conditions, circulating beta-hydroxybutyrate (beta-OHB; the predominant blood ketone body) rarely exceeds 0.3 mmol/L in the fed state and rises to 1--3 mmol/L during prolonged fasting. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), absolute or near-absolute insulin deficiency abolishes the principal brake on lipolysis and hepatic ketogenesis, enabling an unregulated cascade that can raise beta-OHB to 3--25 mmol/L, depress blood pH below 7.10, and produce a life-threatening syndrome -- diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) -- that remains the leading cause of diabetes-related death in children and young adults ([Maahs et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26180108/); [Dhatariya, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32546826/)). Approximately 20--30% of new-onset T1D in children presents as DKA at diagnosis, and recurrent DKA affects roughly 10--15% of those with established disease ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)).

### Ketogenesis biochemistry

Three chemically distinct ketone bodies are produced in hepatic mitochondria. Acetoacetate (AcAc; the primary synthetic product) is reduced to beta-OHB by beta-hydroxybutyrate dehydrogenase in a reaction coupled to the NADH/NAD+ ratio; at the high NADH:NAD+ ratio prevailing during heavy fatty acid oxidation this equilibrium strongly favors beta-OHB, explaining why beta-OHB constitutes approximately 75--80% of total circulating ketones in early DKA and the plasma beta-OHB:AcAc ratio rises from the physiological ~1:1 to approximately 3:1 (and up to 7:1 in severe DKA) ([Laffel, 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10634967/)). AcAc spontaneously decarboxylates to acetone -- the volatile ketone responsible for the characteristic fruity breath odour of DKA -- but acetone is metabolically inert and contributes negligibly to acid load.

The rate-limiting step in hepatic ketogenesis is carnitine palmitoyltransferase-1 (CPT-1; the mitochondrial outer-membrane enzyme that transfers long-chain acyl groups onto carnitine for import into the mitochondrial matrix). CPT-1 is allosterically inhibited by malonyl-CoA, the first committed intermediate of de novo fatty acid synthesis. In the fed, insulin-replete state, high malonyl-CoA maintains CPT-1 in a largely inhibited state, limiting beta-oxidation and confining most incoming acetyl-CoA to the TCA cycle. During insulin deficiency, AMPK-mediated phosphoinositide signalling suppresses acetyl-CoA carboxylase activity, malonyl-CoA falls, and CPT-1 is disinhibited -- a permissive gate through which the massively elevated free fatty acid (FFA) flux generated by adipose lipolysis can flood the mitochondrial matrix ([McGarry & Foster, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6998753/)). Excess acetyl-CoA that cannot be oxidised via the TCA cycle condenses two molecules to form HMG-CoA (3-hydroxy-3-methylglutaryl-CoA, central intermediate in the HMG-CoA pathway) via HMG-CoA synthase; HMG-CoA lyase then cleaves HMG-CoA to yield AcAc and acetyl-CoA, completing the ketogenic cycle.

Glucogenic amino acid catabolism during insulin deficiency simultaneously consumes hepatic oxaloacetate (OAA) via gluconeogenesis, depleting the TCA-cycle intermediate required to condense with acetyl-CoA to form citrate. This OAA deficiency further diverts acetyl-CoA toward HMG-CoA and AcAc, making gluconeogenesis and ketogenesis mutually reinforcing parallel processes -- a biochemical feature that accelerates the DKA cascade once it is established.

### Hepatic fatty acid sources for ketogenesis: lipolysis, chylomicron remnants, de novo lipogenesis, and medium-chain triglycerides

Adipose-derived nonesterified fatty acids and dietary lipid can both contribute substrate to hepatic fatty-acid oxidation. Their routes should be distinguished from the eventual partitioning of hepatic fatty acids between oxidation, storage and export.

**Adipose lipolysis.** Insulin suppresses the release of nonesterified fatty acids from adipose tissue. During reduced insulin availability, increased fatty-acid delivery can support hepatic oxidation and ketogenesis. A concentration–response estimate for suppression of circulating fatty acids is not, however, a measured fraction of ketogenic substrate derived from adipose tissue.

**Dietary lipid.** Long-chain dietary triglycerides are transported in chylomicrons. Fatty acids reach the liver through uptake of chylomicron remnants and through spillover after lipoprotein-lipase-mediated hydrolysis. These are distinct routes, and their contributions depend on feeding, tissue uptake and lipoprotein clearance. Percentages of liver triglyceride derived from dietary or circulating fatty acids are not interchangeable with percentages of acetyl-CoA entering ketogenesis. In particular, the NAFLD liver-triglyceride measurements cited by Donnelly et al. (2005) cannot supply a source partition for T1D ketogenesis.

**De novo lipogenesis and medium-chain fatty acids.** Newly synthesized hepatic fatty acids provide another possible source, whereas medium-chain fatty acids have transport and oxidation kinetics that differ from long-chain dietary lipid. A post-ingestion rise in circulating β-hydroxybutyrate reflects both production and disposal; it does not alone identify a maximal hepatic production flux or a universal proportionality between dietary fat intake and ketogenesis.

**Quantitative limitation.** No numerical partition between adipose lipolysis, dietary lipid and de novo lipogenesis is assigned here in the absence of direct tracer measurements in the specified T1D state. Such measurements would need to distinguish precursor delivery, intrahepatic partitioning, ketone production and peripheral clearance. The routes remain physiologically relevant without treating liver-triglyceride composition or a plasma ketone response as a calibrated ketogenic flux.

### The hormonal trigger: insulin deficiency and glucagon excess

The primary metabolic regulator of ketogenesis is circulating free insulin, not blood glucose. Insulin suppresses adipose lipolysis with an EC50 of approximately 44--68 pmol/L (~8--11 muU/mL), far lower than the EC50 for skeletal muscle glucose uptake (~330 pmol/L) ([Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7014664/)). Consequently, even modest reductions in portal insulin (as during deliberate carbohydrate restriction) activate lipolysis proportionally, while the full pathway to DKA requires near-complete insulin deficiency (typically portal insulin <10--20 pmol/L). Glucagon excess is a necessary co-driver: glucagon activates hepatic cAMP signalling, suppresses fructose-2,6-bisphosphate (shifting hepatic metabolism toward gluconeogenesis), reduces malonyl-CoA via AMPK, and directly upregulates HMG-CoA synthase transcription. In the insulin-deficient state the normal glucagon-to-insulin ratio is inverted -- glucagon rises two- to threefold above basal while insulin is nearly absent -- producing a "double-hit" on hepatic ketogenesis ([Foster, 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6424550/)). Counter-regulatory hormones (epinephrine, cortisol, growth hormone) amplify lipolysis further and contribute to the hepatic metabolic shift, but glucagon excess is considered the dominant intrahepatic driver. *Caveat:* individuals with long-standing T1D and concurrent alpha-cell failure (rare) may have blunted glucagon responses that attenuate ketogenesis -- a clinically relevant exception in older cohorts with autonomic neuropathy.

### Physiological ketosis versus DKA

An elevated ketone concentration does not by itself distinguish fasting-associated ketosis from DKA. Insulin availability, substrate supply, ketone production and disposal, renal function and acid–base status determine the clinical state. During insulin deficiency, increased lipolysis and hepatic ketogenesis can coexist with glucose-driven osmotic diuresis and loss of water and electrolytes. Ketoacid accumulation contributes to metabolic acidosis when production exceeds disposal and excretion.

The relevant distinction is therefore ketosis without metabolic acidosis versus ketosis accompanied by the acid–base abnormalities of DKA. A glucose value in the usual range does not exclude the latter. Conversely, a ketone threshold alone does not establish DKA or demonstrate that a dietary pattern is safe in an individual with T1D ([Glaser et al., 2022](https://www.ispad.org/static/6dd62eae-c8cb-4b4a-84e1efc768505746/Ch11PediatricDiabetes.pdf); [Umpierrez et al., 2024](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11343900/fullTextXML)).

### Anion gap acidosis and electrolyte derangements

The metabolic acidosis of DKA is a high anion gap (AG) acidosis (anion gap = Na+ - [Cl- + HCO3-], elevated when unmeasured anions such as beta-OHB and AcAc accumulate in plasma). AcAc (pKa 3.58) and beta-OHB (pKa 4.70) are almost completely dissociated at physiological pH; their accumulation consumes bicarbonate and raises the AG proportionally. In moderate DKA the AG characteristically exceeds 16 mmol/L (normal 8--12), and values of 25--40 are common in severe cases. A "delta-delta" analysis (comparing the rise in AG to the fall in HCO3-) approximates the pure ketoacid contribution vs. any co-existing hyperchloraemic component that may emerge during treatment with chloride-rich fluids.

Dehydration is typically 5--10% of body weight at presentation (3--8 litres in adults), driven by osmotic diuresis from glucosuria and ketonuria plus insensible losses from the compensatory hyperventilation (Kussmaul breathing: deep, rapid respirations that provide respiratory compensation by blowing off CO2, partially raising pH but increasing water loss). Total body potassium (K+) depletion is invariably present (200--500 mmol deficit in adults) even when serum K+ is normal or elevated at presentation; the apparent normokalemia reflects extracellular K+ shift from transcellular exchange with H+ (acidosis drives K+ out of cells). Insulin therapy and pH correction rapidly return K+ to cells, precipitating potentially fatal hypokalaemia if replacement is not initiated promptly -- the most common cause of iatrogenic mortality in DKA management. Phosphate, magnesium, and sodium deficits also occur but their clinical significance is less acutely critical; phosphate replacement is not routinely recommended in most guidelines due to lack of outcome benefit ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)).

### Diagnostic criteria

DKA requires ketosis and metabolic acidosis in the relevant diabetes context. The 2024 adult consensus requires all three components:

1. Glucose ≥11.1 mmol/L or a prior history of diabetes irrespective of presenting glucose.
2. β-hydroxybutyrate ≥3.0 mmol/L or, when blood measurement is unavailable, urine ketones ≥2+.
3. Venous pH <7.3 and/or bicarbonate <18 mmol/L.

The 2022 paediatric ISPAD guideline uses glucose >11 mmol/L, venous pH <7.3 or bicarbonate <18 mmol/L, and ketonaemia or moderate/large ketonuria. Its suggested blood β-hydroxybutyrate threshold is ≥3 mmol/L. Paediatric severity classification uses the degree of acidosis: mild pH <7.3 or bicarbonate <18 mmol/L; moderate pH <7.2 or bicarbonate <10 mmol/L; severe pH <7.1 or bicarbonate <5 mmol/L. These population-specific classifications should not be combined into a single adult–child rule ([Glaser et al., 2022](https://www.ispad.org/static/6dd62eae-c8cb-4b4a-84e1efc768505746/Ch11PediatricDiabetes.pdf); [Umpierrez et al., 2024](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11343900/fullTextXML)).

Urine tests primarily detect acetoacetate rather than β-hydroxybutyrate. They can underestimate ketonaemia early and overestimate persistent ketosis during recovery as β-hydroxybutyrate is converted to acetoacetate. Direct blood ketone measurement therefore answers a different question from urine testing; neither replaces assessment of acidosis.

### Paediatric-specific considerations

Paediatric DKA requires age-appropriate fluid, electrolyte and neurological assessment. Cerebral injury is a major complication, and its pathophysiology cannot be reduced to a single osmotic mechanism or an assumed benefit from uniformly slower fluid delivery. The 2022 ISPAD guideline no longer requires clinicians to promote a rise in serum sodium during treatment. Its paediatric protocol should be consulted separately from adult recommendations, particularly when interpreting hydration, potassium and neurological findings ([Glaser et al., 2022](https://www.ispad.org/static/6dd62eae-c8cb-4b4a-84e1efc768505746/Ch11PediatricDiabetes.pdf)).

### Euglycaemic DKA

Euglycaemic DKA involves ketosis and acidosis without the marked hyperglycaemia traditionally associated with DKA. Reduced intake, partial insulin exposure and SGLT2-inhibitor use are among the relevant contexts; glucose concentration alone cannot exclude it. SGLT2 inhibition increases urinary glucose loss, which can reduce hyperglycaemia while ketone accumulation and acidosis persist.

The 2024 adult consensus describes DKA in approximately 4% of SGLT2-treated people with T1D. Its separate incidence range of 0.6–4.9 events per 1,000 patient-years concerns T2D. These are different populations and measures: the 4% estimate does not supply a person-time rate, and a T2D rate must not be relabeled as T1D risk. The consensus does not recommend SGLT2-inhibitor therapy in T1D; regulatory authorization is a separate jurisdiction-specific question ([Umpierrez et al., 2024](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11343900/fullTextXML)).

### DKA mortality and epidemiology

DKA mortality in high-income countries has declined substantially over the 20th century with improved access to insulin and critical-care medicine but has plateaued since approximately 1980. Current estimates: case fatality rate approximately 0.15--0.30% in adults in high-income settings ([Kitabchi et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19564476/)), approximately 0.15--0.30% in paediatric series (excluding CE) ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)). Global DKA mortality remains high in low- and middle-income countries due to delayed diagnosis, insulin access barriers, and limited critical-care capacity; in sub-Saharan Africa case fatality rates of 5--30% are reported. In high-income paediatric cohorts, the most common causes of DKA death are CE, hypokalaemia-related cardiac arrhythmia, and aspiration pneumonia.

### Treatment principles

Hospital treatment addresses volume and electrolyte deficits while insulin suppresses lipolysis and ketogenesis. Glucose can fall before ketoacidosis resolves, so dextrose may be needed alongside continuing insulin. Normalization of glucose alone is not the therapeutic endpoint. Fluid composition, rate, insulin delivery and monitoring depend on age, haemodynamic state, renal function and the applicable protocol; adult and paediatric instructions are not interchangeable.

Potassium replacement is a monitored component of treatment, not a fixed hourly dose independent of fluid delivery and renal function. The 2024 adult consensus states that 20–30 mmol potassium **per litre of intravenous fluid** is sufficient for most patients requiring replacement. In adults presenting with potassium <3.5 mmol/L, it recommends beginning replacement at 10 mmol/h and delaying insulin until potassium exceeds 3.5 mmol/L. These conditional adult recommendations require serial electrolyte monitoring and must not be combined with paediatric thresholds ([Umpierrez et al., 2024](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11343900/fullTextXML)).

The 2022 ISPAD guideline provides a separate paediatric protocol. The physiological rationale is to restore perfusion, stop ketone production and correct electrolyte deficits without interpreting one laboratory result as sufficient evidence of resolution ([Glaser et al., 2022](https://www.ispad.org/static/6dd62eae-c8cb-4b4a-84e1efc768505746/Ch11PediatricDiabetes.pdf)).

### Controversies and open questions

Important uncertainties concern mechanisms of cerebral injury, treatment in patients with major comorbidity, and access to timely blood ketone and electrolyte measurements. Guideline recommendations are population- and context-specific; historical thresholds should not be presented as current universal rules. Likewise, an exogenous ketone experiment does not directly identify ketone production or clearance during insulin-deficient DKA.

### Ketone body kinetics and clearance

Plasma ketone concentration reflects production, tissue use, renal handling and distribution. Ketone disappearance after an exogenous dose and the resolution of endogenous ketoacidosis are different experiments; a fitted half-life or compartment model from the former cannot be transferred directly to DKA.

The 2024 adult consensus defines DKA resolution by plasma ketones <0.6 mmol/L together with venous pH ≥7.3 or bicarbonate ≥18 mmol/L; glucose should ideally also be <11.1 mmol/L. It advises against using the anion gap or urine ketones as resolution criteria. Hyperchloraemic acidosis can persist after ketone clearance, while conversion of β-hydroxybutyrate to acetoacetate can sustain a positive urine test during recovery. Neither observation establishes a fixed delay in renal clearance from an interstitial compartment ([Umpierrez et al., 2024](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11343900/fullTextXML)).

Note: as of 2026-06-06 the CPT-1 gate is fed by a combined hepatic FFA pool — adipose-lipolysis-derived FFA plus a weighted fraction of the dietary (chylomicron-remnant + LPL-spillover) pool — so the dietary-fat contribution to ketogenesis discussed above is now represented in lumped form. The chain-length-specific medium-chain triglyceride (MCT) pathway, which bypasses CPT-1 entirely, remains unmodeled and is the residual documented gap relative to the reference physiology.
