<a name="insulin-overdose"></a>
## 24. Insulin overdose and the limitations of counterregulation

Insulin overdose can cause prolonged hypoglycaemia when exogenous insulin action exceeds the available glucose supply. Clinical severity depends on the exposure, formulation and absorption profile, as well as glucose availability, counterregulatory responses and time to treatment. A particular glucose concentration or reported injected dose does not by itself predict outcome. Counterregulatory impairment in T1D is important, but loss of glucagon responses should not be assigned to a universal disease-duration threshold ([§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)).

### Pharmacokinetics of overdose: prolonged and heterogeneous exposure

A large subcutaneous exposure can prolong insulin absorption, but this does not establish a universal switch to zero-order kinetics above a particular dose. Formulation, depot characteristics and the interval sampled affect the observed concentration profile.

In the kinetic substudy of Mégarbane et al. (2007), six patients were studied: two with T1D, one with T2D and three without diabetes. Their terminal insulin decline was described by first-order kinetics, with a median terminal half-life of 3.8 hours (interquartile range 1.5–4.6). A terminal decline describes the observed phase of a concentration curve, not every process of absorption and clearance. This small, heterogeneous sample cannot define a universal overdose absorption law or the pharmacokinetics of usual therapeutic doses.

### Physiologic counterregulation: the Cryer hierarchy and its ceiling

Normal defences against falling glucose include suppression of endogenous insulin secretion, glucagon and sympathoadrenal responses, with slower contributions from cortisol and growth hormone. Exogenous insulin exposure cannot be switched off by β-cell feedback, and glucose thresholds and response amplitudes vary with population and antecedent hypoglycaemia ([§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)).

Counterregulatory glucose production has finite capacity and depends on insulin exposure, hormone responses, hepatic glycogen and gluconeogenic substrate supply. At a reference basal glucose turnover of 2 mg/kg/min, a 70-kg adult has a turnover of 140 mg/min = 0.14 g/min = 8.4 g/h. A three- to fivefold multiple of that reference would arithmetically be 420–700 mg/min, not 20–40 mg/min. This calculation is a dimensional check, not a prediction of maximal counterregulatory output during overdose. High insulin exposure can oppose hepatic glucose production while stimulating peripheral disposal; a single fixed concentration threshold cannot establish complete suppression of gluconeogenesis.

### Hepatic glycogen and glucose production

Hepatic glycogen availability can limit the contribution of glycogenolysis, particularly after reduced intake or prolonged metabolic stress. Total glucose production is nevertheless not equivalent to net hepatic glycogen loss: gluconeogenesis, renal production and changing substrate fluxes also contribute. A fixed depletion time cannot be calculated by dividing an assumed liver glycogen store by total glucose turnover.

Skeletal-muscle glycogen does not directly replenish circulating glucose through release of free glucose, although products of muscle metabolism can participate in interorgan substrate cycling. These distinctions prevent a simple claim that hepatic rescue capacity must be exhausted at a predetermined hour. Glucagon responsiveness and the need for exogenous glucose must be assessed in the actual clinical context.

### HAAF in the overdose context

Hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF) can lower the glucose threshold for sympathoadrenal and symptom responses and reduce their amplitude. These are separate properties, not a fixed sequence leading to symptoms below 2 mmol/L or to simultaneous glycogen exhaustion. Impaired awareness may delay recognition while reduced counterregulation impairs recovery. Antecedent hypoglycaemia and awareness status therefore affect vulnerability independently of the reported insulin dose. Some functional impairments improve with avoidance of hypoglycaemia, but neither a universal recovery schedule nor restoration of every glucagon response follows from that observation ([§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)).

### Case series and recovery dynamics

Mégarbane et al. (2007) prospectively studied 25 ICU patients with intentional insulin overdose, of whom 13 had diabetes. Four had an unfavourable outcome, including two deaths. Thus 16% had an unfavourable outcome and 8% died; 16% is not the mortality rate. The heterogeneous cohort was not a T1D-only experiment.

Glucose infusion requirements varied substantially. The study's clinical and kinetic observations show why reported dose, plasma insulin concentration and required glucose delivery should not be treated as interchangeable endpoints. Timing of treatment and the duration of hypoglycaemia are relevant to outcome, but individual reports do not establish a generally safe insulin concentration, a deterministic recovery time or a universal dose–mortality relationship. Findings from overdose treatment cannot determine ordinary insulin dosing relationships.

### Treatment principles

Insulin overdose requires urgent clinical assessment and monitored correction of hypoglycaemia. Glucose delivery is adjusted to repeated measurements and the persistence of insulin action, with attention to electrolytes and neurological status. A transient correction does not demonstrate that an absorbing depot has ceased to act.

The Mégarbane cohort does not establish an infusion threshold for surgical depot excision or a general indication for adjunctive drugs. Such interventions must not be converted from isolated case reports into a treatment recipe. Likewise, a theoretical glycogen estimate cannot determine when glucagon will cease to help. The physiological conclusion is that endogenous counterregulation may be insufficient during continued exogenous insulin action; the clinical response requires ongoing assessment rather than an inferred fixed dose or duration.

<a name="nonlinear-insulin"></a>
## 25. Non-linearity in insulin action — threshold effects and dose-response saturation

Insulin action is concentration- and endpoint-dependent. Suppression of endogenous glucose production, suppression of lipolysis and stimulation of glucose disposal need not share the same concentration–response relationship. Stepped-clamp experiments can distinguish these responses under controlled conditions, but a systemic insulin concentration is not equivalent to an injected subcutaneous dose. Insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios and correction factors used clinically integrate absorption, glucose targets, intake and several tissue responses; they cannot be derived directly from a healthy-volunteer clamp curve.

### Insulin receptor pharmacology: molecular basis for sigmoidal dose-response

Insulin binds the insulin receptor (IR), a heterotetrameric receptor tyrosine kinase (alpha-2/beta-2), at two distinct binding surfaces on the alpha subunits that together confer negative cooperativity: occupation of the first binding site reduces affinity at the second (De Meyts 2004). Receptor binding cooperativity and whole-body dose-response steepness are different quantities. A Hill coefficient fitted to a whole-body endpoint cannot be assigned to receptor binding without an independently identified mechanistic model.

Receptor binding triggers autophosphorylation of multiple tyrosine residues on the intracellular beta subunits, creating docking sites for IRS-1 (insulin receptor substrate 1, primary docking platform downstream of insulin receptor). IRS-1 recruits phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K), generating phosphatidylinositol 3,4,5-trisphosphate (PIP3), which in turn activates 3-phosphoinositide-dependent kinase-1 (PDK1) and Akt/PKB. Akt phosphorylation then bifurcates into metabolic branches: (i) GLUT4 vesicle translocation to the plasma membrane (glucose uptake in muscle and adipose), (ii) phosphorylation and nuclear exclusion of FOXO1 (suppressing gluconeogenic gene transcription), and (iii) activation of glycogen synthase kinase-3 (GSK3) inhibition (glycogen synthesis) (Saltiel & Kahn 2001; Manning & Cantley 2007). Critically, these downstream branches have different amplification factors and activation thresholds within the signaling cascade, so identical receptor occupancy does not yield proportionally identical biological outputs across pathways.

At the whole-organ level, an additional layer of non-linearity arises from plasma-to-interstitial insulin transport. Insulin must traverse the capillary endothelium to reach receptor-bearing cells; this transport step is itself concentration-dependent and saturable, adding a further delay and non-linearity to the tissue dose-response relationship (Sjöstrand et al. 2002). Computational analyses by Bradley et al. (1993) suggest that hepatic and peripheral insulin effects, despite markedly different EC50 values at the level of glucose fluxes, may share a common rate-limiting transcapillary transport step — though the kinetic parameters remain model-dependent and subject to ongoing debate.

### Hill kinetics framework for insulin dose-response

The sigmoidal insulin dose-response relationship is canonically represented by the Hill equation (Hill kinetics — sigmoidal dose-response curve parameterized by EC50 and Hill coefficient n):

```
E(I) = E_max × I^n / (EC50^n + I^n)
```

where E is the fractional effect (0–1 relative to maximal), I is the plasma insulin concentration (typically µU/mL or pmol/L), EC50 (insulin concentration producing half-maximal effect) is the concentration at half-maximum effect, n is the Hill coefficient governing curve steepness (dimensionless), and E_max is the tissue-specific maximal biological effect. For n = 1 the equation reduces to a Michaelis-Menten hyperbola; values of n > 1 produce progressively steeper sigmoidal transitions. For whole-body insulin action, the Hill coefficient must be estimated for a specified endpoint and protocol. No empirical range for this coefficient is adopted from the abstract-only Rizza record.

The Hill framework captures three pharmacologically distinct dose regimes that are physiologically important:

- **Sub-threshold zone (I << EC50):** Effect is approximately proportional to I^n, not generally linear; tissue-specific responses have not yet reached their steeply rising region.
- **Transition zone (I ≈ EC50):** Half-maximal effect occurs at EC50. The location of maximum slope on a linear concentration axis depends on n; this is not a validated operating range for a personal correction bolus.
- **Saturation zone (I >> EC50):** Effect plateaus near E_max; additional insulin provides diminishing marginal benefit. Supraphysiological doses during insulin overdose or aggressive correction dwell in this region.

Because EC50 differs across tissues, each organ enters and exits these regimes at different absolute insulin concentrations, creating the well-documented sequential hierarchy of insulin effects.

### Endpoint-specific half-maximal insulin concentrations

In 15 healthy participants, Rizza, Mandarino and Gerich compared insulin effects on glucose production and utilization using sequential two-hour insulin infusions and a glucose tracer. The inspected abstract reports half-maximal concentrations of **29±2 μU/mL for suppression of glucose production** and **55±7 μU/mL for stimulation of utilization** ([Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7018254/)). The ratio of these reported means is approximately **1.90**, not threefold. The uncertainty notation is retained as reported; its definition was not independently checked in full text.

These are whole-body experimental endpoints in healthy volunteers, not direct measurements of an anatomical liver or muscle receptor EC50 in T1D. The original has not been fully appraised here. The inherited table's lipolysis values, receptor occupancies and graph-derived Hill coefficients are therefore not reproduced as established Rizza findings. A mechanistic model may use distinct action channels, but its Hill coefficient and tissue attribution require their own evidence or an explicit calibration assumption.

Endogenous insulin enters the portal circulation before peripheral circulation, whereas subcutaneous insulin enters peripheral circulation first. Hepatic extraction and the resulting portal–peripheral gradient vary with physiological conditions; no fixed ratio is adopted here ([Najjar and Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/)).

### HGP suppression versus Rd activation as separable dose-response curves

EGP suppression and Rd stimulation are not merely quantitatively different — they represent qualitatively distinct physiological responses mediated by partially distinct intracellular pathways, and their dose-response relationships are therefore separable and independently modulatable.

**Hepatic EGP suppression** is mediated principally through (i) suppression of glycogenolysis (rapid, via phosphorylase kinase inhibition) and (ii) suppression of gluconeogenesis (slower, via FOXO1 nuclear exclusion and reduced expression of PEPCK and glucose-6-phosphatase). At basal insulin concentrations (~50–100 pmol/L systemic, ~150–300 pmol/L portal), glycogenolysis is already substantially suppressed; gluconeogenesis continues and accounts for ~50% of basal EGP. Near-complete EGP suppression (~90%) requires systemic insulin of ~360 pmol/L (~60 µU/mL) (Rizza et al. 1981). Because hepatic insulin action operates near the lower end of the sigmoidal curve under physiological conditions, small corrections in portal insulin exert large proportional effects on EGP.

**Peripheral Rd stimulation** requires insulin concentrations approximately 2–10 times higher than for EGP suppression to reach equivalent fractional effects. Rd is dominated (~80%) by skeletal muscle, which expresses GLUT4 as its primary glucose transporter; GLUT4 translocation is the key rate-limiting step for insulin-stimulated muscle glucose uptake (Zierath et al. 2000). Because GLUT4 translocation requires high Akt activity and is subject to post-receptor signal attenuation, skeletal muscle operates on the steep portion of its dose-response curve at insulin concentrations corresponding to the late post-meal (or high-dose correction) range. This explains why Thiebaud et al. (1982), using graded insulin infusions in a separate stepped-clamp design, found that whole-body glucose oxidation and glucose storage increased progressively only above ~200 pmol/L (~33 µU/mL) and were near-maximal only above ~700 pmol/L (~117 µU/mL).

Adipose tissue represents a third, more sensitive effector: Stumvoll et al. (2000) demonstrated that suppression of systemic, intramuscular, and subcutaneous lipolysis reaches 80–90% at insulin concentrations of ~100–200 pmol/L, well below concentrations required for substantial Rd stimulation. This creates the physiologically logical hierarchy: at low insulin levels, energy substrate mobilization is arrested first (protecting against ketogenesis), EGP is suppressed next (limiting hepatic glucose output), and peripheral disposal is recruited last (the most energetically demanding step).

### T1D-specific modelling hypotheses: dose dependence and post-meal dynamics

Differences between tissue concentration–response curves provide testable hypotheses about the relative hepatic and peripheral effects of changing insulin exposure. They do not establish which part of a curve is reached by a 1-unit or 4-unit subcutaneous bolus in a person with T1D. This would require the absorption profile, baseline insulin, distribution, clearance and contemporaneous glucose fluxes to be measured or independently identified.

Similarly, an apparent change in a correction factor after a larger dose does not uniquely demonstrate nonlinear recruitment of muscle uptake. Continuing absorption, endogenous glucose production, glucose concentration itself, recent food and activity are alternative or interacting explanations. A supposed "small-dose dead zone" is therefore not established by the healthy clamp hierarchy.

After meals, changing glucose appearance and insulin exposure can alter both endogenous production and disposal. Their relative contribution cannot be inferred from the visible glucose curve alone. Direct T1D studies with measured exposure and tracer-derived fluxes are needed to evaluate these hypotheses; no bolus-concentration mapping or personal dosing rule is assigned here.

### Modulators of EC50: exercise, FFA, and glucotoxicity

Insulin sensitivity is operationally defined by the measurement protocol. Glucose disposal at one insulin concentration, half-maximal concentration, maximal response and total daily insulin requirement are not interchangeable. An observed change in one does not uniquely identify a shift in another.

**Exercise.** Contraction can increase glucose uptake through pathways that do not require the same insulin signal as resting uptake. Adding insulin-independent disposal does not, by itself, demonstrate a leftward shift in an insulin concentration–response curve. Post-exercise changes in insulin action must be distinguished from uptake during contraction and from altered glycogen availability ([§8](#aerobic-exercise)).

**Fatty-acid exposure and hyperglycaemia.** Both can modify insulin action, but a reduction in disposal at one clamp insulin concentration may reflect altered maximal response, apparent sensitivity or other fluxes. Tissue-specific shifts cannot be recovered from a single whole-body percentage change. Molecular mechanisms support hypotheses about such changes; they do not uniquely determine a numerical EC50.

**Clinical dose.** Usual insulin requirements additionally reflect glucose targets, intake, delivery, absorption and endogenous glucose production. Neither an experimentally observed change in disposal nor an assumed EC50 shift supplies a universal percentage change in daily dose.

### Controversies and limitations

Several important caveats surround the quantitative use of published EC50 estimates:

**Population versus individual variability.** The Rizza estimates describe 15 healthy participants. Their abstract does not establish the inherited 30–40% coefficient of variation or a twofold individual range; neither is retained as a verified result.

**Healthy-to-T1D generalizability.** The canonical Rizza dataset was obtained in metabolically healthy subjects under euglycemic clamp conditions. Generalization to T1D introduces two complications: (1) absent portal-to-peripheral gradient alters effective hepatic exposure; (2) variable insulin resistance from disease duration, exercise habits, and glycemic control shifts EC50 in unpredictable directions. Basu et al. (2004) showed that the hepatic dose-response curve is similarly shaped in T2D versus controls but shifted rightward, particularly at low insulin concentrations. Equivalent T1D-specific stepped-clamp data with sufficient steady-state plateaus are sparse.

**Interstitial vs. plasma kinetics.** Whole-body dose-response curves are typically plotted against plasma insulin concentration, but biological effects depend on interstitial (tissue) concentrations, which lag and attenuate plasma concentrations at a rate that is itself insulin-dependent. Sjöstrand et al. (2002) estimated interstitial-to-plasma ratios in forearm muscle of ~0.35–0.55 at steady state, meaning tissue EC50 values in absolute concentration terms differ from plasma-referenced values reported in most clamp studies. Models that apply plasma-referenced EC50 values directly to effect compartments introduce a systematic error.

**Hill coefficient uncertainty.** Steeper and shallower fitted curves produce different local sensitivities even at the same EC50. Identification requires concentration–response observations across an adequate range; choosing n for simulation is a modelling assumption unless a suitable experimental fit is documented.

### Modeling considerations

**Modelling inference.** A saturating response may be appropriate when extrapolation beyond an empirically calibrated operating range is necessary. Its input must be the insulin concentration corresponding to the fitted experiment, or a correctly scaled insulin-effect state. In the Hovorka formulation, Q2 is a glucose pool, not an insulin concentration; substituting Q2 into an insulin Hill equation is dimensionally incorrect. Basal equilibrium, endpoint scaling, maximal effect and dynamic delays must be re-identified and validated together rather than changed by inserting a published EC50 into an unrelated state equation ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15382830/)).

<a name="glucotoxicity"></a>
## 26. Glucotoxicity — hyperglycemia-induced insulin resistance and tissue damage

**Active in simulator**

Sustained hyperglycemia in type 1 diabetes does not merely reflect inadequate insulinization — it actively worsens the situation by inducing insulin resistance in its own right. This phenomenon, termed glucotoxicity, creates a positive-feedback loop: elevated blood glucose impairs intracellular insulin signaling, raising insulin requirements above the dose the patient expects, which in turn permits further hyperglycemia, which deepens the resistance further. The clinical consequence is that a period of poor glycemic control — caused by illness, pump failure, omitted basal, or psychological stress — can require substantially more insulin to resolve than the event that initiated it. In T1D, where no endogenous insulin secretion exists to compensate, this loop is unconstrained; restoration of euglycemia partially reverses the acute component within hours to days, but the chronic component — captured by HbA1c — carries its own complication burden that does not fully reverse even with subsequent normalization, a phenomenon termed metabolic memory. Understanding the biochemical pathways linking hyperglycemia to cellular dysfunction is therefore not merely academic: it provides the mechanistic rationale for why tight early glycemic control in T1D confers a disproportionate long-term benefit on microvascular complication risk (DCCT/EDIC Research Group, 1993; 2000; 2016).

### The Brownlee unifying mechanism: mitochondrial superoxide as the master switch

The dominant current framework for glucotoxic injury, synthesized across a series of landmark papers by Brownlee (2001; 2005), proposes that all four major hyperglycemia-activated damage pathways share a single upstream trigger: **overproduction of superoxide (O₂⁻) by the mitochondrial electron transport chain**. When intracellular glucose concentration is chronically elevated, glycolytic flux overwhelms the capacity of the citric acid cycle and electron transport chain, causing electrons to accumulate on carrier molecules (ubiquinol, NADH) and spill onto molecular oxygen rather than being handed to complex IV in an orderly fashion. The result is a burst of mitochondrial reactive oxygen species (ROS) that does not merely cause oxidative damage to proteins and lipids but activates a specific signaling cascade: superoxide inhibits glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH), the glycolytic enzyme that normally consumes upstream intermediates. GAPDH inhibition causes these intermediates — including glucose-6-phosphate, fructose-6-phosphate, and diacylglycerol (DAG) precursors — to accumulate and overflow into the four damage pathways simultaneously. This is not the whole story (see Open questions below), but it provides a mechanistically coherent explanation for why hyperglycemia produces seemingly disparate complications in vessels, nerves, and kidney via structurally unrelated biochemical routes.

### Pathway 1: Polyol pathway flux

The polyol pathway (aldose reductase converts excess glucose to sorbitol, depleting NADPH) becomes significant only when intracellular glucose is elevated, because aldose reductase has a high Km for glucose (~70 mmol/L) and low affinity at euglycemia. During hyperglycemia, the enzyme becomes quantitatively important: sorbitol accumulates within the cell (it crosses membranes poorly), osmotically stressing the cytoplasm. More importantly for insulin resistance, the NADPH consumed in the reduction of glucose to sorbitol is the same NADPH pool used by glutathione reductase to regenerate reduced glutathione (GSH), the primary intracellular antioxidant. Depletion of NADPH therefore reduces the cell's ability to buffer the superoxide burst, amplifying oxidative damage in a positive feedback loop. In addition, aldose reductase-derived sorbitol is subsequently oxidized to fructose by sorbitol dehydrogenase, consuming NAD+ and generating NADH; excess NADH shifts the cytoplasmic redox state, which among other effects increases DAG synthesis (via glycerol-3-phosphate accumulation) and thereby amplifies PKC activation (see Pathway 3 below). Animal models of diabetic neuropathy — where polyol-pathway activity in Schwann cells and dorsal root ganglia is highest relative to pathway capacity — show partial protection with aldose reductase inhibitors (Yorek, 2003), though clinical trials in humans have been disappointing, possibly because pathway flux in human nerve is lower than in rodent models at the same glycemic exposure, or because inhibition was initiated too late.

### Pathway 2: Advanced glycation end-products (AGEs)

AGEs (advanced glycation end-products: irreversible protein crosslinks formed by non-enzymatic glucose-protein binding) accumulate intracellularly and extracellularly during sustained hyperglycemia via the Maillard reaction. The initial, reversible product (a Schiff base) rearranges over days to weeks into Amadori products (of which HbA1c is the most familiar clinical marker) and then undergoes further irreversible reactions over months to years to yield a heterogeneous class of AGEs including carboxymethyl-lysine, pentosidine, and methylglyoxal-derived hydroimidazolone (MG-H1). Intracellular AGE formation begins rapidly: the reactive intermediate methylglyoxal, generated as a byproduct of glycolysis (from triosephosphate oxidation), can modify proteins within minutes of a glucose surge. Once formed, AGEs on long-lived extracellular matrix proteins (collagen IV in basement membranes, laminin) crosslink adjacent protein chains, altering mechanical stiffness and reducing turnover by impairing protease access. In the vasculature this produces characteristic basement membrane thickening in retinal capillaries (the basis of early diabetic retinopathy) and glomerular mesangium (nephropathy). Critically, circulating and tissue-bound AGEs signal through RAGE (receptor for advanced glycation end-products), a pattern-recognition receptor on endothelial cells, macrophages, and vascular smooth muscle whose activation triggers NF-κB and JNK/p38 MAP kinase pathways, generating a sustained pro-inflammatory state that is self-perpetuating: RAGE itself is upregulated by NF-κB, creating a positive-feedback loop that continues even if glycemia is subsequently normalized (Goldin et al., 2006). In insulin-target tissues, AGE-RAGE signaling reduces GLUT4 protein content by approximately 30% in animal models (Brownlee, 1992) and contributes to the serine phosphorylation of IRS-1 that blocks insulin signal transduction. The HbA1c-complications dose-response relationship from DCCT is steeper than a simple linear model predicts above ~9% (approximately 75 mmol/mol), consistent with the accelerating kinetics of AGE formation at higher glucose concentrations.

### Pathway 3: DAG-PKC activation

Excess glycolytic intermediate dihydroxyacetone phosphate is reduced to glycerol-3-phosphate, which is esterified to DAG (diacylglycerol, lipid second messenger activating PKC isoforms) — the endogenous lipid activator of protein kinase C. Hyperglycemia therefore mimics, in a sustained way, the transient DAG increase that normally occurs during receptor-mediated phospholipase C activation. PKC (protein kinase C, family of serine/threonine kinases) encompasses at least 10 isoforms; in diabetic vascular disease and insulin resistance, PKCβ₁ and PKCβ₂ are the most studied (Geraldes and King, 2010). PKCβ activation in endothelial cells increases NADPH oxidase assembly, generating further superoxide (another positive-feedback loop onto the Brownlee master switch), increases ET-1 (endothelin-1) secretion, reduces eNOS expression, and activates NF-κB, collectively producing vascular dysfunction, impaired vasodilation, and inflammation. In insulin-target tissues (skeletal muscle, adipose, liver), PKCθ and PKCε are the dominant isoforms: they phosphorylate IRS-1 on Ser307 (inhibitory phosphorylation site on insulin receptor substrate-1), which sterically blocks IRS-1 from docking with the activated insulin receptor's Tyr residues. The net effect is that the insulin signal cannot propagate downstream to PI3K → Akt → AS160 → GLUT4 translocation, producing insulin resistance at a post-receptor level. This is the same signaling node targeted by FFA-induced insulin resistance via PKCθ (lipotoxicity), which explains why the two phenomena potentiate each other in glucolipotoxicity (Monaco et al., 2024). Intervention evidence targeting individual PKC isoforms requires trial-specific appraisal; a molecular association alone does not establish a clinical treatment effect or identify the dominant pathway.

### Pathway 4: Hexosamine pathway flux

Under normoglycemia, approximately 2–5% of intracellular glucose-6-phosphate is diverted from glycolysis into the hexosamine biosynthesis pathway (HBP), converging on the synthesis of UDP-N-acetylglucosamine (UDP-GlcNAc). During hyperglycemia, flux through this pathway increases substantially. UDP-GlcNAc is the substrate for O-GlcNAc transferase (OGT), which attaches GlcNAc (O-GlcNAc modification) to serine and threonine residues of hundreds of nuclear and cytoplasmic proteins — including transcription factors (Sp1, p65, FoxO1) and insulin signaling proteins (IRS-1, Akt) (Marshall, 2004). O-GlcNAc modification of IRS-1 on certain Ser residues directly competes with activating Tyr phosphorylation; O-GlcNAcylation of Akt reduces its kinase activity. The result is blunting of the entire insulin signaling cascade without any receptor-level change. In cell studies, 8 hours of 25 mM glucose reduced maximal insulin-stimulated glucose transport by 40–50% via this mechanism (Marshall et al., 1991); O-GlcNAcylation of Sp1 drives TGF-β₁ and PAI-1 transcription, contributing to glomerular fibrosis in nephropathy. The hexosamine pathway is uniquely positioned as an intracellular "glucose sensor" whose output is both rapid (OGT acts within minutes) and capable of persisting through transcriptional reprogramming (Buse, 2006). Glutamine:fructose-6-phosphate amidotransferase (GFAT) is the rate-limiting enzyme; GFAT overexpression in mice recapitulates key features of diabetic insulin resistance in the absence of frank hyperglycemia, confirming that pathway flux — not hyperglycemia per se — is the proximate cause.

### Hyperglycemia → insulin resistance: the downstream convergence on GLUT4 and IRS-1

Hyperglycaemia-associated changes in insulin signalling and glucose disposal must be distinguished from the pathways contributing to chronic tissue injury. The T1D experiment by Vuorinen-Markkola et al. (1992) addresses hyperglycaemia and insulin action directly, but its inherited numerical estimates require full-text protocol appraisal before being used as quantitative targets. A percentage change in clamp disposal does not translate directly into the same change in usual insulin dose.

Rossetti's phlorizin experiment concerned streptozotocin-diabetic rats, not people with T1D. It supports an experimental role for hyperglycaemia in altered insulin action in that animal model; its recovery timing is not a human clinical constant.

Garvey et al. (1985) studied 14 participants with type 2 diabetes before and after three weeks of continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion. Their findings concern this population and include changes in endogenous insulin secretion as well as insulin action. They cannot quantify reversible glucotoxicity in T1D or establish a universal daily insulin-dose multiplier. This population and protocol identification is supported by the indexed abstract; the original full text was not obtained.

### Quantitative magnitude: oxidative stress markers and endothelial dysfunction kinetics

Several quantitative benchmarks allow the glucotoxic burden to be contextualized clinically:

**Oxidative stress markers.** Plasma 8-isoprostane (a non-enzymatic arachidonic acid peroxidation product and validated marker of systemic oxidative stress) is approximately 2–3-fold elevated in poorly controlled T1D (HbA1c >9%) compared to age-matched non-diabetic controls. Plasma thiobarbituric acid reactive substances (TBARS) and urinary 8-OHdG (oxidized guanosine, a marker of mitochondrial DNA oxidative damage) track similarly. These markers fall measurably within 2–4 weeks of glycemic improvement and reach near-normal levels after 3–6 months of sustained HbA1c <7%, consistent with the clearance of the acute PKC and hexosamine components but not the long-lived AGE crosslinks.

**Endothelial dysfunction kinetics.** Flow-mediated dilation (FMD) of the brachial artery — a validated, non-invasive measure of endothelium-dependent vasodilation reflecting eNOS activity — is reduced by approximately 30–50% in poorly controlled T1D versus healthy controls, and is partially restored within ~2 weeks of euglycemia in acute hyperglycemia studies (Ceriello et al., 2002). The mechanism is rapid because it involves PKCβ-driven eNOS uncoupling and superoxide quenching of NO — both of which are partially reversible on the timescale of PKC dephosphorylation (hours to days). Structural changes (basement membrane thickening, reduced capillary density) require months to years to reverse and do so only partially.

**Insulin dose requirement.** Total daily dose is a clinical aggregate, not a direct assay of tissue insulin sensitivity. Glucose targets, intake, delivery, absorption and endogenous glucose production all contribute. Neither the T2D Garvey experiment nor a change in disposal at a single clamp concentration establishes that poorly controlled T1D requires 30–50% more insulin independently of these factors. No universal recovery multiplier or day-by-day dose trajectory is assigned.

### Reversibility: time course of resolution

Reversibility must be evaluated for a defined endpoint and population. Changes in cellular signalling, clamp-measured glucose disposal, usual insulin dose, vascular function and established tissue injury occur on different time scales. Cell or animal pathway experiments cannot assign a human recovery constant to each of these processes.

The three-week intervention in Garvey et al. (1985) involved T2D, including endogenous insulin secretion, and cannot be used as a T1D GLUT4-recovery schedule. Similarly, experimental hyperglycaemia in T1D and longitudinal intensification of clinical treatment are different exposures. A general claim that most excess daily insulin requirement resolves within 1–2 weeks is not established by the evidence appraised here.

Persistence of complication risk after glycaemic improvement is also distinct from persistence of acute insulin resistance. Longitudinal clinical outcomes and mechanistic studies must be evaluated separately before assigning the relative contributions of structural damage and molecular memory.

### HbA1c and the dose-response relationship with complications

The DCCT (1993) established that the relationship between HbA1c and the risk of microvascular complications is continuous, without a clear threshold below which risk is zero. The risk of retinopathy progression is approximately linear with HbA1c across the range 6.5–9% (48–75 mmol/mol) and steepens above ~9%, consistent with accelerating AGE formation and PKC activation at higher glycemic exposures. Each 1% (11 mmol/mol) reduction in HbA1c reduces retinopathy risk by ~35%, nephropathy risk by ~25%, and neuropathy risk by ~25% across the range studied in DCCT (N=1441 patients, mean follow-up 6.5 years). These relationships are dominated by the chronic pathway components (AGEs, structural basement membrane changes) rather than the rapidly reversible hexosamine and PKC components; therefore, they represent the irreducible cost of sustained hyperglycemia even after insulin resistance per se is corrected. The EDIC follow-up (DCCT/EDIC Research Group, 2000; 2016) showed that the intensive treatment group retained a ~57% reduction in severe retinopathy and a >50% reduction in nephropathy risk relative to the conventional group for at least 17 years after the trial ended and glycemic separation disappeared — the duration of follow-up, not just the HbA1c at any point, was the dominant predictor of risk.

### Tissue-specific complications: brief mechanistic summary

Each of the four pathways contributes disproportionately to different complication phenotypes, though the contributions overlap:

- **Retinopathy:** PKCβ-driven increase in VEGF and reduction of eNOS → pericyte loss and capillary non-perfusion → microaneurysms, cotton-wool spots, neovascularization. AGE crosslinking of type IV collagen in basement membranes → thickening and reduced flexibility → capillary occlusion.
- **Nephropathy:** HBP-driven TGF-β₁ overexpression (O-GlcNAc modification of Sp1) → glomerular mesangial expansion and fibrosis → reduced GFR. AGE accumulation in the glomerular basement membrane → proteinuria. PKCβ activation → increased glomerular filtration pressure.
- **Neuropathy:** Polyol-pathway-driven NADPH depletion in Schwann cells and dorsal root ganglia → impaired myelin maintenance; AGE accumulation on axonal proteins; PKC-mediated endoneural ischemia via reduced prostacyclin and increased ET-1. Clinically, slowed nerve conduction velocity is detectable in DCCT patients at HbA1c levels of ~9% with a mean disease duration of ~5 years.

### Open questions and controversies

Several aspects of the glucotoxicity framework remain unresolved:

1. **Which pathway dominates?** The Brownlee unifying mechanism is conceptually elegant, but experimental evidence that GAPDH inhibition by superoxide accounts for the majority of cellular damage in human tissues — as opposed to in aortic endothelial cell cultures — is limited. Inhibiting GAPDH with iodoacetate recapitulates the four-pathway activation (Du et al., 2000), but tissue-specific studies suggest that the hexosamine and PKC pathways dominate in muscle (insulin resistance) while AGEs dominate in long-lived structural proteins. The relative contribution of each pathway likely varies by tissue type, glycemic duration, and individual genetic susceptibility.

2. **Metabolic memory: epigenetic or structural?** The EDIC persistence of complication risk is most commonly attributed to epigenetic modification — Brasacchio et al. (2009, *Diabetes*) showed that transient hyperglycemia induces persistent H3K4me1 methylation of the NF-κB p65 promoter, maintaining elevated p65 expression for ≥6 days after glucose normalization. However, AGE crosslinks on long-lived structural proteins (collagen half-life in glomerular basement membrane ~years) provide a purely mechanical explanation for complication persistence without epigenetic mediation. Both mechanisms are likely operative.

3. **Individual susceptibility:** For a given HbA1c, complication rates vary approximately 10-fold between individuals (DCCT data). Candidate modifiers include genetic polymorphisms in aldose reductase (AKR1B1 promoter), RAGE ligand variants, antioxidant enzyme polymorphisms (MnSOD, CAT), and differences in endogenous methylglyoxal metabolism (glyoxalase I activity). This heterogeneity explains why some patients with decades of poor control develop minimal complications while others develop severe retinopathy and nephropathy at much lower HbA1c — and why population-level dose-response curves cannot reliably predict individual outcomes.

4. **Beta-cell glucotoxicity in T1D context:** In T2D and late-stage T1D (residual C-peptide), glucotoxicity directly impairs beta-cell function and survival via ROS-induced mitochondrial DNA damage and endoplasmic reticulum stress (Andrali et al., 2008). In established T1D with no residual beta-cell mass, this component is irrelevant, but in newly diagnosed patients during the honeymoon period, reducing glucotoxic load may contribute to prolonging C-peptide secretion — a clinically meaningful effect given the association between residual insulin secretion and reduced hypoglycemia risk.

# Part 8: Technology and Research Models

<a name="cgm-technology"></a>
## 27. CGM technology — continuous glucose monitoring sensors and algorithms

Continuous glucose monitors estimate glucose from a local interstitial signal. Physiological transport, sensor response, calibration and digital processing contribute separately to differences between displayed values and blood glucose. Their combined effect depends on the device, glucose trajectory and experimental method. Measurement validity, clinical outcomes and the performance of automated insulin delivery are therefore related but distinct questions.

### Sensor electrochemistry

Sensor chemistry determines how local glucose is converted into a measurable signal. Enzyme-based electrochemical methods and optical sensing are different approaches; it is incorrect to describe every commercial CGM as an electrochemical device. Calibration links the measured signal to reported glucose, while interference depends on the chemistry and device implementation.

Specific claims about electrode potential, electron mediators, drug interference, calibration schedules and sensor lifetime require device-specific technical documentation and validation studies. They cannot be generalized from one sensor generation to another. These characteristics are not assigned a universal value or compared in a nominally current product table here.

### Interstitial versus blood glucose: physiology of the compartment gap

Interstitial glucose and plasma glucose are related through transport and local tissue use, but a displayed CGM value is also affected by sensor and processing behaviour. A first-order compartment approximation may describe particular measurements; its time constant is not automatically the total delay of a commercial device.

Basu et al. (2013) studied glucose tracer transport in healthy participants, not a T1D "clamp-and-decay" experiment. The healthy-human experiment and the subsequent T1D study must remain separate ([§1](#glucose-distribution)).

Basu et al. (2015) used intravenous glucose tracers and abdominal subcutaneous microdialysis in six overnight-fasted adults with T1D maintained on intravenous insulin. The indexed abstract reports a median tracer appearance time of 6.8 minutes, range 4.8–9.8. Time to tracer detection is not a sensor time constant, a dynamic cross-correlation lag or a validated delay during exercise or meals. Detailed protocol appraisal awaits the original full text.

During changing glucose concentrations, interstitial and displayed values may diverge from contemporaneous blood glucose. Rate-of-change units must remain explicit: 2 mg/dL/min is approximately 0.11 mmol/L/min, and 3 mg/dL/min approximately 0.17 mmol/L/min. These conversions do not establish a universal physiological rate limit or a fixed discrepancy during rapid excursions.

### Lag-time kinetics: quantitative summary

| Quantity | What it describes | Interpretation boundary |
|---|---|---|
| Tracer appearance time | Detection of labelled glucose in sampled interstitial fluid | Basu 2015: median 6.8 minutes, range 4.8–9.8, in six fasting adults with T1D; indexed abstract |
| Physiological compartment time constant | Parameter fitted to a specified transport model and dataset | Not interchangeable with tracer detection time |
| Sensor response and processing delay | Conversion and filtering of a device's local signal | Requires device-specific measurement |
| Apparent blood–CGM displacement | Difference observed during a particular glucose trajectory | Can combine physiological, sensor and algorithmic contributions |

A fixed table assigning 5–8 minutes at rest and 15–25 minutes during rapid change is not justified by the tracer experiment. Compression-related artifacts are also not a physiological lag category with a universal duration. Direction, magnitude and duration of discrepancies require validation under the relevant conditions.

### Accuracy metrics and comparison limits

Mean absolute relative difference (MARD) summarizes paired sensor–reference discrepancies:

> MARD = (1/N) × Σ |CGM_i − REF_i| / REF_i × 100%

Here REF_i is the reference glucose paired with the sensor observation. The metric depends on the reference method, temporal pairing and the glucose distribution sampled. A given absolute error becomes a larger relative error at a lower reference concentration, so MARD alone does not describe hypoglycaemia detection performance.

Absolute-error summaries, percentages within predefined error limits and clinical error grids provide complementary information. Their definitions and denominators must be stated explicitly. Comparisons between different pivotal studies may also reflect differences in population, glucose challenges, wear days and reference sampling rather than device performance alone. No common paediatric penalty, hypoglycaemia-range MARD or current ranking is inferred from unmatched studies.

### Signal processing: smoothing, calibration, and trend algorithms

A glucose-estimation system must relate the measured signal to glucose, handle noise and drift, and determine how much temporal smoothing is acceptable. Smoothing may reduce noise while delaying detection of change. These functions do not imply that all devices use Kalman filtering, a particular physiological predictor, the same drift curve or a fixed 2–5-minute delay.

Trend arrows summarize estimated change over a device-defined window. The numerical boundaries, display categories and alert prediction horizons must be read from the relevant documentation. An arrow is neither an independent glucose measurement nor a universal treatment instruction. Calibration and artifact handling likewise require validation for the specific system and conditions of use.

### Known limitations and artefacts

Local pressure, tissue perfusion, insertion-site response and chemical interference can affect the relationship between a sensor signal and circulating glucose. A pressure-associated low signal should not be assigned a universal duration, and dehydration should not be presumed to produce a predictable positive bias through simple concentration of interstitial glucose. Drug effects and warm-up behaviour depend on device chemistry and design.

ISPAD's paediatric sick-day guidance notes that rapid glucose changes, poor perfusion and interfering substances can complicate interpretation. When sensor readings do not correspond to the clinical situation, an independent glucose measurement may be required according to the clinical and device guidance. This is distinct from applying a universal correction factor to the CGM value ([Phelan et al., 2022](https://www.ispad.org/static/6551d05a-156c-4772-a32a6d663fcfc2d4/2022CGCh13PediatricDiabetes-2.pdf)).

### TIR as a clinical outcome metric and its relationship to HbA1c

Time in range (TIR) describes the percentage of monitored time within a specified glucose interval. The 2019 international consensus uses 3.9–10.0 mmol/L for the usual adult T1D/T2D reporting range and recommends, for most adults in these groups, TIR >70%, time below 3.9 mmol/L <4%, time below 3.0 mmol/L <1%, and time above 10.0 mmol/L <25%. Targets differ for pregnancy and for older or high-risk individuals; these population recommendations are not universal personal thresholds ([Battelino et al., 2019](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6973648/fullTextXML)).

TIR and HbA1c are related but noninterchangeable outcomes. Battelino et al., Table 5, distinguish a T1D dataset of 545 adults from a mixed-diabetes analysis: their tabulated cross-sectional slopes are approximately 0.5 and 0.8 HbA1c percentage points per 10 percentage points of TIR, respectively. The original Beck et al. (2019) abstract reports an average longitudinal HbA1c decrease of 0.6 percentage points with a 10-percentage-point TIR increase, with wide individual variation. These different populations and contrasts should not be merged into a universal conversion table.

A given TIR does not uniquely predict an individual's HbA1c or describe the severity of excursions outside the range. Time below and above range, their distribution, data completeness and the monitoring interval remain necessary for interpretation. A short CGM sampling interval and the longer glycaemic exposure represented by HbA1c also answer different temporal questions.

### Clinical impact in T1D

Measurement accuracy and clinical effectiveness require different evidence. An accuracy experiment compares sensor readings with a reference; an outcome trial examines the consequences of using the system in a defined population and treatment setting. A lower MARD does not by itself establish an HbA1c benefit or a reduction in severe hypoglycaemia.

When a sensor contributes feedback to automated insulin delivery, the consequences of noise, missed readings and delay depend on the full system. No particular clinical effect size or requirement for one predictive algorithm follows from the physiological tracer studies or the TIR–HbA1c associations summarized here.

> **Sources:**

> - Basu A, Dube S, Slama M, et al. (2013). "Time lag of glucose from intravascular to interstitial compartment in humans." *Diabetes*, 62(12):4083–4087. PMID: 24009261. Healthy-human tracer study; see §1.
> - Basu A, Dube S, Veettil S, et al. (2015). "Time lag of glucose from intravascular to interstitial compartment in type 1 diabetes." *Journal of Diabetes Science and Technology*, 9(1):63–68. DOI: 10.1177/1932296814554797. PMID: 25305282. Indexed abstract appraised; original full text not obtained.
> - Battelino T, Danne T, Bergenstal RM, et al. (2019). "Clinical Targets for Continuous Glucose Monitoring Data Interpretation: Recommendations From the International Consensus on Time in Range." *Diabetes Care*, 42(8):1593–1603. [Full text](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6973648/fullTextXML).
> - Beck RW, Bergenstal RM, Cheng P, et al. (2019). "The Relationships Between Time in Range, Hyperglycemia Metrics, and HbA1c." *Journal of Diabetes Science and Technology*, 13(4):614–626. DOI: 10.1177/1932296818822496. PMID: 30636519. PMCID: PMC6610606. Indexed abstract appraised; original full-text retrieval unresolved.
> - Phelan H, Hanas R, Hofer SE, et al. (2022). "Sick day management in children and adolescents with diabetes." *Pediatric Diabetes*, 23:912–925. [Full text](https://www.ispad.org/static/6551d05a-156c-4772-a32a6d663fcfc2d4/2022CGCh13PediatricDiabetes-2.pdf).

> 🔧 **Implementation:** See [CGM Sensor Modeling](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#cgm) in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION for how the simulator's CGM is modeled (lag, noise, calibration drift).

---

<a name="mathematical-models"></a>
## 28. Mathematical models of glucose-insulin dynamics in research

Glucose concentration is an observable consequence of several simultaneous fluxes: intestinal appearance, endogenous production, tissue uptake and renal loss. A concentration trace alone does not identify their individual contributions. Models make those contributions explicit, but their physiological interpretation depends on the measurements used to constrain them. This distinction motivated the use of tracer-derived fluxes, rather than glucose and insulin concentrations alone, in meal-model development ([Dalla Man et al., 2007](https://isbgroup.eu/files/Dalla%20Man%202007.pdf)).

For ==type 1 diabetes (T1D)==, modelling must also distinguish endogenous secretion into the portal circulation from insulin delivered into subcutaneous tissue. An experimental identification model, a controller's short-horizon predictor and a virtual subject used to test that controller serve different purposes. This section examines those purposes and their evidential requirements; [§29](#model-comparison) contains the model profiles and comparison table. The synthesis is a targeted appraisal of selected model publications, not an exhaustive inventory of every contemporary platform.

### 28.1 From physiological mechanism to mathematical representation

A compartment is a mathematically defined pool within which a substance is treated as well mixed. Its state may be an amount, a concentration or an effective action. For a constant distribution volume, the mass-balance relation is:

<pre>V · dC/dt = inflow − outflow + production − removal</pre>

Here V is volume, C is concentration and each right-hand term has units of amount per time. Consequently, a plasma concentration cannot be substituted directly for an amount without its distribution volume. Organ-resolved models specify blood-flow connections and local metabolic rates; lumped models combine tissues whose separate behaviour cannot be estimated or is unnecessary for the intended question. Both approaches appear in the reconstructed model comparison of [Pompa et al. (2021)](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0257789).

An insulin-action state is not necessarily an anatomical insulin pool. In the Hovorka formulation, three delayed signals modulate glucose transport, disposal and endogenous production. They represent effective actions inferred from system dynamics, not direct measurements of three tissue insulin concentrations. Likewise, a mathematical glucose compartment need not coincide with the interstitial fluid sampled by a glucose sensor. The observation model must specify how the simulated states generate the measured variable ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://doi.org/10.1088/0967-3334/25/4/010); [Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

Mechanistic structure does not eliminate empirical assumptions. Blood-flow balances can coexist with fitted saturation functions, fixed distribution volumes and phenomenological delays. The informative question is therefore not whether a model is wholly “mechanistic”, but which relationships follow from conservation, which were estimated experimentally and which were selected for tractability. A named organ or hormone establishes representational scope, not accuracy.

### 28.2 Identifiability and what can be learned from data

**Structural identifiability** asks whether ideal, noise-free observations could uniquely determine the unknown parameters for a specified model, set of inputs, measured outputs and initial-condition assumptions. Global identifiability permits one parameter solution; local identifiability permits a finite set. If only a parameter combination is identifiable, additional sampling of the same outputs cannot necessarily separate its components. The property does not depend on equations alone: changing the observed variables or known inputs can change the answer ([Wanika et al., 2024](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13036-024-00410-x)).

**Practical identifiability** concerns estimation from the actual finite, noisy dataset. A structurally identifiable parameter may remain poorly constrained because the sampling interval, observation period or perturbation provides little information about it. Parameter count alone proves neither form of identifiability. A detailed model with many fixed constants may estimate only a small subset; a compact model may still contain inseparable parameter combinations. Profile likelihood assesses practical uncertainty by varying one parameter while reoptimising the others. Wide or unbounded intervals expose uncertainty that a single best-fit value conceals ([Wanika et al., 2024](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13036-024-00410-x)).

This matters directly for glucose modelling. A postprandial rise can reflect faster intestinal appearance, weaker suppression of ==endogenous glucose production (EGP)== or reduced tissue uptake. Compensating errors can reproduce plasma glucose while assigning the wrong contributions to those processes. The forcing-function strategy used by Dalla Man and colleagues constrains a subsystem with measured or experimentally reconstructed inputs while estimating its remaining relationships. It reduces ambiguity compared with fitting the complete feedback system only to concentration data, although uncertainty in the reconstructed fluxes remains relevant ([Dalla Man et al., 2007](https://isbgroup.eu/files/Dalla%20Man%202007.pdf)).

Informative priors provide another constraint. Hovorka and colleagues repeatedly estimated six parameters using prior distributions and recent glucose measurements, while fixing other quantities. This is a defensible adaptation strategy, but it does not demonstrate that every physiological parameter is independently recoverable from routine glucose data. Prior constraints and data-derived information should remain distinguishable when interpreting a fitted “individual” ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://doi.org/10.1088/0967-3334/25/4/010)).

### 28.3 Experimental protocols constrain different mechanisms

An ==intravenous glucose tolerance test (IVGTT)== perturbs circulating glucose without intestinal absorption. Measured insulin can be supplied as an input to estimate delayed insulin action and glucose effectiveness. The latter describes glucose's contribution to its own disappearance, including effects on net production, at basal insulin. Such a test does not by itself establish the kinetics of an oral meal or a subcutaneous insulin depot. The original minimal-model study compared seven candidate descriptions using glucose injections in conscious dogs; it was not a study of autonomous daily-life prediction in people with T1D ([Bergman et al., 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/443421/), abstract; [Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

A hyperinsulinaemic euglycaemic clamp maintains glucose near a target while insulin is raised experimentally. At steady state, glucose infusion balances the difference between disposal and endogenous production; infusion rate alone is not a direct measurement of disposal if endogenous production remains appreciable. Tracer measurements can separate these quantities. Hypoglycaemic clamps answer a different question: the response to falling glucose under imposed insulin exposure. Results must be interpreted with the imposed glucose trajectory and the available hormone or tracer measurements, rather than treated as validation of unrestricted daily-life dynamics ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

Mixed-meal tracer protocols provide information about intestinal appearance, endogenous production and disposal during oral intake. In the 2007 meal-model study, a labelled meal and two intravenously infused tracers supported reconstruction of glucose fluxes in participants without diabetes and with ==type 2 diabetes (T2D)==. The model was fitted to population-average concentration and flux profiles. The source sample therefore must not be counted as independently identified T1D virtual subjects or an external validation cohort; its composition is detailed in §29 ([Dalla Man et al., 2007](https://isbgroup.eu/files/Dalla%20Man%202007.pdf)).

==Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM)== adds dense observations in daily life but measures an interstitial signal with sensor-specific delay and error. It does not directly observe hepatic production, intestinal appearance or insulin action. A daily-life comparison must therefore distinguish physiological prediction error from input-recording and measurement error. A sensor model can be necessary for evaluating a closed-loop system even when it adds no new physiological mechanism ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

### 28.4 Fit, prediction and validation are different results

A fitted curve tests whether the selected structure and parameters can describe the observations used for estimation. Prediction tests performance on data not used for that estimate. Independence has several levels: later time points in the same experiment, another occasion in the same person, different people, or a different protocol. An independent dataset need not have been collected prospectively, and withholding later samples from a fitted experiment does not establish transportability to another population.

Hovorka's 2004 study illustrates a bounded prediction claim. The authors evaluated predictions after four hours following a meal, allowing time for parameter learning and reducing the influence of absorption. Short-horizon agreement under these conditions supports the adaptive predictor's fasting application; it does not establish accurate prediction of the early meal excursion, exercise or prolonged insulin deficiency. Its quantitative results are reported in §29 rather than repeated here ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://doi.org/10.1088/0967-3334/25/4/010)).

Aggregate clinical endpoints provide a complementary test. A simulator may reproduce a population's overnight mean glucose without reproducing individual trajectories. Conversely, a small average pointwise error can conceal failures during hypoglycaemia. A useful appraisal therefore relates each metric to the claim: trajectory error, timing of peaks or nadirs, flux accuracy, time within a specified glucose range, or robustness of a controller. Nonsignificant differences do not establish equivalence without a prespecified acceptable margin and adequate precision. These distinctions are particularly relevant to the population comparison in [Wilinska et al. (2010)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2825634/).

### 28.5 Virtual cohorts and sources of uncertainty

A virtual subject is a model plus a parameter set and initial conditions. A virtual cohort is a collection of such subjects, not necessarily a set of one-to-one replicas of observed patients. Cohort construction can combine estimated distributions, fixed literature values, clinical constraints and stochastic sampling. Reporting only the number of virtual subjects conceals how much of their variability was measured and how much was assumed ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

Three sources of variation require separate interpretation. Between-person variation represents persistent differences among parameter sets. Within-person variation changes a given subject's physiology or effective parameters over time. Input and device uncertainty concern meals, insulin delivery and measurements ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)). Consequently, randomising a meal schedule does not add evidence about between-person physiology, and adding sensor noise does not create biological heterogeneity. Repeated simulations quantify behaviour under these selected distributions; they do not independently confirm that the distributions represent patients.

As a methodological inference, cohort evaluation should examine joint parameter combinations and output distributions, not just plausible marginal ranges. Preserving correlation can matter when insulin sensitivity, basal requirements and meal responses constrain one another. Sampling many more subjects reduces Monte Carlo variability for the assumed model; it cannot remove structural bias. A thousand runs of one hypothetical patient remain a thousand runs of that patient, rather than a clinical sample of a thousand people.

### 28.6 Controller performance is not model accuracy

In ==model predictive control (MPC)==, a prediction model estimates future glucose under candidate insulin deliveries; an optimiser selects a constrained action and repeats the calculation after new observations. The virtual subject used to test this controller is a separate object. Their structures may be related, but testing only against the same equations and parameters risks an undemanding evaluation. Assessment should disclose the differences between the controller's internal assumptions and the simulated subject ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://doi.org/10.1088/0967-3334/25/4/010); [Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

A feedback controller can compensate for prediction error through repeated measurements, parameter adaptation and safety constraints. Successful control therefore supports the performance of the complete system in the tested setting, not the correctness of every physiological equation. Nor are all automated insulin-delivery algorithms MPC systems: proportional–integral–derivative and other control architectures should not be described as though they necessarily run a physiological forward model ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

### 28.7 Interpretation for physiological research

The strongest comparison identifies an intended population, intervention, time horizon and observable endpoint before comparing models. Mechanistic breadth, parameter recoverability and predictive accuracy then become distinct criteria rather than synonyms. Model-to-model agreement establishes consistency between representations; patient observations are needed to decide whether either representation is accurate. The comparison by [Pompa et al. (2021)](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0257789) demonstrates why plausible simulation curves alone cannot resolve that choice.

Prediction outside the development protocol requires additional evidence, whether the model is mechanistic, statistical or hybrid. A fitted parameter described as “insulin sensitivity” may absorb errors in meals or other unobserved processes rather than isolate that physiological property. Conversely, a well-defined parameter may be identifiable without the model adequately reproducing all observed outputs. Identifiability is therefore necessary for interpreting individual parameter estimates, but insufficient for establishing physiological validity ([Wanika et al., 2024](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13036-024-00410-x)).
