### Abbreviation glossary

**CGM** — continuous glucose monitoring, repeated estimation of glucose from an interstitial sensor. **EGP** — endogenous glucose production, glucose entering the circulation from endogenous sources. **IVGTT** — intravenous glucose tolerance test, an intravenous perturbation used to study glucose disappearance. **MPC** — model predictive control, repeated optimisation based on predicted future outputs. **T1D/T2D** — type 1/type 2 diabetes.

---

<a name="model-comparison"></a>
## 29. Comparison of major T1D glucose-insulin research models

The models below differ in the questions they were designed to answer, not simply in complexity. Some estimate a physiological quantity from an experiment; others reproduce meal responses or evaluate insulin-delivery algorithms. The Al Ahdab model is included as a ==type 2 diabetes (T2D)== comparator, because it combines organ-level physiology with medication, activity and stress. It should not be classified as a validated ==type 1 diabetes (T1D)== model. The ==University of Virginia/University of Padova (UVA/Padova)== platform is identified by its publication versions.

The comparison distinguishes publication versions, measured participants and simulated subjects. It does not rank products, equate parameter counts with accuracy or audit the current commercial availability of every implementation. [§28](#mathematical-models) explains identifiability and validation; the profiles here examine the actual mechanisms and evidence.

### 29.1 Comparative overview

| Model and publication | Intended task | Distinctive representation | Evidence basis examined | Principal boundary |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bergman et al., 1979 | Estimate insulin sensitivity from intravenous testing | Measured insulin drives delayed action on glucose disappearance | Original abstract: experimental comparison in conscious dogs; later formulation reviewed by Cobelli et al. | Not an autonomous meal-and-insulin simulator |
| Sorensen, 1985; reconstruction by Pompa et al., 2021 | Represent organ-level metabolism and investigate insulin therapy | Blood-flow-linked glucose, insulin and glucagon subsystems | Literature-derived structure; later reconstruction and comparative simulations | Physiological detail is not independent validation |
| Hovorka et al., 2004 | Adaptive prediction for fasting T1D control | Two glucose pools, subcutaneous insulin and three insulin-action signals | 15 clinical experiments in 10 people with T1D | Prediction assessment excluded early postprandial data |
| Dalla Man et al., 2007 | Reproduce postprandial concentration and flux responses | Gastric processing, intestinal appearance, production, uptake and secretion | Average data from 204 people without diabetes and 14 with T2D | Original paper is not a T1D virtual-cohort validation |
| UVA/Padova, S2008/S2013 | Preclinical testing of T1D control systems | Population simulation; later hypoglycaemia and glucagon extensions | Version-specific component data and virtual-cohort checks | Virtual samples and regulatory use have defined limits |
| Wilinska et al., 2010 | Reproduce population-level overnight control outcomes | Cambridge physiology plus temporal variation and device models | 18 virtual subjects compared with 12 young people with T1D | Aggregate overnight agreement, not individual equivalence |
| Al Ahdab et al., 2021 | Explore combined T2D treatment and lifestyle effects | Organ compartments, endogenous secretion, drugs, activity and stress | Integration of published components; numerical scenarios | Combined model still required confirmation with patient data |

Sources and quantitative qualifications are given in the corresponding profiles below. State counts are deliberately not used as a ranking: explicit depot states, analytic input functions and optional sensor modules make unqualified counts misleading.

### 29.2 Bergman minimal model: an identification model

**Purpose and structure.** The glucose minimal model estimates insulin sensitivity from an ==intravenous glucose tolerance test (IVGTT)==. Measured plasma insulin is an input, rather than an output that must be predicted by a pancreatic secretion subsystem. A common formulation has two dynamic states:

<pre>dG/dt = −(S_G + X)G + S_G G_b
dX/dt = −p_2 X + p_3(I − I_b)
S_I = p_3 / p_2</pre>

G and I denote glucose and insulin concentrations; the subscript b denotes basal values. X is delayed insulin action, S_G is glucose effectiveness and S_I is insulin sensitivity. S_I is a derived ratio, not an additional independent parameter alongside both p_2 and p_3. An estimated initial glucose value can contribute to the number of fitted quantities without adding a dynamic state ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

**Evidence and interpretation.** The original study compared seven models after intravenous glucose injections of 100, 200 or 300 mg/kg in conscious dogs. The accessible abstract reports reproducible insulin-sensitivity estimation in the 300 mg/kg experiments, not clinical validation of meal prediction in T1D ([Bergman et al., 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/443421/), abstract only). The model's subsequent importance should not erase that original population or transform identification evidence into autonomous prediction evidence.

**Scope and access.** The equations and their physiological interpretation are documented in the later open review. Oral absorption and subcutaneous insulin kinetics require additional inputs or submodels. The estimated net insulin effect does not separately identify hepatic suppression and peripheral disposal from unlabelled glucose alone. This makes the model useful for a defined physiological measurement, but insufficient by itself for investigating the distinct responses to food composition, insulin formulation or exercise ([Cobelli et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2951686/)).

### 29.3 Sorensen: organ resolution and its cost

**Purpose and structure.** Sorensen's thesis represents glucose metabolism through organ-level glucose, insulin and glucagon subsystems, with blood-flow connections among the brain, heart/lungs, gut, liver, kidney and peripheral tissues. Unlike a single effective insulin-action signal, this structure permits local concentrations and metabolic source/sink functions to differ among organs. The thesis explicitly concerns the design and assessment of insulin therapy; describing it as a model never intended for control research would contradict its stated purpose ([Sorensen, 1985](https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~dmilam/files/sorensen_thesis.pdf); [Pompa et al., 2021](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0257789)).

**Evidence and interpretation.** The detailed formulation reviewed here is the later reconstruction by Pompa and colleagues, not a fresh revalidation of every equation in the thesis. Their comparison uses a corrected, extended Sorensen formulation, including gastrointestinal and subcutaneous insulin components. Removing pancreatic insulin secretion changes the input dependence, but does not establish that all remaining physiological parameters are appropriate for T1D. They also adjusted metabolic and distribution parameters to obtain responses comparable with the other models.

**Scope and access.** The thesis and reconstructed equations are available for inspection. Their value lies in explicit organ-level hypotheses and inspectable assumptions, not status as physiological “ground truth”. A large fixed literature-derived parameter set can permit simulation without making all parameters individually estimable. Conversely, its size alone does not prove structural non-identifiability. For reuse, the relevant object is the specified version, parameter provenance and initialization procedure; “Sorensen model” without those details can refer to materially different systems ([Pompa et al., 2021](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0257789)).

### 29.4 Hovorka: compact dynamics with bounded clinical prediction evidence

**Purpose and structure.** Hovorka and colleagues developed an adaptive ==model predictive control (MPC)== system for fasting glucose regulation. Its physiological model separates accessible glucose Q_1 from a second pool Q_2; two subcutaneous insulin depots feed plasma insulin. Three action states modulate transport/distribution, disposal and ==endogenous glucose production (EGP)==. The structure is compact without making the complete glucose–insulin system linear: insulin-action states multiply glucose amounts in the flux equations ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://doi.org/10.1088/0967-3334/25/4/010)).

The distinction between glucose exchange and meal absorption is explicit:

<pre>dQ_2/dt = x_1 Q_1 − (k_12 + x_2)Q_2
U_G(t) = D_G A_G t exp(−t/t_max,G) / t_max,G²</pre>

In the original paper, k_12 = 0.066 min⁻¹ transfers glucose from Q_2 to Q_1; it is **not** a gastrointestinal absorption constant. The meal expression uses ingested carbohydrate expressed as glucose amount D_G, bioavailability A_G = 0.8 and t_max,G = 40 min. It is equivalent to two serial first-order transit stages with rate 1/t_max,G. These are model specifications, not universal meal constants.

The action equations have the form dx_i/dt = −k_ai x_i + k_bi I. Published deactivation rates are 0.006, 0.06 and 0.03 min⁻¹. Their isolated first-order time constants, **calculated as 1/k_ai**, are approximately 167, 16.7 and 33.3 min. They are not whole-body insulin-action peak times: depot absorption, plasma clearance, interactions with glucose pools and the initial state also determine the observed response. Tables 1–2 distinguish fixed constants from six parameters used in Bayesian adaptation, rather than presenting approximately 30 freely estimable individual parameters.

**Evidence and interpretation.** The clinical evaluation comprised 15 fasting experiments lasting 8–10 h in 10 people with T1D; five participated twice. Glucose sampling and insulin adjustment occurred every 15 min. Prediction assessment began 240 min after food intake. Across 1,674 measured–predicted pairs at 15–60 min horizons, 95% fell in Clarke error-grid zone A and 5% in zone B. These are correlated predictions from a small participant sample, not 1,674 independent clinical observations. The paper's results section reports ==root mean square error (RMSE)== increasing by approximately 0.2 mmol/L per additional 15 min of prediction horizon.

**Scope and access.** Published equations and parameter information permit inspection and reimplementation. The reported performance concerns an adaptive model with informative priors under a fasting-oriented protocol; it is not an external validation of all fixed-parameter daily-life trajectories. Explicit glucagon dynamics, exercise mechanisms and meal-composition pathways are absent from this formulation. Its action states are linear in insulin, but that does not make the complete coupled system linear or establish a particular error magnitude at high doses.

### 29.5 Dalla Man: separating postprandial fluxes

**Purpose and structure.** The 2007 meal model uses two glucose compartments, hepatic and plasma insulin kinetics, pancreatic secretion and a gastrointestinal model distinguishing solid gastric content, liquid gastric content and intestinal content. Gastric emptying depends on stomach content. This permits structured meal-appearance dynamics, but does not amount to separate digestion and metabolism of carbohydrate, protein and fat ([Dalla Man et al., 2007](https://isbgroup.eu/files/Dalla%20Man%202007.pdf)).

Its insulin-dependent tissue utilization is represented by a Michaelis–Menten relation. In the form subsequently retained in S2008:

<pre>U_id(t) = [V_m0 + V_mx X(t)] G_t(t) / [K_m0 + G_t(t)]</pre>

G_t is glucose amount in the tissue compartment, not plasma glucose concentration. K_m0 has the corresponding amount units. The fraction saturates with respect to **tissue glucose**; the displayed numerator remains linear in the insulin-action signal X. Calling this equation an insulin-dose saturation mechanism therefore misidentifies the nonlinearity. The 2007 paper considered a more general insulin-dependent affinity term, which collapsed to an insulin-independent affinity in its fitted dataset.

**Evidence and interpretation.** Component parameters were fitted using average concentration and tracer-derived flux profiles from 204 participants without diabetes; the same approach was applied to 14 participants with T2D. Measured or reconstructed inputs constrained each subsystem before the coupled system was simulated. This provides a stronger basis for attributing production and uptake than concentration-only fitting, but the displayed agreement with those same average profiles remains a development-data result, not an independent T1D validation.

**Scope and access.** The paper supplies equations and parameter tables for the represented populations. Its whole-day examples impose changes in sensitivity and secretion parameters rather than independently establishing every daily pattern. The authors identify missing counterregulatory hormones and other substrates as limitations. This publication is an important ancestor of T1D simulation platforms, but should not be conflated with later T1D-specific populations or regulatory submissions.

### 29.6 UVA/Padova: version-specific population simulation

**Purpose and structure.** The UVA/Padova platform extends meal modelling to virtual T1D populations. S2008 already included 300 virtual subjects: 100 adults, 100 adolescents and 100 children. Their parameter vectors were sampled from joint distributions, not individually fitted to 300 observed patients. The original distributions used nondiabetic adult data with specified changes for T1D and age groups ([Dalla Man et al., 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4454102/)).

**Evidence and interpretation.** The 2014 publication describes **S2013**, adding hypoglycaemia-related utilization, glucagon secretion/action and subcutaneous glucagon kinetics. Its component evidence includes clamp data from 32 adults with T1D and glucagon-kinetic data from 11 adults, contributing 13 datasets. The clamp protocol lacked tracers, limiting separation of production from disposal. These are component-level evidence sets, not a single independent validation of the complete platform. The update did not introduce the claimed glucagon-like peptide-1/glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide incretin pair.

**Scope and access.** The reported 2008 acceptance by the ==United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA)== concerned specified preclinical testing, not replacement of human efficacy studies or blanket approval of derivative software. Access to a paper, its equations and a licensed distribution are separate questions; present licensing was not audited. Pompa's reconstruction required additional parameter choices and fixed some time-varying relationships, illustrating why a reimplementation should not automatically be treated as the original platform ([Pompa et al., 2021](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0257789)).

### 29.7 Cambridge extension: variability and device behaviour

**Purpose and structure.** Wilinska and colleagues combined Cambridge physiology with ==continuous glucose monitoring (CGM)== and pump-error models. Selected parameters oscillated with **5% amplitude and a 3 h period**, with random phases. This is not the previously described 20% circadian sensitivity oscillation. Eighteen virtual parameter sets combined estimated quantities with informed sampling ([Wilinska et al., 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2825634/)).

**Evidence and interpretation.** Population outputs were compared with overnight control in **12 children and adolescents**, aged 13.1 ± 4.2 years. Mean overnight glucose was 7.6 ± 1.2 mmol/L in simulation versus 7.8 ± 1.4 mmol/L clinically. Median time at 3.9–8.0 mmol/L was 69% (interquartile range 62–78%) versus 63% (49–78%). Reported nonsignificant differences support compatibility under that protocol; they do not establish equivalence or accurate individual predictions.

**Scope and access.** The article supplies model equations and parameter information, but this does not establish a currently maintained public software release. The evaluation concerns a defined overnight experiment and device assumptions. Oscillatory parameters can test sensitivity to variation without demonstrating a physiological mechanism for that variation.

### 29.8 Al Ahdab: combined treatment and lifestyle modelling in T2D

**Purpose and structure.** [Al Ahdab et al. (2021)](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/glucose-insulin-mathematical-model-for-the-combined-effect-of-med/) integrate repeated meals, insulin injections, metformin, vildagliptin, physical activity and stress into an organ-resolved T2D model. The formulation builds on the Sorensen-derived lineage through subsequent T2D extensions. Glucose and insulin compartments follow the circulation through brain, heart/lungs, gut, liver, kidney and peripheral tissues; glucagon and incretin subsystems modify metabolic and secretory responses. Endogenous pancreatic insulin secretion remains part of the model.

Meals are explicitly simplified to **glucose ingestion**. Three gastrointestinal states and a meal-size-dependent gastric-emptying relation describe its transit; additional bookkeeping permits repeated meals. The ability to enter different meal sizes does not establish representation of protein-driven glucose production, fat-dependent delayed responses or fibre effects. Those mechanisms would require additional structure and evidence.

Fast- and long-acting injected insulin are represented with formulation-related absorption compartments. Metformin and vildagliptin have separate pharmacological components. Physical activity modifies glucose uptake and insulin-related metabolic effects through heart-rate-driven states, while a dimensionless stress input modifies production and hormonal responses. These are specified model mechanisms assembled from earlier literature, not independently measured effects of every combination in this paper.

**Evidence and interpretation.** The authors report more than 120 parameters and discuss the additional measurements needed for identification. Much of the base physiology originates in a healthy 70 kg male parameterisation; added subsystems use their respective literature sources. The combined model's examples are numerical demonstrations, not a newly enrolled T2D cohort. Figures 7–8 use 1,000 simulation runs with varying inputs, while Figures 9–10 compare three hypothetical subjects. Selected sensitivity parameters were perturbed by ±90%; the authors explicitly describe that choice as arbitrary and the physiologically admissible perturbation range as uncertain.

The paper therefore establishes a transparent combined formulation and demonstrates its numerical behaviour. It does not establish patient-level predictive accuracy, effectiveness of a treatment strategy or validity in T1D. Its conclusion explicitly states that confirmation with real patient data remains necessary. That limitation applies to the integrated model even when individual component mechanisms have supporting studies.

**Scope and access.** The final article is openly licensed, and an [AAU T2D Simulator code repository](https://gitlab.com/aau-adapt-t2d/aau-t2d-simulator) was reachable on 22 September 2026; execution was not tested. The publication is the 2021 *Biochemical Engineering Journal* article, not the differently titled later arXiv version. For T1D research, its principal relevance is as an inspectable example of multi-intervention model composition. Transferring it would require explicit reconsideration of secretion, hormone regulation, medication relevance and parameter evidence, rather than merely relabelling the virtual subject.

### 29.9 What head-to-head comparisons establish

[Pompa et al. (2021)](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0257789) compared reconstructed Sorensen, Hovorka and UVA/Padova formulations through intravenous and oral perturbations and fitting to an ==oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT)== from one person without diabetes. They adjusted model parameters and inferred external insulin inputs to reproduce that observed glucose trace. This is a test of representational flexibility under the selected assumptions, not a clinical head-to-head prediction study in T1D.

The inferred insulin boluses were approximately 2.3, 8.5 and 8 units for Hovorka, UVA/Padova and Sorensen, respectively. These are **model-fitting outputs**, not observed treatment requirements or recommended doses. Their divergence illustrates that similar glucose fits can arise from different inferred mechanisms and inputs. It does not identify which dose would have been appropriate for a patient.

The study also modified the Sorensen formulation and used selected or calibrated values for incompletely specified UVA/Padova parameters. Consequently, its results concern those reconstructions. They should not be generalized unqualifiedly to every release of the named platforms. The authors themselves conclude that in-silico comparison alone cannot determine physiological superiority.

### 29.10 Synthesis and remaining evidence gaps

Across the examined publications, increasing representational breadth does not produce a corresponding hierarchy of validation strength. A component can have experimental support while its interaction with other components remains untested; population-level agreement can coexist with uncertain individual predictions. These are distinct gaps requiring different experiments, not interchangeable reasons to call an entire model either validated or invalid.

For model selection, the following are **appraisal recommendations**, not results of a trial comparing selection strategies. Specify the phenomenon and population first; then examine input definitions, initialization, parameter provenance and the validation endpoint. Require additional evidence when extending from glucose ingestion to mixed-food mechanisms, from fasting prediction to exercise, or from population agreement to individual prediction. State and parameter counts are useful engineering descriptions only after their counting conventions are defined.

The examined literature does not provide a harmonised external benchmark across all these models using identical T1D participants, meals, insulin formulations, activity protocols and endpoints. It therefore cannot support a universal accuracy ranking. Nor does it establish current licensing for all releases, performance of every exercise extension, or the state of every machine-learning and digital-twin approach. Those questions require separate, version-specific searches rather than unsupported conclusions about an entire field.

### Abbreviation glossary

**CGM** — continuous glucose monitoring, repeated estimation of glucose from an interstitial sensor. **EGP** — endogenous glucose production, glucose supplied from endogenous sources. **FDA** — United States Food and Drug Administration. **IVGTT/OGTT** — intravenous/oral glucose tolerance test. **MPC** — model predictive control, repeated optimisation using predicted future outputs. **RMSE** — root mean square error, a trajectory-error summary in the units of the measured variable. **T1D/T2D** — type 1/type 2 diabetes. **UVA/Padova** — University of Virginia/University of Padova simulation platform.
